Actively patient handout library — draft for review

Generated 2026-10-02 from app/src/db/handout-library/ · 116 handouts in 5 categories · 0 approved so far

How to review

Every handout below was drafted by Claude (AI) for your review as the site's board-certified dermatologist. They are live for signed-in clinicians, each marked “Draft”, and every word is editable by the clinician before it reaches a patient. Tick one box per row and per handout, and note edits; send the file back or list row numbers. Approved handouts get reviewed: true and lose the draft marker. Regenerate with npx tsx src/db/handout-library-review.ts in app/.

Drafting rules: plain language (about 6th–8th grade); no prescription doses, strengths or schedules (“as prescribed”); OTC products “as directed on the label”; no brand names; no isotretinoin; pregnancy cautions where standard; timings hedged (“usually”, “unless we told you otherwise”); sources are organization patient pages or guidelines cited by author, journal and year only. The two universal stop rules below are added to every handout.

Universal stop rules (added to every handout):
  1. Hives, swelling of the lips, tongue or face, or trouble breathing: call 911.
  2. A rash that spreads fast, blisters, or comes with fever: get seen the same day.

For the attorney

  1. Patient email stays in the clinic's own email app. “Email” buttons open the clinician's mail client (mailto:) with the link pre-filled; Actively never receives the patient's address. Confirm this keeps Actively outside a business-associate relationship for these features.
  2. MA patch-test reader. Grades, the chart note and the patient's name stay in the browser tab. The only data that leaves is the avoid-list link (allergen code + reading date, no identifiers), and only when printed, shown as a QR or emailed from the clinic's app. Confirm no HIPAA obligations attach; consider whether the reader page should carry any terms-of-use line for clinics.
  3. Avoid list saved to accounts. A signed-in person's avoid list (their allergies) is now stored with their email, like their shelf and plans. This is consumer health data (WA My Health My Data, CT, NV). The privacy policy needs a line for it; deletion is covered by account deletion. Confirm consent wording on the sign-up prompt is sufficient.
  4. Public draft previews. /clinic-tools/handouts/[id] shows each draft handout's full text to anyone (noindex). Keep public, or require a clinician sign-in until reviewed?
  5. Clinician responsibility. Library text is a starting point; the clinician edits and issues it under their name. Is the builder's draft marker plus the terms enough, or do you want an explicit attestation at first use?

Skin conditions (46)

Start here: what the drafter was least sure of
  1. Melanoma: follow-up 'often every 3 to 12 months for the first few years, then at least once a year'; genetic counseling if 2+ relatives had melanoma or relatives had pancreatic cancer; stop rule for unexplained symptoms over 2 weeks (weight loss, cough, headaches, bone pain); ask before starting vitamin D.
  2. SCC: more common with chronic leukemia; higher-risk SCC follow-up 'often every few months at first'. Atypical moles: mild atypia 'often no more treatment is needed, even if the edges were not clear' (practice varies).
  3. Seborrheic keratoses: 'many new growths appearing suddenly' stop rule (Leser-Trélat, unnamed). Lipomas: red flag 'larger than about 5 cm'.
  4. Ingrown toenails: cotton or floss under the nail edge (AAD, but debated); '3 to 5 days' of home care before calling is a default.
  5. Molluscum in adults: genital bumps suggest STI testing; many or large facial bumps suggest an immune check (worded gently).
  6. Hair loss: finasteride label warnings (lowers PSA, pregnancy handling, no blood donation, breast-change stop rule); a mood stop rule includes the 988 crisis line (cut if unwanted); spironolactone and minoxidil avoided in pregnancy and breastfeeding; biotin interferes with lab tests.
  7. Melasma (education): tranexamic acid pill users get an emergency stop rule for leg swelling, chest pain or shortness of breath; FDA mercury warning in skin lighteners also in the PIH handout.
  8. Seborrheic keratoses and skin tags: cosmetic-only removal 'often treated as cosmetic by insurance; ask us' (general statement, not clinic policy).
  9. Sources: guidelines cited by first author/journal/year only (Eisen 2021 AK; Kim 2018 BCC; Alam 2018 SCC; Swetter 2019 melanoma). Some AAD titles approximate: 'Nail changes a dermatologist should examine', 'Hyperpigmentation: How to fade dark spots', 'Hair shedding'.
  10. Acne: a stop rule names headache with vision change on an acne pill (tetracycline intracranial hypertension); some pills need a full glass of water, not right before lying down.
  11. Hives: 'sometimes we advise taking more than the label says; only if we told you' (guideline up-dosing); aspirin/NSAIDs listed as triggers.
  12. Lichen planus: skin disease 'often clears within about 1 to 2 years'; may check for hepatitis C; long-lasting oral or genital disease carries a small cancer risk. Lichen sclerosus: fingertip amount of steroid unless told otherwise; checks usually at least yearly; small SCC risk.
  13. Perioral dermatitis: rebound after stopping a steroid called expected; clearing in 2 to 3 months; rinse after steroid inhalers or nasal sprays and don't stop them without asking.
  14. Hand eczema: alcohol sanitizer often gentler than soap when hands aren't visibly dirty. Stasis dermatitis: ask before compression with arterial disease, diabetes or heart failure; leg skin easily becomes allergic to lanolin, fragrance and antibiotic ointments; one-sided swelling (possible clot) seen the same day; swelling with shortness of breath or chest pain, 911.
  15. Shingles: antivirals work best within 3 days; vaccine recommended for most adults 50+ and some younger immunocompromised adults (no dose count). Scabies: itch for 2 to 4 weeks after is normal; cream 'usually from the neck down'.
  16. Onychomycosis: 'waiting is a reasonable choice' if it doesn't bother you; terbinafine label stop rules (liver, taste or smell loss, low mood). Hyperhidrosis: antiperspirant at bedtime, washed off in the morning; 911 for sweating with chest pain. Tinea: 'keep using for the full time on the label' (no weeks given).
  17. Sources (inflammatory): page titles from memory, e.g. AAD 'Stasis dermatitis: Overview', NRS 'Rosacea triggers'. Guidelines by first author/journal/year: Reynolds 2024, Thiboutot 2020, Sidbury 2023, Davis 2024, Thyssen 2022, Clark 2015, Elmets 2021, Menter 2019, Alikhan 2019, Zuberbier 2022, Lewis 2018, Kalra 2014, Lipner and Scher 2019.

Acne: overview and what to expect cond-acne

Patient sees: Understanding your acne treatment · Clinician summary: What causes acne, how long treatment takes, early side effects, daily care and why maintenance matters.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H1
Section		What it is

Acne starts in the pores. Oil and dead skin cells plug a pore, bacteria that normally live on the skin grow inside it, and the skin around it gets inflamed (red and swollen). That makes blackheads, whiteheads, red bumps, pus bumps and sometimes deep, painful lumps.

Hormones, genes, some medicines and heavy skin or hair products can all play a part. Acne is not caused by dirty skin, and scrubbing does not help.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H2
Section		What to expect from treatment

Acne treatment works slowly because it prevents new spots rather than drying up the ones you have.

  • Most people start to see a change at 6 to 8 weeks and real improvement at about 12 weeks.
  • In the first few weeks, skin can be dry, red or flaky, and some people have a short burst of new spots. This usually settles.
  • Use your treatment on the whole area where you break out, not just on single spots.
  • Once your skin is clear, keep going with a maintenance plan. Stopping completely often lets acne come back.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H3
Section		Caring for your skin
  • Wash twice a day and after sweating with a gentle cleanser and your fingertips. Pat dry.
  • Use a moisturizer and sunscreen labeled non-comedogenic (won't clog pores).
  • Don't pick, pop or squeeze. It makes spots last longer and can leave dark marks or scars.
  • Skip scrubs, rough cloths and harsh toners.
  • Benzoyl peroxide can bleach towels, sheets and clothes. Use white linens if you can.
  • Some acne treatments make you sunburn more easily. Use sunscreen every day.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H4
Section		Medicines and pregnancy

Take any acne pill exactly as prescribed. Some antibiotic pills need a full glass of water and should not be taken right before lying down; follow the instructions you were given.

Some acne medicines, including retinoid creams, some antibiotic pills and some hormone-blocking pills, are not safe in pregnancy. Tell us if you are pregnant, trying to get pregnant or breastfeeding.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H5
Section		About dark marks and scars

Flat pink, red or brown marks left after a spot heals are not scars. They usually fade over months, faster with daily sunscreen. Pitted or raised scars are different. If you are getting scars, tell us: controlling acne early is the best way to prevent more.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H6
Stop and call if		Redness, burning or peeling that is severe, or doesn't settle after a few days off the treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H7
Stop and call if		You become pregnant or are planning a pregnancy.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H8
Stop and call if		Deep, painful lumps, or new scars forming.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H9
Stop and call if		No improvement after 12 weeks of using your treatment every day as directed.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H10
Stop and call if		On an acne pill: a bad headache with vision changes, a new rash, or severe stomach upset.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: Reynolds RV et al. Guidelines of care for the management of acne vulgaris. J Am Acad Dermatol 2024 (AAD) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Acne: Tips for managing" (patient education) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Acne: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education)

Rosacea: overview cond-rosacea

Patient sees: Living with rosacea · Clinician summary: Types of rosacea, common triggers, gentle skin care, sun protection, eye symptoms and treatment options.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H11
Section		What it is

Rosacea is a long-lasting skin condition of the face. It often causes flushing (sudden redness), lasting redness, small visible blood vessels, and acne-like bumps. Skin may sting or burn easily. In some people the eyes feel gritty or dry, and over time the nose can thicken.

Rosacea is common, especially after age 30. It is not caused by poor hygiene and is not contagious. There is no cure, but treatment controls it well for most people.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H12
Section		Know your triggers

Many people find certain things bring on flushing or flares. Common triggers include:

  • Sun, heat and hot weather
  • Hot drinks, spicy food and alcohol (especially red wine)
  • Stress and strong emotions
  • Hard exercise
  • Wind and cold
  • Some skin and hair products

Triggers are different for everyone. Keeping a simple diary for a few weeks can show you which ones matter for you.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H13
Section		Caring for your skin
  • Wash with lukewarm water and a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser. Pat dry.
  • Moisturize every day. Let medicine dry first if you use a cream.
  • Use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher daily. Mineral sunscreens (zinc oxide or titanium dioxide) are often less stinging.
  • Avoid scrubs, rough cloths, alcohol-based toners, menthol, camphor and witch hazel.
  • Test a new product on a small patch first.
  • A hat, shade and cooling off quickly help with flushing.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H14
Section		Treatment options

Treatment depends on what bothers you most.

  • Bumps: prescription creams or gels, and sometimes a low-strength antibiotic pill taken for its anti-inflammatory effect.
  • Redness and flushing: some creams calm redness for several hours. Laser or light treatments can reduce visible blood vessels.
  • Eyes: warm compresses, gentle eyelid cleaning and sometimes an eye doctor visit.

Most treatments take 8 to 12 weeks to show their full effect. Many people need ongoing treatment to keep rosacea calm. Some rosacea pills are not used in pregnancy, so tell us if you are pregnant or trying.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H15
Stop and call if		Eye pain, red or gritty eyes that don't settle, sensitivity to light, or any change in vision.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H16
Stop and call if		Burning, stinging or redness that gets worse with treatment instead of better.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H17
Stop and call if		Thickening or swelling of the nose or other skin.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H18
Stop and call if		No improvement after 12 weeks of using your treatment as directed.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: Thiboutot D et al. Standard management options for rosacea: the 2019 update by the National Rosacea Society Expert Committee. J Am Acad Dermatol 2020 · American Academy of Dermatology, "Rosacea: Tips for managing" (patient education) · National Rosacea Society, "Rosacea triggers" (patient education)

Atopic dermatitis (eczema) in adults cond-atopic-dermatitis-adult

Patient sees: Managing eczema as an adult · Clinician summary: Why eczema flares, bathing and moisturizing, using flare creams safely, newer treatments, and infection warning signs.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H19
Section		What it is

Atopic dermatitis is the most common type of eczema. The skin's protective outer layer (the skin barrier) does not hold in water well, so skin gets dry, itchy and irritated easily. Scratching damages the skin more and makes the itch worse.

It often runs in families and is linked to asthma, hay fever and food allergy. It can last from childhood or start in adulthood. It is not contagious. It tends to come and go, with flares and calmer times.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H20
Section		Common triggers
  • Dry air, cold weather, or heat and sweating
  • Hot showers and harsh or scented soaps
  • Fragrance in skin, laundry and cleaning products
  • Wool and rough fabrics
  • Stress and poor sleep
  • Dust mites, pollen or pet dander for some people

If your eczema is in an unusual spot or keeps getting worse despite treatment, an allergy to something touching your skin may be involved. We may suggest patch testing.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H21
Section		Daily skin care
  • Take a short (5 to 10 minute) lukewarm bath or shower.
  • Use a mild, fragrance-free cleanser only where needed.
  • Pat dry and put on moisturizer within 3 minutes, while skin is still damp.
  • Moisturize at least twice a day, all over, even when your skin is clear.
  • Thick creams and ointments work better than lotions.
  • Wear soft, breathable fabrics like cotton.
  • Keep nails short. Try pressing or cooling an itchy spot instead of scratching.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H22
Section		Using your treatment

Flare creams calm red, itchy patches. Use them exactly as prescribed: on active patches only and for the length of time we discussed. Used this way, steroid creams are safe. Overuse on the same spot for a long time can thin the skin.

Non-steroid creams can be used on the face, eyelids and folds, and some can be used to prevent flares. For eczema that stays moderate or severe, there are other options, such as light therapy, injections and pills. Ask us if your eczema is not well controlled.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H23
Step · Morning and night		Moisturizer (every day) (OTC; picker search “fragrance-free moisturizer”)
At least twice a day, all over, and within 3 minutes of bathing. A thick, fragrance-free cream or ointment works best.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H24
Step · Night		Gentle cleanser (OTC; picker search “gentle cleanser”)
A short lukewarm bath or shower. Use a fragrance-free cleanser only where needed, then pat dry.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H25
Stop and call if		Signs of infection: yellow crusts, pus, spreading redness, or fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H26
Stop and call if		Painful clusters of small punched-out sores or blisters on eczema (call the same day).☐ Approve

☐ Edit

H28
Stop and call if		No improvement after 2 weeks of your flare treatment, or flares that come back quickly.☐ Approve

Allergic contact dermatitis and patch-test results cond-allergic-contact-dermatitis

Patient sees: Living with a skin allergy: your patch-test results · Clinician summary: What a contact allergy is, what positive patch tests mean day to day, how to read ingredient labels, and how long healing takes.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H30
Section		What it is

Allergic contact dermatitis is an itchy rash caused by an allergy to something that touches your skin. Common causes include fragrance, preservatives, nickel, hair dye, rubber chemicals, some glues and some ingredients in creams and medicines.

The rash usually appears 1 to 3 days after contact, sometimes later. That delay is why the cause is often hard to spot. Once you are allergic to something, the allergy usually lasts for life. You can be allergic to a product you have used for years without problems.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H31
Section		What your patch-test results mean

Each positive result is a chemical your skin reacted to. Not every positive explains your rash. We will go over which ones are likely relevant to you.

  • You will need to avoid each relevant allergen for good, not just during flares.
  • Very small amounts can be enough to cause a rash.
  • Some allergens have several names, or are related to other chemicals that can also cause a reaction.
  • A negative result means you are unlikely to be allergic to that chemical. It does not rule out allergy to things that were not tested.
  • Keep your results list and bring it to other doctors, dentists and hair stylists.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H32
Section		Reading labels
  • Read the full ingredient list every time, even on products you have bought before. Formulas change.
  • Words like hypoallergenic, natural, organic, dermatologist-tested and sensitive skin do not mean a product is free of your allergen.
  • Unscented products can still contain a masking fragrance. Look for fragrance-free, and still check the list.
  • Plant extracts and essential oils can contain fragrance chemicals.
  • Check everything that touches your skin: soap, shampoo, conditioner, makeup, sunscreen, wipes, creams, laundry products and medicines.
  • If you have a safe-product list from us, start with it.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H33
Section		What to expect

Once you fully avoid your allergen, the rash usually improves over several weeks. It can take 6 weeks or longer to clear completely. A short course of a prescribed cream may help while it heals.

If your rash keeps coming back, look again at all your products, hobbies and workplace exposures. Gloves help with many work exposures, but some allergens pass through certain glove materials. Ask us which gloves suit your allergen.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H34
Stop and call if		No improvement after 6 to 8 weeks of strict avoidance.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H35
Stop and call if		The rash spreads, blisters or affects your eyelids or face badly.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H36
Stop and call if		Signs of infection: yellow crusts, pus, spreading redness or fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H37
Stop and call if		You are unsure whether a product, medicine or work material contains your allergen.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Contact dermatitis: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · American Contact Dermatitis Society, patient resources and Contact Allergen Management Program (safe-product lists) · DermNet, "Allergic contact dermatitis" (patient information)

Irritant contact dermatitis cond-irritant-contact-dermatitis

Patient sees: Irritant skin rash: protecting your skin · Clinician summary: How wet work, soaps and chemicals damage the skin barrier, protecting skin at home and work, and healing time.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H38
Section		What it is

Irritant contact dermatitis is a rash caused by something that damages the skin's outer layer. It is not an allergy. It can happen to anyone with enough exposure.

Common causes are frequent hand washing, water, soaps, detergents, cleaning products, solvents, oils, friction and some foods. Harsh chemicals can cause a rash quickly. Milder irritants cause it slowly, after days or weeks of repeated contact. It is very common in health care, cleaning, food work, hairdressing, construction and parenting young children.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H39
Section		What to expect

Skin may be dry, red, cracked, scaly or sore, and it can burn or sting more than it itches. Once the skin barrier is damaged, it takes time to repair, often weeks to months, even after the cause is removed. It can flare again quickly with new exposure, so protection needs to continue after the skin looks better.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H40
Section		Protecting your skin
  • Wear waterproof gloves for wet work and cleaning. Cotton liners underneath help with sweat.
  • Keep glove use short, and take gloves off if water gets inside.
  • Use lukewarm water and a mild, fragrance-free cleanser. Rinse well and pat dry.
  • Moisturize after every wash and before bed. Thick, fragrance-free creams or ointments work best.
  • Use tools instead of hands for solvents, oils and harsh cleaners.
  • Take rings off for washing and wet work. Soap trapped under rings is a common cause.
  • Barrier creams are not a substitute for gloves.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H41
Section		Helpful tips

If the rash keeps coming back despite good protection, or it spreads to places that did not touch the irritant, you may also have a contact allergy. We may suggest patch testing.

If your rash is related to your job, talk with us about a note for your workplace about gloves or changes to your tasks.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H42
Stop and call if		Deep, painful cracks, bleeding, or skin that weeps.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H43
Stop and call if		Signs of infection: yellow crusts, pus, spreading redness or fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H44
Stop and call if		The rash spreads beyond the area that touched the irritant.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H45
Stop and call if		No improvement after 4 weeks of protecting your skin and using your treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Contact dermatitis: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · DermNet, "Irritant contact dermatitis" (patient information)

Hand eczema cond-hand-eczema

Patient sees: Caring for hand eczema · Clinician summary: Causes of hand eczema, hand washing and glove tips, overnight moisturizing, and using prescribed creams.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H46
Section		What it is

Hand eczema is a red, dry, itchy, cracked or blistered rash on the hands. It is very common. It can be caused by irritation from water and soap, by an allergy to something you touch, by the tendency to eczema, or a mix of these.

Hands are hard to rest, so hand eczema can last a long time. Protecting your skin every day is the most important part of treatment.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H47
Section		Washing your hands
  • Use lukewarm water, not hot.
  • Use a mild, fragrance-free soap or soap substitute. Rinse it off well.
  • Pat dry gently, including between the fingers.
  • Moisturize right after washing, every time.
  • When your hands are not visibly dirty, an alcohol hand sanitizer is often gentler than soap and water. It may sting on cracked skin.
  • Take rings off before washing and wet work.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H48
Section		Protecting your hands
  • Wear waterproof gloves for dishes, cleaning, hair washing, peeling fruit and vegetables, and other wet work.
  • Put thin cotton gloves on underneath, and change them when damp.
  • Wear warm gloves outside in cold weather.
  • Wear work gloves for gardening, building or handling chemicals.
  • At bedtime, put on a thick layer of ointment and cover with cotton gloves overnight.
  • Keep a tube of moisturizer by every sink, in your bag and at work.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H49
Section		Using your treatment

Use any prescribed cream or ointment exactly as directed, usually on the rash only and for a set time. Keep moisturizing even when the eczema clears.

Hand eczema often improves slowly, over weeks. If your rash does not improve, we may suggest patch testing to look for an allergy, or other treatments such as light therapy or pills.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Seborrheic dermatitis: overview cond-seborrheic-dermatitis

Patient sees: Understanding seborrheic dermatitis · Clinician summary: Dandruff and flaky red patches on the scalp, face and chest: causes, medicated shampoos and keeping it under control.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H57
Section		What it is

Seborrheic dermatitis is a common rash that causes flaking and redness in oily areas: the scalp (dandruff), eyebrows, sides of the nose, behind the ears, beard area and center of the chest. It can itch.

It is linked to a yeast that normally lives on everyone's skin, along with the skin's reaction to it. It is not caused by poor hygiene, is not an allergy and is not contagious.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H58
Section		What to expect

Seborrheic dermatitis tends to come and go. It is often worse in winter, in dry air, and with stress or lack of sleep. Treatment controls it well, but it usually comes back if you stop completely. Most people do best with a regular maintenance routine.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H59
Section		Using medicated shampoo

Shampoos with ketoconazole, selenium sulfide, zinc pyrithione, salicylic acid or coal tar can help. Use them as directed on the label or by us.

  • Lather into the scalp, not just the hair.
  • Leave the lather on for about 5 minutes, then rinse.
  • The same lather can be used on the beard, eyebrows, face and chest. Rinse well.
  • Use it more often during a flare. Once it clears, keep using it about once a week.
  • Switching between two types of shampoo can help if one stops working.
  • Coal tar and selenium sulfide can discolor light or color-treated hair.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H60
Section		Helpful tips
  • Soften thick scalp scale with a little oil before washing, then shampoo it out.
  • Don't scratch or pick at scale.
  • Wash your face with a gentle cleanser and use a light, fragrance-free moisturizer.
  • Use any prescribed cream only as directed. Steroid creams on the face are usually for short courses only.
  • Shaving a beard often helps beard-area flares.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H61
Step · As directed		Medicated shampoo (OTC; picker search “dandruff shampoo”)
As directed on the label: lather into the scalp and other flaky areas, leave on about 5 minutes, then rinse. Once clear, about once a week to keep it away.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Psoriasis: overview cond-psoriasis

Patient sees: Living with psoriasis · Clinician summary: What psoriasis is, triggers, skin care, joint symptoms to watch for, linked health conditions and treatment options.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H66
Section		What it is

Psoriasis is a long-term condition where the immune system speeds up the growth of skin cells. This causes thick, red or dark patches with silvery or white scale, often on the elbows, knees, scalp, lower back and nails. It can itch or hurt.

Psoriasis is not contagious. It often runs in families. There is no cure, but many effective treatments can clear or nearly clear the skin for most people.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H67
Section		Common triggers
  • Stress
  • Infections, especially strep throat
  • Injury to the skin, such as cuts, scrapes, tattoos or sunburn (new patches can form there)
  • Cold, dry weather
  • Smoking and heavy drinking
  • Some medicines. Tell us about all medicines you take, and do not stop a prescribed medicine without asking the doctor who prescribed it.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H68
Section		Caring for your skin
  • Moisturize every day, especially after bathing. Thick creams and ointments help soften scale.
  • Take lukewarm baths or showers. Soaking can soften scale; pat dry afterwards.
  • Don't scratch, pick or scrape off scale. This can cause new patches.
  • Medicated shampoos with salicylic acid or coal tar can help the scalp.
  • Small amounts of sun can help some people, but sunburn can trigger flares. Ask us before using sun or tanning as treatment.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H69
Section		Your whole health

Psoriasis affects more than the skin. Some people get psoriatic arthritis: joint pain, swelling or stiffness, especially in the morning, or a swollen finger or toe. Finding it early helps prevent joint damage.

People with psoriasis also have a higher chance of heart disease, diabetes, high blood pressure and depression. Regular checkups with your primary care doctor, staying active, not smoking and limiting alcohol all help.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H70
Section		Treatment options

Treatment depends on how much skin is involved and how it affects your life. Options include prescription creams and foams, light therapy, pills, and injected medicines called biologics. Use your treatment exactly as prescribed.

If you take a medicine that affects the immune system, tell us about any infection, surgery, pregnancy plans or new vaccines.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H71
Stop and call if		Joint pain, swelling or morning stiffness, or a swollen finger or toe.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H72
Stop and call if		Redness that spreads over most of your body, or new pus bumps on red skin (get seen the same day).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H73
Stop and call if		On a pill or injection: fever, cough, burning when you urinate, or any infection that isn't getting better.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H74
Stop and call if		No improvement after 8 weeks of using your treatment as directed.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H75

Hidradenitis suppurativa (HS) cond-hidradenitis-suppurativa

Patient sees: Living with hidradenitis suppurativa · Clinician summary: What HS is, flare care, daily habits that help, treatment options, and support.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H76
Section		What it is

Hidradenitis suppurativa (HS) is a long-term condition that causes painful lumps, boils and tunnels under the skin. It usually affects areas where skin rubs together: the armpits, groin, buttocks, inner thighs and under the breasts.

HS starts in the hair follicles. It is not caused by poor hygiene and is not contagious. It often runs in families. It tends to come and go in flares. Treatment can reduce flares, pain and scarring, and starting early helps most.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H77
Section		During a flare
  • Warm compresses for 10 minutes a few times a day can ease pain.
  • Do not squeeze, pop or cut lumps yourself.
  • Cover draining areas with soft, absorbent dressings that don't stick. Change them when wet.
  • Over-the-counter pain relievers can help if they are safe for you. Use them as directed on the label.
  • Some flares can be treated in our office with an injection or by draining a lump.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H78
Section		Daily habits that help
  • Wash affected areas with an antiseptic wash such as benzoyl peroxide or chlorhexidine, if we suggest it. Benzoyl peroxide can bleach fabric.
  • Wear loose, soft, breathable clothing and underwear.
  • Reduce shaving in affected areas, or trim instead. Ask us about laser hair removal.
  • If you smoke, quitting is one of the most helpful things you can do for HS.
  • For people with extra weight, weight loss can lessen flares.
  • Keep skin folds cool and dry.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H79
Section		Treatment options

Treatment is matched to how active your HS is. Options include antiseptic washes, antibiotic creams or pills, hormone treatments, injected biologic medicines and other immune-calming pills. Procedures can remove tunnels and scarred areas that keep coming back.

Use all treatment exactly as prescribed. Some HS medicines are not safe in pregnancy, so tell us if you are pregnant or planning to be.

HS can be hard to live with. If it affects your mood, relationships or work, please tell us. Support groups can help too.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H80
Stop and call if		A lump that is very painful, growing fast, or has red, warm skin spreading around it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit

H81
Stop and call if		Fever or chills with a flare.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H82
Stop and call if		New flares despite treatment, or new draining tunnels.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H83
Stop and call if		On a biologic or other immune medicine: signs of infection or a cough that won't go away.☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Hives (urticaria) cond-urticaria

Patient sees: Understanding hives · Clinician summary: Short-term and chronic hives, common triggers, how to take antihistamines, and when swelling needs urgent care.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H85
Section		What it is

Hives are raised, itchy welts on the skin. Each welt usually fades within 24 hours without leaving a mark, while new ones may appear elsewhere. Some people also get deeper swelling of the lips, eyelids, hands or feet, called angioedema.

Hives are caused by a chemical called histamine being released in the skin. They are not contagious.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H86
Section		Short-term and long-term hives

Hives that last less than 6 weeks are called acute. They often follow a virus or cold, and sometimes a food, medicine or insect sting.

Hives that keep coming back for more than 6 weeks are called chronic. In most adults with chronic hives, no outside cause is found. The immune system is set off from within. Allergy testing is usually not helpful for chronic hives. The good news is that chronic hives often go away on their own over time, and treatment controls them well while they last.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H87
Section		Taking your antihistamine

Non-drowsy antihistamines are the main treatment.

  • Take it every day, not just when hives appear. Daily use works much better for chronic hives.
  • Take it as directed on the label, or as we told you. Sometimes we advise taking more than the label says. Only do this if we have told you to.
  • Tell us if you are pregnant or breastfeeding so we can choose the best option.
  • If antihistamines are not enough, other prescription treatments, including an injection, can help.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H88
Section		Helpful tips

Things that can make hives worse:

  • Heat, hot showers and sweating
  • Tight clothing, belts and pressure on the skin
  • Alcohol
  • Stress
  • Aspirin and anti-inflammatory pain relievers such as ibuprofen and naproxen. Ask us before stopping a medicine another doctor prescribed.

Cool compresses and loose, light clothing can ease itch. Taking photos of your hives can help us at your next visit.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H89
Stop and call if		Single welts that last more than 24 to 48 hours, burn more than itch, or leave bruise-like marks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H90
Stop and call if		Hives with fever, joint pain or feeling unwell.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H91
Stop and call if		Hives that are not controlled by your antihistamine, or that disturb your sleep.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H92
Stop and call if		Swelling of the eyelids, hands or feet that keeps coming back.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H93
Stop and call if		Hives that started after a new medicine.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: Zuberbier T et al. The international EAACI/GA2LEN/EuroGuiDerm/APAAACI guideline for the definition, classification, diagnosis, and management of urticaria. Allergy 2022 · American Academy of Dermatology, "Hives: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · American Academy of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology, "Hives (urticaria)" (patient education)

Lichen planus cond-lichen-planus

Patient sees: Understanding lichen planus · Clinician summary: Skin, mouth, genital, scalp and nail lichen planus: what to expect, mouth care and follow-up.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H94
Section		What it is

Lichen planus is an inflammatory condition where the immune system attacks the skin or the lining of the mouth or genitals. It is not contagious and is not caused by anything you did.

  • Skin: flat, itchy, purple or dark bumps, often on the wrists, ankles and lower back.
  • Mouth: white, lacy patches inside the cheeks, or red, sore areas.
  • Genitals: white patches, soreness or sores.
  • Scalp and nails: patchy hair loss, or thin, ridged or split nails.

Sometimes a medicine causes a similar rash. Tell us about all medicines you take. We may also check your blood for hepatitis C, which is linked to lichen planus.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H95
Section		What to expect

Lichen planus on the skin often clears on its own, usually within about 1 to 2 years. Treatment helps with itch and speeds healing. Dark marks often remain after the bumps flatten. They fade slowly over months.

Lichen planus in the mouth, genitals, scalp or nails tends to last longer and may need ongoing treatment. Treating the scalp early can help prevent permanent hair loss.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H96
Section		Caring for your skin and mouth
  • Use your prescribed treatment exactly as directed.
  • Use gentle, fragrance-free soap and moisturize daily.
  • Try not to scratch. Cool compresses can ease itch.
  • For mouth lichen planus: brush gently with a soft toothbrush and keep up with dental checkups.
  • A mild toothpaste without strong flavors may sting less.
  • Avoid foods that sting, such as spicy, salty, acidic or crunchy foods, during flares.
  • Avoid tobacco and limit alcohol.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H97
Section		Follow-up

Lichen planus in the mouth or genitals needs regular checks, because over many years there is a small increased risk of skin cancer in long-lasting sore areas. Keep your follow-up visits, and show us or your dentist any area that changes.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Lichen sclerosus cond-lichen-sclerosus

Patient sees: Understanding lichen sclerosus · Clinician summary: Genital lichen sclerosus: symptoms, long-term steroid ointment use, gentle care, and why regular checks matter.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H103
Section		What it is

Lichen sclerosus is a long-term inflammatory skin condition. It most often affects the skin of the vulva and around the anus, and in men the foreskin and tip of the penis. It can occur at any age.

The skin may look white, shiny, thin or wrinkled, and can itch, tear, bruise or hurt. Some people have pain with sex or when urinating.

It is not an infection, not a sexually transmitted disease and not contagious. It is not caused by poor hygiene.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H104
Section		Treatment

The main treatment is a strong steroid ointment, used exactly as prescribed. It is safe on this skin when used as directed, and it works well for most people.

  • Usually there is a first course to calm the skin, then a lower amount to keep it calm.
  • Many people need some ointment for the long term, even when the skin feels better.
  • Treatment relieves symptoms and helps prevent scarring and changes in the shape of the skin.
  • Use a fingertip amount only, unless we told you otherwise.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H105
Section		Gentle care
  • Wash with plain water or a soap substitute. Avoid soap, bubble bath, scented wipes and douches.
  • Pat dry gently.
  • A plain barrier ointment such as petroleum jelly can protect the skin, and can be put on before urinating if urine stings.
  • Wear loose cotton underwear and avoid tight clothing.
  • Use a water-based lubricant for sex if helpful.
  • Don't scratch. A cool compress can ease itch.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H106
Section		Why regular checks matter

Over many years, people with genital lichen sclerosus have a small increased risk of skin cancer in the affected area. Keeping the condition well controlled is thought to lower this risk.

We will usually see you at least once a year once it is stable. Look at the area with a hand mirror from time to time so you notice any changes.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Perioral dermatitis cond-perioral-dermatitis

Patient sees: Understanding perioral dermatitis · Clinician summary: Small red bumps around the mouth, nose or eyes: common causes including steroid creams, stopping heavy products, and treatment time.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H112
Section		What it is

Perioral dermatitis is a rash of small red or skin-colored bumps, sometimes with tiny pus bumps or flaking, around the mouth. It can also appear around the nose and eyes. There is often a thin clear band of skin right next to the lips. It may burn or itch.

It is not contagious. A common cause is steroid creams on the face. Steroid nasal sprays and inhalers, heavy creams and makeup can also play a part.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H113
Section		What to expect
  • If you have been using a steroid cream on your face, we will usually ask you to stop it. Follow our instructions on how.
  • The rash often gets worse for a few weeks after stopping a steroid. This is expected. Do not restart the steroid cream.
  • Prescription treatment may be a cream or gel, and sometimes an antibiotic pill taken for its anti-inflammatory effect.
  • Improvement usually takes several weeks, and clearing can take 2 to 3 months.
  • It can come back, so keep your routine simple.

Some treatment pills are not used in pregnancy. Tell us if you are pregnant or breastfeeding.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H114
Section		Caring for your skin
  • Keep your routine to the minimum: a gentle cleanser with lukewarm water, and your prescribed treatment.
  • Stop heavy creams, ointments, face oils, foundation and powders on the rash, unless we told you otherwise.
  • If your skin is dry, use only a light, fragrance-free moisturizer we have okayed.
  • Avoid scrubs and products with fragrance.
  • Don't put steroid creams on your face, including over-the-counter hydrocortisone, unless we prescribe it.
  • If you use a steroid nasal spray or inhaler, rinse your mouth and wipe your face after use. Don't stop it without asking your doctor.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H115
Stop and call if		The rash is spreading or very uncomfortable after stopping a steroid cream.☐ Approve

Folliculitis cond-folliculitis

Patient sees: Understanding folliculitis · Clinician summary: Inflamed hair follicles from bacteria, yeast, hot tubs or shaving: home care, prevention and when a bump may be a boil.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H119
Section		What it is

Folliculitis is inflammation of the hair follicles, the tiny openings that hairs grow from. It looks like small red bumps or pus bumps around hairs, and can itch or feel tender. Common causes include:

  • Bacteria on the skin
  • Yeast, often itchy bumps on the chest, back and shoulders
  • Hot tubs or pools that are not well cleaned, a few days after use
  • Shaving, waxing or tight clothing that rubs
  • Sweat and heat

Mild folliculitis often clears in a week or two with good care. Some types keep coming back and need treatment.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H120
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Put a warm, wet cloth on the area for 10 to 15 minutes a few times a day.
  • Wash with a gentle cleanser, or an antibacterial wash such as benzoyl peroxide if we suggested it. Benzoyl peroxide can bleach fabric.
  • Don't squeeze, pick or scratch the bumps.
  • Use your own towel and washcloth and wash them in hot water.
  • Use any prescribed cream, wash or pill exactly as directed.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H121
Section		Preventing it
  • Shower soon after sweating or exercise.
  • Wear loose, breathable clothing.
  • Shave less often, or use an electric razor or trimmer. Shave in the direction the hair grows, with a clean, sharp blade and shaving gel.
  • Don't share razors or towels.
  • Avoid hot tubs that may not be well kept. Shower after using one.
  • For yeast folliculitis, an antifungal wash may help keep it away.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H122
Section		Boils

Sometimes infection goes deeper and forms a boil: a painful, swollen lump that may fill with pus. Warm compresses can help it drain on its own. Do not cut or squeeze a boil. If you keep getting boils, tell us. We may test for the type of bacteria and suggest steps for you and your household.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H123
Stop and call if		A painful lump that is growing, or a boil larger than a pea.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H124
Stop and call if		Redness, warmth or swelling spreading around the bumps, or fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H125
Stop and call if		Bumps that keep coming back, or boils in you or others at home.☐ Approve

Intertrigo (skin-fold rash) cond-intertrigo

Patient sees: Caring for rash in skin folds · Clinician summary: Rash in skin folds from rubbing, heat and moisture; keeping folds dry, barrier creams and signs of yeast or bacterial infection.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H127
Section		What it is

Intertrigo is a rash in places where skin touches skin: under the breasts, in the groin, armpits, belly folds, between the buttocks and between the toes. Rubbing, heat, sweat and trapped moisture irritate the skin. The skin becomes red, raw, itchy or sore, and may smell or ooze.

Warm, moist skin is also a good home for yeast and bacteria, so the rash can become infected. It is more common in hot weather, with diabetes, with extra body weight, and in people who are less mobile.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H128
Section		What to expect

Intertrigo usually improves within 1 to 2 weeks once the folds are kept dry and treatment is started. If yeast or bacteria are involved, we may prescribe an antifungal or antibacterial cream, or a short course of a mild anti-inflammatory cream. Use it exactly as prescribed. Because the folds stay the same, the rash can come back, so daily prevention matters.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H129
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Wash folds daily with lukewarm water and a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser. Rinse well.
  • Dry thoroughly. Pat gently, or use a hair dryer on the cool setting.
  • Keep folds apart with a thin layer of soft cotton or a moisture-wicking fabric made for skin folds.
  • A thin layer of zinc oxide or petrolatum barrier cream can protect raw skin.
  • Wear loose, breathable clothing, and change out of damp clothes quickly.
  • Wear a well-fitting, supportive bra if the rash is under the breasts.
  • Avoid scented products, and don't scrub.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

After washing and drying the fold, a thin layer on irritated skin, as directed on the label.		☐ Approve

Stop and call if		Bright red, raw skin with small red spots spreading outward (possible yeast infection).☐ Approve

☐ Cut
H132
Stop and call if		Yellow crusts, pus, a bad smell, or skin that keeps weeping.☐ Approve

Dry skin and itch (xerosis) cond-xerosis-itch

Patient sees: Relief for dry, itchy skin · Clinician summary: Why skin gets dry, bathing and moisturizing habits, what to look for in a moisturizer, itch relief, and when itch needs a check.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H135
Section		What it is

Dry skin (xerosis) happens when the skin's outer layer loses water and oils. Skin feels tight, rough, flaky or itchy, and can crack. It is often worst on the lower legs, arms and hands.

It is very common, especially in winter, in dry indoor heat, and as we get older. Hot showers, harsh soaps and some medicines can make it worse. Scratching dry skin can lead to rashes and more itch.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H136
Section		Bathing
  • Take short showers or baths, about 5 to 10 minutes.
  • Use lukewarm water, not hot.
  • Use a mild, fragrance-free cleanser, mainly on the underarms, groin and feet. Many areas only need water.
  • Pat dry gently. Don't rub.
  • Put on moisturizer within 3 minutes, while your skin is still damp.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H137
Section		Moisturizing
  • Moisturize at least once a day, all over, and again whenever skin feels dry.
  • Creams and ointments work better than lotions.
  • Look for fragrance-free products with ingredients such as petrolatum, glycerin, ceramides, shea butter or dimethicone.
  • For very rough, scaly skin, creams with urea or lactic acid can help. They may sting on cracked skin.
  • Use a humidifier in your bedroom in dry weather.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H138
Section		Easing the itch
  • Keep nails short. Press or pat an itchy spot instead of scratching.
  • A cool, damp cloth can calm itch quickly.
  • Itch-relief creams with pramoxine or menthol can help. Use them as directed on the label.
  • Wear soft, loose fabrics like cotton. Avoid wool against the skin.
  • Use fragrance-free laundry detergent and skip fabric softener.
  • Drink enough water, though skin care matters more than water for dry skin.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Step · Night		Gentle cleanser (OTC; picker search “gentle cleanser”)
A short lukewarm shower or bath. Use only where needed, then pat dry.		☐ Approve

Stasis dermatitis and leg swelling cond-stasis-dermatitis

Patient sees: Caring for your legs: stasis dermatitis · Clinician summary: Poor vein circulation in the legs: swelling, skin changes, elevation, compression, skin care and warning signs.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H145
Section		What it is

Stasis dermatitis is a rash on the lower legs caused by poor blood flow in the veins. Valves inside the leg veins normally help push blood back up to the heart. When they weaken, blood and fluid pool in the lower legs.

This can cause ankle swelling that is worse at the end of the day, itch, scaly or red patches, brown discoloration, varicose veins, and skin that becomes hard or shiny. Without care, the skin can break down into slow-healing sores (leg ulcers).

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H146
Section		Reduce swelling
  • Raise your legs above the level of your heart for about 30 minutes, several times a day, and when you sleep if you can.
  • Walk every day. Moving your calf muscles pumps blood upward.
  • When sitting or standing for long periods, flex your ankles up and down often.
  • Wear compression stockings or wraps if we recommended them. Put them on in the morning before swelling builds. If you have poor circulation in your arteries, diabetes or heart failure, check with us first.
  • Avoid sitting with legs crossed or standing still for long periods.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H147
Section		Caring for your skin
  • Wash gently with lukewarm water and a fragrance-free cleanser.
  • Moisturize the legs every day. Plain petroleum jelly or a simple fragrance-free cream is best.
  • Skin on the lower legs easily becomes allergic to creams. Avoid products with fragrance, lanolin and antibiotic ointments unless we suggest them.
  • Use prescribed creams for itchy patches exactly as directed.
  • Protect your legs from bumps and scrapes. Even small injuries can turn into slow-healing sores.
  • Don't scratch.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H148
Step · Morning and night		Leg moisturizer (OTC; picker search “petrolatum”)
Once or twice a day, a thin layer on the lower legs, especially after bathing. Plain and fragrance-free.		☐ Approve

Cold sores (herpes simplex) cond-cold-sores

Patient sees: Managing cold sores · Clinician summary: How cold sores start, spread and heal; early treatment, triggers, protecting others and before facial procedures.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H154
Section		What it is

Cold sores are small, painful blisters on or around the lips, caused by the herpes simplex virus. Most adults carry this virus. After the first infection, the virus stays quietly in the nerves and can wake up from time to time.

An outbreak often starts with tingling, burning or itching. Blisters appear within a day or so, break open, crust over and usually heal in 1 to 2 weeks without a scar.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H155
Section		Treatment
  • Prescription antiviral pills work best when started at the first tingle. If you get cold sores often, ask us about keeping a supply at home, or about a daily medicine to prevent them.
  • Over-the-counter docosanol cream can shorten an outbreak a little if started early. Use as directed on the label.
  • Cool compresses and over-the-counter pain relievers can ease discomfort.
  • Keep the sore clean and dry, and use a plain lip balm to keep crusts soft.
  • Don't pick at blisters or crusts.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H156
Section		Protecting others

Cold sores are contagious from the first tingle until the skin is fully healed. The virus can also spread when no sore is showing, though less often.

  • Don't kiss or have oral sex during an outbreak.
  • Don't share cups, utensils, lip balm, razors or towels.
  • Wash your hands after touching the sore. Avoid touching your eyes.
  • Keep away from newborn babies, people with eczema and people with weak immune systems while you have a sore.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H157
Section		Helpful tips

Common triggers include sun, wind, stress, illness, fever, tiredness, menstrual periods, dental work and injury to the lips.

  • Wear a lip balm with SPF 30 or higher outdoors.
  • If you are having a laser treatment, peel, filler or other procedure near the mouth, tell us you get cold sores. We may give you medicine to prevent an outbreak.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H158
Stop and call if		Eye pain, redness, light sensitivity or blurred vision during an outbreak (get seen the same day).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H159
Stop and call if		Sores spreading over eczema or other broken skin.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H160
Stop and call if		A sore that hasn't healed after 2 weeks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H161
Stop and call if		Frequent outbreaks, or a weakened immune system.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H162
Stop and call if		Fever or feeling very unwell with a first outbreak.☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Shingles (herpes zoster) cond-shingles

Patient sees: Recovering from shingles · Clinician summary: What shingles is, early antiviral treatment, rash and pain care, protecting others, warning signs and the vaccine.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H163
Section		What it is

Shingles is a painful rash caused by the chickenpox virus. After chickenpox, the virus stays inactive in the nerves for years. Later, often with age, stress or a weakened immune system, it can wake up.

Shingles often starts with burning, tingling or pain on one side of the body. A few days later, a band or patch of blisters appears in the same area. The blisters crust over in about 7 to 10 days, and the rash usually heals in 2 to 4 weeks.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H164
Section		Treatment
  • Antiviral pills work best when started within 3 days of the rash appearing. Take them exactly as prescribed and finish the course.
  • Pain can be strong. Over-the-counter pain relievers, cool compresses and prescription pain medicine can help. Tell us if your pain is not controlled.
  • Some people have nerve pain that lasts after the rash heals. Early treatment may lower that risk, and there are treatments that help.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H165
Section		Caring for the rash
  • Keep the rash clean and dry. Wash gently with mild soap and water.
  • Cover blisters with a loose, non-stick dressing.
  • Cool, damp compresses can soothe pain and itch.
  • Wear loose, soft clothing.
  • Don't pick or scratch the blisters.
  • Avoid thick creams or adhesive bandages on open blisters, unless we told you otherwise.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H166
Section		Protecting others

You can't give someone shingles, but until all blisters have crusted, you can give chickenpox to someone who has never had chickenpox or the vaccine.

  • Keep the rash covered.
  • Wash your hands often.
  • Until the blisters crust over, stay away from pregnant people who have not had chickenpox, newborn babies, and people with weak immune systems.

A shingles vaccine is recommended for most adults aged 50 and older, and for some younger adults with weakened immune systems, even if you have already had shingles. Ask us or your primary care doctor.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H167

H170

Scabies (adult and household) cond-scabies

Patient sees: Treating scabies in your household · Clinician summary: How scabies spreads, treating everyone at once, cleaning bedding and clothes, and why itch can last after treatment.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H172
Section		What it is

Scabies is a very itchy rash caused by tiny mites that burrow into the top layer of skin. Itch is often worst at night. The rash is common between the fingers, on the wrists, elbows, waist, buttocks, genitals and around the nipples.

Scabies spreads mainly through close skin-to-skin contact that lasts a while, such as living together or sexual contact. It is not a sign of being dirty. Pets do not spread human scabies. Itch can start 2 to 6 weeks after you first get it, so others may have it without symptoms yet.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H173
Section		Treating everyone
  • Everyone in your household and close contacts, including sexual partners, should be treated at the same time, even if they don't itch.
  • Use your treatment exactly as prescribed. For cream, apply to clean, dry skin over the whole body as directed, usually from the neck down, including between fingers and toes, under nails, and the genitals.
  • Leave it on for the full time on the label, then wash it off.
  • Reapply to hands if you wash them during that time.
  • A second treatment is often needed. Follow the schedule we gave you.
  • Tell us if anyone is pregnant, breastfeeding, or a young child. Some treatments are not used for them.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H174
Section		Cleaning your home

On the day of treatment:

  • Wash bedding, towels and clothes used in the past 3 days in hot water and dry them on a hot setting.
  • Items that can't be washed can be sealed in a plastic bag for at least 3 days.
  • Vacuum furniture, carpets and car seats.

You don't need to fumigate your home or treat pets.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H175
Section		What to expect

Itch often continues for 2 to 4 weeks after successful treatment. This is the skin calming down, not a sign treatment failed. Cool compresses, moisturizer and an antihistamine can help. Don't re-treat on your own: too much treatment can irritate the skin.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit

H178
Stop and call if		Sores with pus, crusting or spreading redness.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H179
Stop and call if		Thick, crusted skin on anyone in the household, especially an older adult or someone with a weak immune system.☐ Approve

Sources: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, "Scabies" (treatment and prevention information) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Scabies: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education)

Tinea: athlete's foot, jock itch and ringworm cond-tinea

Patient sees: Treating fungal skin infections · Clinician summary: Athlete's foot, jock itch and body ringworm: how they spread, using antifungal creams correctly, and preventing return.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H180
Section		What it is

Tinea is a common skin infection caused by a fungus, not a worm. It has different names depending on where it is:

  • Athlete's foot: itchy, peeling or cracked skin between the toes or on the soles.
  • Jock itch: an itchy, red rash in the groin and inner thighs.
  • Ringworm: a round, scaly patch on the body, often clearer in the middle with a raised edge.

The fungus loves warm, damp places. It spreads by touching infected skin, pets (often cats and dogs), or damp floors in locker rooms and showers. It can spread from your feet to other parts of your body.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H181
Section		Treatment
  • Most tinea on the skin clears with an antifungal cream such as terbinafine, clotrimazole or miconazole. Use as directed on the label, or as we prescribed.
  • Spread the cream over the rash and a little beyond its edge.
  • Keep using it for the full time on the label or that we gave you, even if the rash looks gone sooner. Stopping early lets it come back.
  • If you have athlete's foot and jock itch, treat both at the same time.
  • Don't use steroid creams on a fungal rash unless we prescribed them. They can make it spread.
  • Tinea on the scalp, beard or nails needs prescription treatment.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H182
Section		Preventing it
  • Dry well after bathing, especially between the toes and in the groin.
  • Wear breathable socks and change them daily, or more often if they get damp.
  • Rotate shoes so each pair can dry out.
  • Wear sandals in locker rooms, public showers and pool areas.
  • Put your socks on before your underwear, so the fungus doesn't travel from feet to groin.
  • Don't share towels, shoes or razors.
  • If a pet has patches of hair loss, have it checked by a vet.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H183

Tinea versicolor cond-tinea-versicolor

Patient sees: Understanding tinea versicolor · Clinician summary: Light or dark spots on the chest and back from a skin yeast: antifungal washes, slow return of skin color, and preventing return.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H189
Section		What it is

Tinea versicolor is a common skin condition caused by overgrowth of a yeast that normally lives on everyone's skin. It causes small spots or patches that can be lighter or darker than your skin, or pink. They are usually on the chest, back, shoulders and upper arms, and may be slightly scaly or itchy.

It is not contagious and is not caused by poor hygiene. It is more common in hot, humid weather, with sweating, and in teens and young adults.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H190
Section		Treatment
  • Antifungal shampoos with selenium sulfide or ketoconazole can be used as a body wash. Spread on the affected skin, leave on for the time on the label or that we told you, then rinse.
  • Antifungal creams work for small areas.
  • For widespread or stubborn cases, we may prescribe antifungal pills. Tell us about all your medicines, and if you are pregnant or breastfeeding.
  • Use your treatment exactly as directed.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H191
Section		What to expect

The yeast is usually cleared within a few weeks. But your skin color can take several months to even out after the yeast is gone. Spots that stay lighter after treatment do not mean the treatment failed.

Tanning makes the spots stand out more, because the affected skin doesn't tan the same way. Daily sunscreen helps your skin tone stay even while it recovers.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H192
Section		Preventing it

Tinea versicolor often comes back, especially in warm months.

  • Use the antifungal wash once or twice a month, or more often in hot weather, if we suggest it.
  • Wear loose, breathable clothing.
  • Shower after sweating.
  • Avoid heavy, oily skin products on the chest and back.

Fungal nail infection (onychomycosis) cond-onychomycosis

Patient sees: Treating nail fungus · Clinician summary: Fungal nail infection: confirming the diagnosis, pill and topical options, how long nails take, and preventing return.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H197
Section		What it is

A fungal nail infection makes nails thick, discolored (white, yellow or brown), crumbly or lifted from the nail bed. It is much more common in toenails. It often starts with athlete's foot and is more common with age, diabetes and poor circulation.

Other conditions, such as psoriasis or injury to the nail, can look the same. We usually test a nail clipping to confirm fungus before starting treatment.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H198
Section		Treatment options
  • Antifungal pills work best for many people. We may check blood tests before or during treatment. Take them exactly as prescribed and tell us about all your other medicines.
  • Prescription nail solutions are painted on the nail as directed, usually every day for many months. They work best for mild infections.
  • Over-the-counter nail products rarely cure nail fungus.
  • Treatment is not always needed. If the nail doesn't bother you and you are healthy, waiting is a reasonable choice.

Tell us if you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy or breastfeeding.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H199
Section		What to expect

Treatment kills the fungus, but the damaged nail does not heal. A healthy nail has to grow out to replace it. Fingernails take about 6 months and toenails 12 to 18 months to grow out fully. You may see clear nail growing in from the base first.

Even after successful treatment, nail fungus often comes back, so prevention matters.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H200
Section		Helpful tips
  • Treat athlete's foot when you have it.
  • Keep feet clean and dry, and change socks daily.
  • Wear shoes that fit well and rotate pairs so they can dry.
  • Wear sandals in locker rooms and public showers.
  • Trim nails straight across and keep them short. Use separate clippers for infected nails.
  • An antifungal powder in shoes may help prevent return.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H201
Stop and call if		Pain, redness, swelling or pus around a nail.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H202
Stop and call if		Diabetes or poor circulation with any foot sore, crack or color change.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H203
Stop and call if		On antifungal pills: yellow skin or eyes, dark urine, pale stools, ongoing nausea or stomach pain, or unusual tiredness.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H204
Stop and call if		On antifungal pills: a new rash, loss of taste or smell, or low mood.☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Keratosis pilaris cond-keratosis-pilaris

Patient sees: Smoothing keratosis pilaris · Clinician summary: Harmless rough bumps on the arms and thighs: gentle care, exfoliating moisturizers and realistic expectations.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H205
Section		What it is

Keratosis pilaris (KP) causes small, rough bumps that feel like sandpaper. They are most common on the backs of the upper arms, the thighs, buttocks and sometimes the cheeks. The bumps can be skin-colored, red or brown, and are occasionally itchy.

The bumps are plugs of keratin, a protein in skin, that block hair follicles. KP is harmless and very common. It often runs in families and is more common with dry skin and eczema.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H206
Section		What to expect

There is no cure, but the skin can be made smoother and less red. KP is often worse in winter and better in summer. It often improves with age.

Treatment takes time. It can take several weeks of daily use to notice a difference, and the bumps return if you stop. Think of it as an ongoing routine, like brushing your teeth.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H207
Section		Caring for your skin
  • Use a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser and lukewarm water.
  • After bathing, while skin is damp, apply a moisturizer containing urea, lactic acid or salicylic acid, as directed on the label. These gently loosen the plugs.
  • They may sting at first, especially on irritated skin. Use them every other day at first if so.
  • On days you don't use it, or if it stings, use a plain fragrance-free moisturizer.
  • Don't scrub hard, pick or squeeze the bumps. It makes redness worse.
  • Use a humidifier in dry weather.

If we prescribe a retinoid cream, do not use it if you are pregnant or trying to get pregnant.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H208
Step · Night		Exfoliating moisturizer (OTC; picker search “urea lotion”)
After bathing, on damp skin over the bumpy areas, as directed on the label. If it stings, use it every other day.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H209
Step · Night		Gentle cleanser (OTC; picker search “gentle cleanser”)
Lukewarm water and a fragrance-free cleanser. No scrubbing. Pat dry.		☐ Approve

Hyperhidrosis (excessive sweating) cond-hyperhidrosis

Patient sees: Managing excessive sweating · Clinician summary: Excessive sweating: causes, using clinical-strength antiperspirant, treatment options from wipes to injections, and daily tips.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H213
Section		What it is

Hyperhidrosis means sweating much more than the body needs to stay cool. It most often affects the underarms, palms, soles of the feet or face. It usually starts in childhood or the teen years and often runs in families. It is a medical condition, not a sign of poor hygiene or nerves.

Less often, heavy sweating is caused by another health problem or a medicine. This is more likely if the sweating is all over your body, started in adulthood, or happens during sleep. We may check for these causes.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H214
Section		Using antiperspirant

Clinical-strength antiperspirant is usually the first treatment.

  • Apply it at bedtime to completely dry skin, then wash it off in the morning. Sweat glands are quiet at night, so it works better.
  • Don't apply it right after shaving or on broken skin.
  • It can also be used on hands and feet.
  • It may itch or sting. Using it less often, or a gentle moisturizer in the morning, can help.
  • Once sweating is controlled, many people only need it a few times a week.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H215
Section		Other treatment options

If antiperspirant is not enough, other options include:

  • Prescription wipes or creams that block sweat signals. Wash your hands after use and keep away from the eyes.
  • Iontophoresis: soaking hands or feet in water while a gentle electric current passes through.
  • Injections of a muscle-relaxing toxin, which can reduce sweating for several months.
  • Pills that reduce sweating all over the body. They can cause dry mouth, constipation and blurred vision.
  • Procedures for the underarms.

Use all treatments exactly as prescribed. Tell us if you are pregnant or breastfeeding.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H216
Section		Helpful tips
  • Wear loose, breathable or moisture-wicking fabrics.
  • Choose darker colors or patterns to hide sweat marks, or try underarm shields.
  • Wear moisture-wicking socks and change them during the day. Rotate shoes so they dry out.
  • Absorbent insoles and foot powder help with sweaty feet.
  • Carry a spare shirt or socks if it helps you feel at ease.
  • Sweating can affect confidence and daily life. Let us know how it affects you.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H217
Step · Night		Clinical-strength antiperspirant (OTC; picker search “clinical strength antiperspirant”)
At bedtime on completely dry skin, as directed on the label. Wash off in the morning. Not on freshly shaved or broken skin.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H218

Actinic keratoses: overview and treatment cond-actinic-keratoses

Patient sees: Actinic keratoses (sun damage spots) · Clinician summary: What AKs are, why we treat them, freezing vs. field creams, what a treatment reaction looks like, sun protection.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H223
Section		What they are

Actinic keratoses (AKs) are rough, scaly spots caused by years of sun exposure. They are often easier to feel than to see. They show up most on the face, ears, scalp, lips, backs of the hands and forearms.

AKs are called "precancers." Most never become cancer, but a small number can turn into a skin cancer called squamous cell carcinoma. That is why we treat them and keep an eye on your skin.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H224
Section		How we treat them
  • Freezing (cryotherapy): we spray very cold liquid on single spots. The area may sting, then turn red, blister or crust. It usually heals in 1 to 3 weeks.
  • Creams for a whole area (field treatment): you put a prescription cream on a larger patch of skin, as prescribed. This treats spots you can see and ones that are just starting.
  • Light treatment (photodynamic therapy): we apply a liquid to the skin, then shine a special light on it.
  • Thick or unusual spots may need a biopsy (a small skin sample) to make sure they are not cancer.

If you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy or breastfeeding, tell us. Some creams, such as 5-fluorouracil, are not used in pregnancy.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H225
Section		What to expect with a treatment cream

With field creams, the skin usually gets red, scaly and crusty, and may feel sore or raw. This reaction is expected and means the cream is working on damaged skin. It often looks worse before it looks better.

  • Use the cream only for the number of days we prescribed.
  • Wash your hands after applying it, and keep it away from your eyes, lips and the inside of your nose.
  • A plain moisturizer or petrolatum can soothe the skin once you finish, unless we told you otherwise.
  • The skin usually heals within a few weeks after you stop.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H226
Section		Protecting your skin from here on

Having AKs means your skin has had a lot of sun. New spots can keep appearing, so protection matters for the rest of your life.

  • Use a broad-spectrum sunscreen, SPF 30 or higher, every day on exposed skin. Reapply every 2 hours outdoors.
  • Wear a wide-brimmed hat, sunglasses and long sleeves when you can.
  • Seek shade, especially from about 10 a.m. to 4 p.m.
  • Use a lip balm with SPF.
  • Never use tanning beds.
  • Check your skin once a month and keep your regular skin checks with us.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H227
Stop and call if		A spot that grows quickly, becomes thick, tender or painful, or bleeds.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H228
Stop and call if		A spot that comes back or does not go away after treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H229
Stop and call if		A cream reaction that is much stronger than we described: open sores, severe pain, or redness spreading well past where you applied it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H230
Stop and call if		A sore, scaly or cracked area on the lip that does not heal.☐ Approve

Guidelines of care for the management of actinic keratosis. J Am Acad Dermatol 2021 (AAD)

Basal cell carcinoma: after diagnosis cond-basal-cell-carcinoma

Patient sees: You have a basal cell carcinoma: what happens next · Clinician summary: What a BCC diagnosis means, the usual treatment options, follow-up skin checks and sun protection.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H231
Section		What it is

Basal cell carcinoma (BCC) is the most common type of skin cancer. It is mostly caused by sun exposure over many years.

Hearing the word "cancer" can be scary. The good news is that BCC grows slowly and very rarely spreads to other parts of the body. When it is treated, it is usually cured. If it is left alone, though, it can keep growing deeper and damage the skin and tissue around it. That is why we treat it.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H232
Section		How it is treated

The best treatment depends on the type of BCC, its size and where it is. We will talk with you about which is right for you. Common choices:

  • Surgical removal (excision): we numb the skin, cut out the cancer with a small border of normal skin, and close it with stitches.
  • Mohs surgery: the cancer is removed in thin layers and checked under a microscope during the visit until it is all gone. Often used on the face, ears and for larger BCCs or ones that came back.
  • Scraping and burning (curettage and electrodesiccation): used for some small, low-risk BCCs on the body.
  • Prescription creams or light treatment: used for some very shallow (superficial) BCCs.
  • Radiation: an option for some people who cannot have surgery.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H233
Section		After treatment

If you had surgery, follow the wound care instructions we gave you. Most scars look pink or raised at first and fade over several months.

Having one BCC means you have a higher chance of getting another skin cancer in the future. This is not a sign that the treatment failed. It reflects the sun damage your skin has already had.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H234
Section		Follow-up and sun protection
  • Keep your skin check appointments, usually at least once a year, or as often as we recommend.
  • Check your own skin once a month in good light, using a mirror for your back or asking someone to help.
  • Look for a new shiny or pearly bump, a pink scaly patch, or a sore that bleeds and does not heal.
  • Use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher every day, wear a hat and protective clothing, and seek shade.
  • Never use tanning beds.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H235
Stop and call if		A sore that bleeds, scabs over and opens again, or does not heal in about 4 weeks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H236
Stop and call if		A new shiny or pearly bump, or a pink scaly patch that keeps growing.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H237
Stop and call if		A bump, sore or scab that appears in or next to the scar where the cancer was treated.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H238
Stop and call if		Redness, warmth, swelling or pus at a surgery site, or a fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Basal cell carcinoma: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · Skin Cancer Foundation, "Basal Cell Carcinoma" (patient education) · Kim JYS et al. Guidelines of care for the management of basal cell carcinoma. J Am Acad Dermatol 2018 (AAD Work Group)
