Squamous cell carcinoma: after diagnosis cond-squamous-cell-carcinoma

Patient sees: You have a squamous cell carcinoma: what happens next · Clinician summary: What an SCC diagnosis means, treatment options, follow-up including lymph node checks, higher-risk groups, sun protection.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H239
Section		What it is

Squamous cell carcinoma (SCC) is the second most common type of skin cancer. It is mostly caused by sun exposure over many years. It often looks like a rough, scaly patch, a firm bump, or a sore that does not heal.

Most SCCs are found early and are cured with treatment. Unlike basal cell carcinoma, an SCC has a small chance of spreading, most often to nearby lymph nodes (small glands in the neck, armpits and groin). The risk is higher for large or deep SCCs, ones on the lip or ear, and in people with a weakened immune system. That is why we treat SCC promptly and follow you closely afterward.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H240
Section		How it is treated

The best treatment depends on the SCC's size, depth and location. We will go over your options.

  • Surgical removal (excision) with a border of normal skin, closed with stitches.
  • Mohs surgery: removing the cancer in thin layers, checked under a microscope during the visit. Often used on the face, ears, lips and for higher-risk SCCs.
  • Scraping and burning (curettage and electrodesiccation) for some small, low-risk SCCs.
  • For SCC that is only in the top layer of skin ("in situ"), prescription creams or light treatment may be options.
  • Radiation may be used for some people.

A few higher-risk SCCs need extra tests or care from other specialists. We will tell you if that applies to you.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H241
Section		Follow-up

After an SCC, you have a higher chance of getting another skin cancer. Regular checks help us find any new ones early, when they are easiest to treat.

  • Keep your skin check appointments as often as we recommend. This is often every few months at first for higher-risk SCCs, and at least once a year after that.
  • At visits we may also feel the lymph nodes near the treated area.
  • If you have had an organ transplant, take medicines that weaken your immune system, or have a condition such as chronic leukemia, tell us. You will likely need more frequent checks.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H242
Section		Caring for your skin at home
  • Check your skin once a month, including your scalp, ears, lips, and the backs of your hands.
  • Gently feel the skin around your scar and the lymph nodes in your neck, armpits or groin on that side.
  • Use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher every day. Reapply every 2 hours outdoors.
  • Wear a wide-brimmed hat, protective clothing and SPF lip balm.
  • Never use tanning beds.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H243
Stop and call if		A new lump under the skin near your scar, or in your neck, armpit or groin, that lasts more than 2 weeks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H244
Stop and call if		A new scaly patch or bump that grows quickly, is tender, or bleeds.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H245
Stop and call if		A sore that does not heal in about 4 weeks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H246
Stop and call if		Any change, bump or sore in the scar where the cancer was treated.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H247
Stop and call if		Redness, warmth, swelling or pus at a surgery site, or a fever.☐ Approve

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Squamous cell carcinoma: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · Skin Cancer Foundation, "Squamous Cell Carcinoma" (patient education) · Alam M et al. Melanoma: after diagnosis (next steps, follow-up, family) cond-melanoma-after-diagnosis

Patient sees: You have a melanoma: what happens next · Clinician summary: What the pathology report guides, wide excision and possible lymph node testing, follow-up schedule, self-checks, what it means for family.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H248
Section		What it means

Melanoma is a skin cancer that starts in the cells that give skin its color. It is serious, but when it is found early it is usually treated with surgery alone, and most people do very well.

Your biopsy report tells us important details, such as how deep the melanoma goes (its thickness). These details decide your next steps. We will go over your report with you. It is normal to feel worried. Write down your questions and bring someone with you to visits if that helps.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H249
Section		What happens next
  • Wider removal (wide local excision): almost everyone with melanoma has a second, larger surgery. We remove a border of normal-looking skin around the biopsy site to make sure no melanoma cells are left. The size of the border depends on the melanoma's depth.
  • Lymph node testing: for some melanomas, we may talk about a sentinel lymph node biopsy. This checks the first lymph node the melanoma would drain to. Many thin melanomas do not need it.
  • Other tests or specialists: depending on the stage, some people get scans or see a cancer doctor (oncologist) or surgeon. Many people with early melanoma do not need scans.

We will explain which of these apply to you.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H250
Section		Follow-up visits

After melanoma, you need regular full skin exams. People who have had one melanoma have a higher chance of getting another.

  • Visits are often every 3 to 12 months for the first few years, depending on your stage, then at least once a year for life. We will set your schedule.
  • At visits we check all your skin and may feel your lymph nodes.
  • Keep every appointment, even when you feel well.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H251
Section		Checking your own skin

Check your whole body once a month in good light, with a full-length and a hand mirror, or with a partner's help. Include your scalp, between your toes, the soles of your feet, under your nails and the genital area. Photos can help you notice changes.

Look for the ABCDEs: Asymmetry, uneven Borders, more than one Color, Diameter larger than a pencil eraser, and Evolving (changing). Also look for an "ugly duckling": a spot that looks different from your other moles.

Gently feel around your scar and the lymph nodes in your neck, armpits and groin.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H252
Section		What it means for your family

Your parents, brothers, sisters and children have a higher chance of melanoma than average. Encourage them to:

  • Get a full skin exam by a dermatologist.
  • Check their own skin monthly and protect it from the sun.
  • Avoid tanning beds.

If two or more people in your family have had melanoma, or if family members have had pancreatic cancer, tell us. We may suggest genetic counseling.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H253
Section		Protecting your skin
  • Use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher every day, and reapply every 2 hours outdoors.
  • Wear a wide-brimmed hat, sunglasses and protective clothing.
  • Seek shade, especially from about 10 a.m. to 4 p.m.
  • Never use tanning beds.
  • Ask us before starting any vitamin D supplement, since you may be getting less sun.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H254
Stop and call if		A new mole or spot, or a mole that changes in size, shape or color, itches, or bleeds.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H255
Stop and call if		Any lump, bump, dark spot or color change in or next to your scar.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H256
Stop and call if		A swollen lymph node or lump in your neck, armpit or groin that lasts more than 2 weeks.☐ Approve

☐ Cut

Atypical (dysplastic) moles cond-atypical-moles

Patient sees: Atypical moles: what they mean and how to watch them · Clinician summary: What an atypical mole or biopsy result means, why removing all moles is not needed, self-exams, family checks.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H259
Section		What they are

Atypical moles (also called dysplastic nevi) are moles that look different from common moles. They are often larger than a pencil eraser, with uneven edges or more than one shade of brown or pink.

An atypical mole is not cancer. Most never turn into melanoma. But people who have atypical moles, especially many of them, have a higher chance of getting melanoma somewhere on their skin over their lifetime. Many melanomas start as a brand-new spot rather than in an existing mole. So the goal is to watch all of your skin, not just these moles.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H260
Section		If your biopsy showed an atypical mole

Under the microscope, atypical moles are graded as mild, moderate or severe.

  • Mild: often no more treatment is needed, even if the edges of the sample were not completely clear.
  • Moderate or severe: we may recommend removing a little more skin around the site to make sure the whole mole is gone.

We will tell you which applies to you. Sometimes a little color comes back in the scar. Let us know if it does, so we can check it.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H261
Section		Why we don't remove every mole

Removing all of your moles does not prevent melanoma, because many melanomas show up as new spots. It also leaves many scars. Instead, we watch your skin closely and remove moles that change or look worrisome. Photos of your skin, taken by you or by us, make changes easier to spot.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H262
Section		Watching your skin at home

Check your whole body once a month in good light. Use a full-length mirror and a hand mirror, or ask someone to help with your back and scalp.

  • Look for the ABCDEs: Asymmetry, uneven Borders, more than one Color, Diameter larger than a pencil eraser, and Evolving (changing).
  • Look for an "ugly duckling": a mole that looks different from all your others.
  • Taking photos every few months helps you compare.
  • Keep your regular skin checks with us.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H263
Section		Sun protection and family
  • Use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher every day. Wear hats and protective clothing.
  • Never use tanning beds.
  • Atypical moles often run in families. If you have many of them or a family history of melanoma, your parents, siblings and children should have their skin checked too.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H264
Stop and call if		A mole that changes in size, shape or color, or starts to itch, crust or bleed.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H265
Stop and call if		A new mole as an adult that keeps growing or looks different from your other moles.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H266
Stop and call if		Dark color coming back in or around a biopsy scar.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H267
Stop and call if		A family member is diagnosed with melanoma (tell us at your next visit).☐ Approve

Seborrheic keratoses cond-seborrheic-keratoses

Patient sees: Seborrheic keratoses (harmless "stuck-on" growths) · Clinician summary: What SKs are, that they are harmless, removal options and what to expect, when a spot needs a second look.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H268
Section		What they are

Seborrheic keratoses (SKs) are very common, harmless skin growths. They look waxy, rough or "stuck on," and can be tan, brown or black. They often appear on the chest, back, face and scalp, and most people get more of them as they age. They tend to run in families.

SKs are not cancer and do not turn into cancer. They are not contagious.

In people with brown or black skin, many small dark bumps on the face and neck (called dermatosis papulosa nigra) are a closely related, harmless condition.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H269
Section		Do they need treatment?

No. Treatment is only needed if a growth gets irritated, itches, catches on clothing or jewelry, or bothers you. If removal is only for looks, insurance often treats it as cosmetic, so ask us about this first.

Ways we remove them:

  • Freezing (cryotherapy)
  • Scraping (curettage), sometimes with light burning to stop bleeding
  • Burning with an electric needle (electrocautery) or laser

The treated skin usually crusts and heals in 1 to 3 weeks. It may stay lighter or darker than the skin around it for a while, especially in darker skin. Removing an SK does not stop new ones from appearing.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H270
Section		Caring for them at home
  • Don't pick, scratch or try to cut them off. This can cause bleeding, infection and scars.
  • A plain fragrance-free moisturizer can calm dry, itchy growths.
  • If one gets irritated by a bra strap or waistband, a soft bandage can help until we see it.
  • Keep checking all your skin monthly. Seborrheic keratoses can look like other spots, so point out any that look different from the rest.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H271
Stop and call if		A growth that bleeds without being injured, grows quickly, or looks different from your other spots.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H272
Stop and call if		Many new growths that appear suddenly over a few weeks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H273
Stop and call if		A treated spot that does not heal within about 4 weeks, or comes back looking different.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Seborrheic keratoses: Overview" and "Seborrheic keratoses: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education)

☐ Approve as written☐ Approve with edits☐ Don't publish

Epidermoid cysts cond-epidermoid-cysts

Patient sees: Epidermoid cysts · Clinician summary: What a cyst is, why squeezing makes it worse, inflamed cysts, drainage vs. full removal.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H274
Section		What it is

An epidermoid cyst is a small sac under the skin. The sac is filled with keratin, a soft, cheesy material that skin makes. It may have a bad smell if it leaks. Cysts are often on the face, neck, chest, back or behind the ears. Many have a tiny dark pore on top.

People sometimes call these "sebaceous cysts." They are not cancer and are not contagious.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H275
Section		What to expect

A cyst can stay the same size for years. Sometimes the sac breaks under the skin, and the area becomes red, swollen and painful. This is called an inflamed cyst. It is often not an infection, but it can look like one.

  • A calm cyst that doesn't bother you can be left alone.
  • An inflamed cyst may need an injection to calm it, or a small cut to drain it. Draining relieves pain, but the sac stays behind, so the cyst can come back.
  • Removing the whole sac (a minor surgery, usually done once the cyst is calm) gives the best chance it will not return. It leaves a scar.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H276
Section		Caring for it at home

Do:

  • Leave it alone and keep the skin clean.
  • For a sore, swollen cyst, hold a warm, damp washcloth on it for 10 to 15 minutes, a few times a day.
  • Cover it with a bandage if it drains.

Don't:

  • Squeeze, pop or poke it with a needle. This pushes the contents deeper, causes inflammation, and makes scarring and regrowth more likely.
  • Try to cut it out yourself.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H277
Stop and call if		The cyst becomes very painful, hot or swollen, or red streaks spread from it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H278
Stop and call if		Fever or chills along with a sore cyst.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H279
Stop and call if		Pus draining from the cyst.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H280
Stop and call if		A lump that grows quickly, feels hard, or seems stuck in place.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H281
Stop and call if		The cyst comes back after removal.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Cysts: Overview" and "Cysts: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education)

☐ Approve as written☐ Approve with edits☐ Don't publish
Notes:

Lipomas cond-lipomas

Patient sees: Lipomas (soft fatty lumps) · Clinician summary: What a lipoma is, when removal makes sense, and the features that call for a closer look.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H282
Section		What it is

A lipoma is a soft, rubbery lump made of fat cells. It sits just under the skin and usually moves a little when you press on it. Lipomas grow slowly and are usually painless. They are common on the back, shoulders, neck, arms and trunk.

Lipomas are not cancer. Some people get several, and they can run in families.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H283
Section		Do they need treatment?

Most lipomas do not need treatment. We may suggest removing one if it:

  • Is painful or presses on something
  • Keeps growing
  • Bothers you because of how it looks or where it is
  • Looks or feels unusual, so we want to be sure what it is

The usual treatment is a minor surgery under local numbing to cut it out. This leaves a scar. For some lipomas, especially large or deep ones, we may order an imaging test such as an ultrasound or MRI first. A lipoma can occasionally come back after removal.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H284
Section		Keeping an eye on it
  • Notice its size from time to time. A photo next to a coin or ruler makes it easier to compare.
  • There is nothing you need to put on it.
  • Don't try to squeeze or drain it. Unlike a cyst, a lipoma has no opening and nothing to squeeze out.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H285
Stop and call if		The lump grows quickly, or becomes larger than about 5 cm (2 inches, about the size of a golf ball).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H286
Stop and call if		The lump becomes hard, painful, or stops moving when you press on it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H287
Stop and call if		A new lump that feels deep, under the muscle.☐ Approve

Skin tags and cherry angiomas cond-skin-tags-cherry-angiomas

Patient sees: Skin tags and cherry angiomas · Clinician summary: Two common harmless growths: what they are, removal options, why not to remove them at home.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H289
Section		Skin tags

Skin tags are small, soft flaps of skin that hang from a thin stalk. They are common on the neck, armpits, eyelids, groin and under the breasts, where skin rubs against skin or clothing.

They become more common with age, weight gain and pregnancy. People with high blood sugar or diabetes also tend to get more of them. If you have many skin tags, ask your primary care doctor whether a blood sugar test makes sense.

Skin tags are harmless and do not turn into cancer.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H290
Section		Cherry angiomas

Cherry angiomas are small, bright red to purple dots or bumps made of tiny blood vessels. Most adults get some, and they often increase after age 30. They are usually on the trunk, arms and legs.

They are harmless. They can bleed a lot if scratched or nicked because they are made of blood vessels.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H291
Section		Removal

Neither needs treatment. We can remove them if they get irritated, catch on clothing or jewelry, bleed, or bother you. If removal is only for looks, insurance often treats it as cosmetic, so ask us first.

  • Skin tags: snipped off, frozen, or burned with an electric needle.
  • Cherry angiomas: treated with a laser or an electric needle.

The area usually heals in 1 to 2 weeks. New ones may still appear over time.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H292
Section		Caring for them at home
  • Don't cut, tie off or freeze skin tags yourself. They can bleed more than you expect, and the area can get infected or scar.
  • Avoid over-the-counter "removal" products unless we recommend one. Some contain harsh acids that can burn the skin.
  • If an angioma or skin tag bleeds, press firmly on it with clean gauze for 10 to 15 minutes without peeking.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H293
Stop and call if		A red bump that grows quickly over a few weeks and bleeds easily.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H294
Stop and call if		A spot that keeps bleeding after 15 minutes of firm pressure.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H295
Stop and call if		A skin tag that turns dark, swollen and painful (it may have twisted).☐ Approve

☐ Cut
H296
Stop and call if		A growth that changes color, shape or size, or looks different from your others.☐ Approve

Warts in adults (common and plantar) cond-warts-adults

Patient sees: Warts: common and plantar warts · Clinician summary: What warts are, home salicylic acid routine, office treatments, preventing spread, foot cautions with diabetes.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H297
Section		What they are

Warts are skin growths caused by a common virus called human papillomavirus (HPV). The virus infects the top layer of skin, often through tiny cuts.

  • Common warts: rough, raised bumps, often on the hands and fingers.
  • Plantar warts: on the soles of the feet. Walking presses them flat, so they can hurt like a pebble in your shoe. They often have tiny black dots, which are small clotted blood vessels.

Warts spread by touch and from damp floors in showers and pool areas. The types that cause hand and foot warts are different from those that cause genital warts.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H298
Section		What to expect

Many warts go away on their own, but in adults this can take months to years. Treatment works by slowly destroying the wart or helping your immune system fight it. Most treatments take several weeks or several visits, and warts can come back. Being patient and sticking with treatment makes the biggest difference.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H299
Section		Treating warts at home

Over-the-counter salicylic acid is a good first treatment for many warts. Use it as directed on the label, usually every day for up to 12 weeks:

  • Soak the wart in warm water for 5 to 10 minutes.
  • Gently file off the dead white skin with an emery board or pumice stone. Use that file only for the wart and don't share it.
  • Apply the salicylic acid to the wart only, not the skin around it.
  • Cover it with a bandage or tape if the label says to.

If you have diabetes, poor circulation or numbness in your feet, do not treat foot warts yourself. Ask us first.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H300
Section		Treatments in our office

If home treatment hasn't worked, options include freezing (often repeated every 2 to 4 weeks), blistering liquids, prescription creams, injections and lasers. Freezing can cause a blister and soreness for a few days.

Tell us if you are pregnant or breastfeeding. Some wart treatments are not used during pregnancy.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H301
Section		Preventing spread
  • Don't pick, bite or shave over warts.
  • Wash your hands after touching a wart.
  • Wear flip-flops in locker rooms, gym showers and pool areas.
  • Keep your feet dry, and change socks if they get sweaty.
  • Don't share towels, razors, nail files or shoes.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Molluscum contagiosum in adults cond-molluscum-adults

Patient sees: Molluscum contagiosum · Clinician summary: Adult molluscum: how it spreads (including sexual contact), what to expect, treatment, stopping spread, STI testing.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H308
Section		What it is

Molluscum contagiosum is a common skin infection caused by a virus. It causes small, firm, smooth bumps that are skin-colored, pink or pearly. Many bumps have a tiny dent in the center.

It spreads by skin-to-skin contact, by sharing towels or razors, and from one part of your body to another by scratching or shaving. In adults it is often spread through sexual contact, so the bumps are commonly on the lower belly, inner thighs and genital area.

If you have bumps in the genital area, we may suggest testing for other sexually transmitted infections. If you have many or large bumps, especially on the face, we may suggest checking your immune system.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H309
Section		What to expect

Molluscum is harmless and does not leave lasting effects in most people. Without treatment, bumps usually go away on their own. This often takes 6 to 12 months and sometimes longer, as new bumps can keep appearing.

A bump may become red and swollen before it goes away. This is often a sign that your body is clearing it, not an infection.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H310
Section		Treatment

In adults, we often treat molluscum so it does not spread to other areas or to partners. Options include:

  • Freezing (cryotherapy)
  • Scraping the bumps off (curettage)
  • A blistering liquid applied in the office
  • Prescription creams or gels, used as prescribed

More than one visit is often needed. Tell us if you are pregnant or breastfeeding, since some treatments are not used then.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H311
Section		Stopping the spread
  • Don't scratch, pick or squeeze the bumps.
  • Don't shave or wax over areas with bumps. Trimming with clippers is safer until they clear.
  • Cover bumps with clothing or a bandage when you can.
  • Don't share towels, washcloths, razors or clothing.
  • Avoid sexual contact while there are bumps in the genital area. Condoms do not cover all the skin, so they do not fully prevent spread.
  • Wash your hands after touching a bump.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H312
Stop and call if		Bumps on or near the eyelid, or a red, irritated eye.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H313
Stop and call if		Bumps spreading quickly, or many large bumps.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H314
Stop and call if		A bump that becomes very painful, hot or swollen, or drains pus.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H315
Stop and call if		Bumps in the genital area that have not been checked by us.☐ Approve

Sources: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, "Molluscum Contagiosum" (patient information) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Molluscum contagiosum: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education)

Vitiligo: overview cond-vitiligo

Patient sees: Living with vitiligo · Clinician summary: What vitiligo is, treatment options and realistic timelines, sun protection, cover-up options, emotional support.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H316
Section		What it is

Vitiligo causes patches of skin to lose their color and turn white. It happens when the immune system attacks the cells that make pigment. Hair in the patches may also turn white.

Vitiligo is not contagious and is not caused by anything you did or ate. It does not harm your general health. Some people with vitiligo also have thyroid disease or another immune condition, so we may suggest blood tests.

The most common type shows up on both sides of the body, often on the face, hands, feet, and around the eyes and mouth. Another type stays on one side or one area.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H317
Section		Treatment

Treatment aims to stop new patches and bring color back. Options include:

  • Prescription creams or ointments, such as steroid creams, calcineurin inhibitor ointments, or a JAK-inhibitor cream, used as prescribed
  • Light therapy (narrowband UVB), usually 2 to 3 times a week in the office
  • Pills to calm the immune system when vitiligo is spreading quickly
  • Skin grafting procedures for stable patches that haven't responded

Color comes back slowly, often as small dots that join together. It can take 3 to 6 months to see change and a year or more for the full effect. The face usually responds best, the hands and feet the least.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H318
Section		Caring for your skin
  • White patches burn easily. Use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher every day and wear protective clothing.
  • Sunburn, cuts, scrapes and constant rubbing can trigger new patches. Protect your skin and choose clothes that don't rub.
  • Avoid tanning beds.
  • Self-tanners (with dihydroxyacetone) and cover-up makeup can even out skin tone. Try a small area first.
  • Tanning the skin around the patches makes them stand out more, so sun protection also helps them look less noticeable.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H319
Section		Your feelings matter too

Vitiligo can affect how you feel about yourself. Feeling self-conscious, sad or anxious is common. Tell us how you are doing. Support groups, in person or online, help many people, and talking with a counselor can help too.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H320
Stop and call if		New patches appearing quickly over a few weeks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H321
Stop and call if		Sunburn or blisters on the white patches.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H322
Stop and call if		Thinning skin, stretch marks or a new rash where you use a prescription cream.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H323
Stop and call if		Signs of a thyroid problem: unusual tiredness, weight change, feeling too hot or cold, or a racing heart.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H324
Stop and call if		Feeling down or anxious about your skin most days.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Vitiligo: Overview" and "Vitiligo: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · National Institute of Arthritis and Musculoskeletal and Skin Diseases (NIAMS), "Vitiligo" (health topic)

Melasma: what it is (education) cond-melasma-education

Patient sees: Understanding melasma · Clinician summary: Education companion to the melasma regimen: causes and triggers, realistic expectations, treatment overview, daily habits.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H325
Section		What it is

Melasma causes brown or gray-brown patches, usually on the cheeks, forehead, upper lip, bridge of the nose and chin. It is more common in women and in people with medium to dark skin.

Several things work together to cause it:

  • Sunlight, and also visible light and heat
  • Hormones: pregnancy (sometimes called "the mask of pregnancy"), birth control pills and hormone therapy
  • Genes: it often runs in families

Melasma is not harmful and is not contagious. It is a long-term condition that can be controlled, but it often comes back.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H326
Section		What to expect

Melasma improves slowly. Most treatments take 2 to 3 months to show a difference, and it can take longer. Even after it fades, sun exposure and heat can bring it back quickly, so most people need some ongoing care.

Melasma that starts in pregnancy may fade on its own in the months after the baby is born, but not always.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H327
Section		Treatment options
  • Daily sun protection is the base of every plan. Without it, other treatments don't work well.
  • Prescription lightening creams, such as hydroquinone or a combination cream, used on the patches for a limited time as prescribed.
  • Other creams, such as azelaic acid or a retinoid.
  • Tranexamic acid, as a cream or as prescription pills. Pills are not right for everyone, especially people with a history of blood clots.
  • Chemical peels or certain lasers in some cases. These must be done carefully, because heat and irritation can make melasma darker.

If you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy or breastfeeding, tell us. Many melasma treatments, including retinoids, are avoided then.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H328
Section		Daily habits that help
  • Use a tinted mineral sunscreen with iron oxides, SPF 30 or higher, every morning. Tint helps block visible light.
  • Reapply every 2 hours outdoors, and wear a wide-brimmed hat.
  • Avoid heat when you can: saunas, hot yoga, and long time near ovens or stoves.
  • Use gentle, fragrance-free skin care. Irritation can darken patches.
  • Avoid waxing the areas with melasma.
  • Don't use skin lighteners bought online or from unknown sources. Some contain mercury or hidden steroids.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H329
Stop and call if		Patches that turn darker, gray or blue-black while using a lightening cream (stop the cream and call us).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H330
Stop and call if		Redness, burning, peeling or a rash from a treatment cream.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H331
Stop and call if		If you take tranexamic acid pills: leg swelling or pain, chest pain, or shortness of breath (get emergency care).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H332
Stop and call if		A single dark spot that changes in size, shape or color, or looks different from your melasma.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H333
Stop and call if		You become pregnant or start breastfeeding.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Melasma: Overview" and "Melasma: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · U.S. Food and Drug Administration, consumer warning on skin-lightening products that contain mercury (consumer update)

Post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation (dark marks) cond-post-inflammatory-hyperpigmentation

Patient sees: Dark marks after acne, rashes or injury · Clinician summary: What PIH is, how long it lasts, treating the cause, helpful ingredients, what not to do.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H334
Section		What it is

Post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation (PIH) is a flat dark mark left behind after the skin has been inflamed or hurt. Common causes are acne, eczema, bug bites, burns, cuts and some skin treatments.

The marks can be tan, brown, dark brown or gray. They are more common and last longer in people with medium to dark skin.

PIH is not a scar. The skin's texture is normal. The darker color comes from extra pigment that was made while the skin was healing.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H335
Section		What to expect

PIH usually fades on its own over time. Marks near the surface often fade over several months. Deeper marks, which tend to look gray or blue-gray, can take a year or longer.

Treatment can speed things up, but it still takes time. Most people need 2 to 3 months to notice a difference.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H336
Section		What helps
  • Treat the cause first. If acne or eczema keeps flaring, new marks will keep forming.
  • Use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher every day. Sun makes dark marks darker and slows fading. A tinted sunscreen gives extra protection.
  • Ingredients that can help fade marks include azelaic acid, niacinamide, vitamin C and retinoids such as adapalene. Use over-the-counter products as directed on the label.
  • For stubborn marks, we may prescribe a lightening cream or a stronger retinoid, as prescribed, or talk about peels.

If you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy or breastfeeding, tell us. Retinoids and some lightening creams are avoided then.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H337
Section		What to avoid
  • Picking, squeezing or scratching spots. This causes more marks.
  • Scrubs, rough washcloths and harsh products. Irritation can make PIH worse.
  • Home remedies like lemon juice or baking soda, which can burn the skin.
  • Skin lighteners bought online or from unknown sources. Some contain mercury or hidden steroids.
  • Starting several new products at once. Add one at a time so you can tell what irritates.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H338
Stop and call if		Marks get darker or spread even with treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H339
Stop and call if		Redness, burning or peeling from a product that does not settle after you stop it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H340
Stop and call if		A dark patch that appears without any rash or injury before it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H341
Stop and call if		A dark spot that changes in size, shape or color, or is raised.☐ Approve

Alopecia areata cond-alopecia-areata

Patient sees: Alopecia areata: patchy hair loss · Clinician summary: What alopecia areata is, the unpredictable course, treatment options from injections to JAK pills, coping and cover-up options.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H342
Section		What it is

Alopecia areata is a condition where the immune system attacks hair follicles (the roots that make hair). It usually causes round, smooth bald patches on the scalp. It can also affect the beard, eyebrows, eyelashes or body hair. Some people also get tiny dents or ridges in their nails.

The hair follicles are not destroyed, so hair can grow back. Alopecia areata is not contagious and is not caused by anything you did. It is more common in people with other immune conditions, such as thyroid disease, or with eczema or allergies.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H343
Section		What to expect

Alopecia areata is unpredictable. Many people with only a few small patches regrow their hair within a year, sometimes without treatment. It can come back later, though. A smaller number of people lose more hair, and in some cases all scalp or body hair.

New hair may grow in white or fine at first. It usually gets its normal color and texture back over time.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H344
Section		Treatment options

Treatment can help hair regrow but does not cure the condition. What we suggest depends on how much hair is affected.

  • Steroid injections into the patches, usually every 4 to 6 weeks. This is common for a few patches.
  • Steroid creams, solutions or foams, used as prescribed
  • Minoxidil, to support regrowth
  • Medicine applied to the scalp to cause a mild allergic rash that can trigger regrowth
  • For severe or widespread hair loss: JAK-inhibitor pills. These need blood tests and regular check-ins.

Most treatments take at least 3 months to show results. Tell us if you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy or breastfeeding, since some treatments are not used then.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H345
Section		Coping and day-to-day care
  • Bald areas of the scalp can sunburn. Use sunscreen or wear a hat.
  • Wigs, hairpieces, scarves, hats, and eyebrow pencils or powders can help you feel more like yourself.
  • If you lose eyelashes, glasses can help protect your eyes from dust.
  • Hair loss can be very upsetting. Support groups, in person or online, help many people. Tell us how you're coping.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H346
Stop and call if		Hair loss that spreads quickly or affects large areas.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H347
Stop and call if		Loss of eyebrows or eyelashes.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H348
Stop and call if		Redness, scaling, pain or scarring in a bald patch.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H349
Stop and call if		A dent in the skin at an injection site that does not fill back in after a few months.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H350
Stop and call if		Feeling down, anxious or withdrawn because of your hair loss.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Alopecia areata: Overview" and "Alopecia areata: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · National Alopecia Areata Foundation, "What is alopecia areata?" (patient information) · National Institute of Arthritis and Musculoskeletal and Skin Diseases (NIAMS), "Alopecia Areata" (health topic)

Female pattern hair loss cond-female-pattern-hair-loss

Patient sees: Female pattern hair loss · Clinician summary: What FPHL is, checking for other causes, minoxidil (and early shedding), prescription options, pregnancy cautions, hair care.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H351
Section		What it is

Female pattern hair loss is the most common cause of thinning hair in women. Hair slowly gets thinner on the top of the head, and the part gets wider. The hairline at the front usually stays in place.

It happens because hair follicles slowly shrink and make finer, shorter hairs. Genes and hormones both play a role. It can start any time after puberty and often becomes more noticeable around menopause.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H352
Section		Checking for other causes

Other things can cause or add to thinning, such as low iron, thyroid problems, recent illness or stress, and some medicines. We may order blood tests, such as iron and thyroid levels.

If you have irregular periods, adult acne or extra hair growth on the face or body, tell us. We may check your hormone levels.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H353
Section		Treatment

Treatment works best to stop more thinning and can regrow some hair. Starting early helps.

  • Minoxidil applied to the scalp is available without a prescription. Use it as directed on the label.
  • Many people shed more hair in the first 1 to 2 months of minoxidil. This usually means new hairs are pushing out old ones, so keep going.
  • It takes about 6 to 12 months to see results. If you stop, the hair you gained is usually lost within a few months.
  • Prescription options, used as prescribed, include minoxidil pills and spironolactone. Other options include platelet-rich plasma (PRP) injections and laser light devices.

If you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy or breastfeeding, tell us. Minoxidil and spironolactone are not used then.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H354
Section		Hair care tips
  • Wash and style gently. Normal washing does not cause more hair loss.
  • Avoid tight ponytails, braids and extensions that pull on the hair.
  • Limit harsh chemical treatments and high heat.
  • A shorter cut, a new part, volumizing products, or colored scalp powders can make thinning less noticeable.
  • Biotin and hair supplements don't help unless you are low in a nutrient. Biotin can also throw off some blood tests, so tell your doctors if you take it.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H355
Step · As directed		Minoxidil (scalp) (OTC; picker search “minoxidil 5% foam”)
As directed on the label, to a dry scalp over the thinning areas. Wash your hands afterward. Use it every day; results take 6 to 12 months. Not during pregnancy or breastfeeding.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H356

☐ Edit
☐ Cut

☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H358

Notes:

Male pattern hair loss cond-male-pattern-hair-loss

Patient sees: Male pattern hair loss · Clinician summary: What male pattern balding is, minoxidil and finasteride (including side effects and handling cautions), other options.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H361
Section		What it is

Male pattern hair loss is the most common cause of hair loss in men. It usually starts with a receding hairline at the temples, thinning at the crown, or both. It can begin as early as the late teens or twenties.

It is caused by genes and a hormone called DHT, which slowly shrinks hair follicles. Over time they make shorter, finer hairs until some stop growing hair at all.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H362
Section		Treatment

Treatment works best to keep the hair you have and can regrow some. The earlier you start, the more you can keep.

  • Minoxidil on the scalp, without a prescription. Use it as directed on the label. Extra shedding in the first 1 to 2 months is common and usually means new hairs are coming in.
  • Finasteride, a prescription pill taken as prescribed. It lowers DHT and slows hair loss for most men.
  • Other options include minoxidil pills, platelet-rich plasma (PRP) injections, laser light devices and hair transplant surgery.

Results take 6 to 12 months. Treatments only work while you use them. If you stop, the hair you gained or kept is usually lost within months.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H363
Section		About finasteride

Most men have no side effects. A small number notice a lower sex drive, trouble with erections or less semen. Mood changes, including depression, have also been reported. Tell us about any of these.

  • Finasteride lowers PSA, a blood test for prostate health. Tell any doctor ordering a PSA test that you take it.
  • People who are or may become pregnant should not handle crushed or broken tablets.
  • Don't donate blood while taking it, and ask us how long to wait after stopping.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H364
Section		Helpful tips
  • Take photos of your scalp in the same light every 3 to 6 months. Changes are slow and hard to see day to day.
  • Shampoos, supplements and vitamins sold for hair loss are mostly unproven.
  • Biotin can throw off some blood tests. Tell your doctors if you take it.
  • A shorter haircut can make thinning less noticeable.
  • Protect a thinning scalp from the sun with a hat or sunscreen.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H365
Step · As directed		Minoxidil (scalp) (OTC; picker search “minoxidil 5% foam”)
As directed on the label, to a dry scalp over the thinning areas. Wash your hands afterward. Use it every day; results take 6 to 12 months.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Notes:

Telogen effluvium (shedding after illness, stress or childbirth) cond-telogen-effluvium

Patient sees: Hair shedding after illness, stress or childbirth · Clinician summary: Why sudden shedding happens months after a trigger, what's normal, the usual timeline to recovery, what helps.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H371
Section		What it is

Telogen effluvium is a type of hair shedding that happens all over the scalp. A shock to the body pushes many hairs into their resting phase at the same time. A few months later, those hairs fall out together.

Common triggers:

  • High fever or a serious illness, including COVID-19
  • Surgery
  • Having a baby
  • Major emotional stress
  • Fast weight loss or a very strict diet
  • Starting or stopping some medicines, including birth control pills
  • Low iron or a thyroid problem
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H372
Section		What to expect

Shedding usually starts about 2 to 3 months after the trigger, which is why the cause is often not obvious. You may see lots of hair in the shower, on your pillow or in your brush. Many fallen hairs have a small white bulb at the root. That is normal for this kind of shedding.

The good news is that the hair roots are not damaged. Once the trigger has passed, shedding usually slows within about 6 months. You may notice short new hairs at your hairline. Getting back your full thickness can take 6 to 12 months or more, because hair grows slowly.

After childbirth, shedding often peaks a few months after the baby is born and settles by the baby's first birthday.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H373
Section		Finding the cause

We may order blood tests, such as iron and thyroid levels, and review your medicines and recent health. Treating a cause like low iron helps the hair recover. Don't stop a prescribed medicine on your own. Talk to us or the doctor who prescribed it first.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H374
Section		What helps
  • Eat regular, balanced meals with enough protein.
  • Keep washing and brushing your hair normally. Washing less won't save hairs that are already going to fall out.
  • Be gentle: avoid tight hairstyles, harsh chemical treatments and high heat.
  • Take supplements only if a test shows you are low in something.
  • Be patient. Taking a photo every month or two can help you see the recovery.
  • In some cases we may suggest minoxidil to help regrowth.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H375
Stop and call if		Heavy shedding that continues for more than 6 months.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H376
Stop and call if		Bald patches, or thinning that is mainly on the top of the head or at the part.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H377
Stop and call if		Scalp redness, scaling, pain or burning.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H378
Stop and call if		Tiredness, weight change, feeling cold, or very heavy periods along with the shedding.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Hair shedding" (patient education) · DermNet, "Telogen effluvium" (patient information)

Ingrown toenails cond-ingrown-toenails

Patient sees: Ingrown toenails · Clinician summary: Causes, home care for mild cases, office treatment, prevention, and cautions for diabetes or poor circulation.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H379
Section		What it is

An ingrown toenail happens when the edge of a nail grows into the skin beside it. It most often affects the big toe. The skin gets red, swollen and sore, and it can become infected.

Common causes:

  • Cutting nails too short or rounding the corners
  • Tight or pointed shoes
  • Stubbing or injuring the toe
  • Naturally curved nails, which can run in families
  • Sweaty feet
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H380
Section		Home care for a mild ingrown nail

If the toe is only a little red and sore, home care often helps:

  • Soak your foot in warm water for 10 to 15 minutes, 2 to 3 times a day.
  • After soaking, dry the toe well. Gently place a small piece of cotton or waxed dental floss under the edge of the nail to help it grow above the skin. Change it daily.
  • Apply petroleum jelly and a bandage.
  • Wear roomy shoes or open-toed sandals.
  • Take an over-the-counter pain reliever as directed on the label if needed.

Don't dig out or cut the nail edge yourself. Cutting a V in the nail does not help.

If you have diabetes, poor circulation or numbness in your feet, skip home care and see us or a foot doctor.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H381
Section		Treatment in our office

If home care isn't working or the toe is infected, we can numb the toe and remove the ingrown edge of the nail. For nails that keep growing in, we may also treat the nail root so that edge does not grow back.

Afterward, follow the wound care instructions we give you. The toe is usually sore for a few days and heals over a few weeks.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H382
Section		Preventing it
  • Cut toenails straight across, and leave the corners slightly visible above the skin.
  • Don't cut them too short.
  • Wear shoes with enough room for your toes, and protective shoes for work or sports.
  • Keep your feet clean and dry.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H383
Stop and call if		Pus, increasing redness, swelling or warmth, or red streaks spreading up the foot.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H384
Stop and call if		Fever or chills.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H385
Stop and call if		No improvement after 3 to 5 days of home care.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H386
Stop and call if		Any ingrown nail if you have diabetes, poor circulation or numb feet.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H387
Stop and call if		After a nail procedure: bleeding that doesn't stop with 15 minutes of pressure, or pain that keeps getting worse.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "How to treat an ingrown toenail" (patient education) · American Academy of Family Physicians (familydoctor.org), "Ingrown Toenails" (patient education)

Brittle and splitting nails cond-brittle-nails

Patient sees: Brittle and splitting nails · Clinician summary: Common causes, nail-care do's and don'ts, why improvement takes months, when to look for another cause.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H388
Section		What it is

Brittle nails split, peel in layers, or break easily. This is very common, especially in women and as people get older.

The most common causes are things that dry out the nail:

  • Hands in water often, such as washing dishes or frequent handwashing
  • Cleaning products and other harsh chemicals
  • Nail polish remover, especially acetone
  • Gel or acrylic nails and their removal
  • Cold, dry weather

Less often, brittle nails are linked to low iron, thyroid problems, or a skin condition such as psoriasis.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H389
Section		What to expect

Nails grow slowly. A fingernail takes about 4 to 6 months to grow out completely, and a toenail can take a year or more. The damaged part won't repair itself, so you will see improvement as healthier nail grows in from the base. Give your new routine at least a few months.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H390
Section		Caring for your nails

Do:

  • Wear cotton-lined rubber gloves for dishes, cleaning and other wet work.
  • Rub a thick moisturizer into your nails and cuticles after washing your hands and at bedtime.
  • Keep nails short and file them in one direction with a fine file.
  • Use an acetone-free polish remover, and remove polish no more than about once a week.

Don't:

  • Use your nails as tools to open, scrape or pry.
  • Pick, peel or bite your nails or cuticles.
  • Cut or push back your cuticles. They protect the nail.
  • Peel off gel polish. Give your nails breaks from gel and acrylic nails.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H391
Section		About supplements

Biotin is often sold for nails. A few small studies suggest it may help some people, but the evidence is limited. Biotin can also throw off some blood tests, including thyroid and heart tests. If you take it, tell your doctors before blood work. If we find you are low in iron, treating that may help your nails.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H392
Step · Morning and night		Hand and nail moisturizer (OTC; picker search “hand cream”)
After every handwashing and at bedtime, rub into the nails and cuticles. Thick, fragrance-free creams or ointments work best.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H393
Stop and call if		A nail that changes color, thickens, crumbles, or lifts off the nail bed.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H394
Stop and call if		A new dark streak in a nail.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H395
Stop and call if		Pain, redness or swelling around a nail.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H396
Stop and call if		Brittle nails along with tiredness, hair loss, feeling cold, or other new symptoms.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H397
Stop and call if		No improvement after about 6 months of gentle nail care.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Nail care: 12 tips for healthy nails" and "Tips for healthy nails" (patient education) · DermNet, "Brittle nails" (patient information)

Nail psoriasis and nail changes worth a visit cond-nail-psoriasis-nail-changes

Patient sees: Nail psoriasis and other nail changes to have checked · Clinician summary: What nail psoriasis looks like, treatment and nail care, the joint link, plus a list of nail changes that need a visit.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H398
Section		Nail psoriasis

Psoriasis can affect the fingernails and toenails, sometimes even when there is little or no psoriasis on the skin. Signs include:

  • Small dents or pits in the nail
  • The nail lifting off the skin underneath, often with white or yellow color at the tip
  • Orange-brown "oil drop" spots under the nail
  • Thick, crumbly or ridged nails

Nail psoriasis can look like a nail fungus. We may test a nail clipping to tell them apart.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H399
Section		Nail psoriasis and your joints

People with nail psoriasis have a higher chance of psoriatic arthritis. Tell us if you have joint pain, swelling or stiffness, morning stiffness that lasts more than 30 minutes, a swollen finger or toe, or heel pain. Treating arthritis early helps protect your joints.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H400
Section		Treatment and nail care

Treatment options include prescription creams or solutions, injections into the skin around the nail, and medicines for psoriasis that work through the whole body. All are used as prescribed. Nails grow slowly, so it usually takes several months to see a change.

At home:

  • Keep nails short so they don't catch and lift further.
  • Wear gloves for wet work and protect your hands from injury. Injury can trigger psoriasis.
  • Don't dig or clean under a lifted nail. This makes it lift more.
  • Don't push back or cut your cuticles.
  • Nail polish can hide changes, but remove it with an acetone-free remover.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H401
Section		Other nail changes to have checked

Ask us to look at:

  • A new dark brown or black streak in a nail, especially in just one nail, or one that widens or spreads onto the skin around it
  • A bump, growth, bleeding or sore under or around a nail that doesn't heal
  • A nail that lifts or splits because of something growing under it
  • Thick, yellow, crumbly nails (fungus, psoriasis or other causes)
  • Red, swollen, painful skin around a nail
  • Fingertips that become rounded with nails that curve over them

Many nail changes are harmless, such as small white spots from minor bumps. Lines across all nails can appear a few months after an illness and grow out on their own.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H402
Stop and call if		A new or changing dark streak in a nail, or dark color on the skin next to a nail.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H403
Stop and call if		A bump, bleeding or a sore at a nail that does not heal in a few weeks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H404
Stop and call if		Painful, swollen or stiff joints, or a swollen finger or toe.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H405
Stop and call if		Red, swollen, painful skin around a nail, or pus.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H406
Stop and call if		A change in only one nail that keeps getting worse.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Nail psoriasis: Overview" and "Nail psoriasis: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Nail changes a dermatologist should examine" (patient education) · National Psoriasis Foundation, "Nail Psoriasis" (patient information)

Surgery & procedures (19)

Start here: what the drafter was least sure of
  1. Bleeding: 20 minutes of firm pressure, repeat once, then call or go to urgent care/ER. The older post-procedure template says 15 minutes; pick one.
  2. Pain relief: acetaminophen usually first; ask before aspirin or ibuprofen.
  3. Before surgery: fish oil, vitamin E, ginkgo and garlic pills listed as 'ask us whether to pause' (no fixed number of days). Alcohol: avoid for a day or two before and after.
  4. Driving: a driver advised for surgery near the eye, on a hand or foot, larger repairs, or any calming medicine.
  5. Default timings, all hedged: stitches out in 1 to 2 weeks; keep the first bandage dry 24 to 48 hours; a graft's bolster dressing comes off in about a week; scars mature over 12 to 18 months; silicone for at least 2 to 3 months.
  6. PDT: 48 hours of light avoidance (per the ALA/MAL labels); 'sunscreen alone does not protect you'. No daylight-PDT wording.
  7. Patch testing: no sun on the back 1 to 2 weeks before; no topical steroids on the back about a week before; ask about (never stop alone) steroid pills or shots, methotrexate, cyclosporine, azathioprine, mycophenolate, biologics and JAK inhibitors; antihistamines can usually continue.
  8. Nail surgery: drainage 2 to 6 weeks after a chemical matrixectomy; regrowth 12 to 18 months for a toenail, about 6 months for a fingernail.
  9. Cantharidin: wash off at 'the time we told you, often a few hours' (no fixed hours); ring warts mentioned; tell us if pregnant before treatment.
  10. Steroid injections: repeats 'often every 4 to 6 weeks'; blood sugar may run higher for a day or two with diabetes.
  11. Phototherapy: most people improve after about 15 to 25 treatments; long-term skin cancer risk stated with a hedge.
  12. Infection: fever threshold 100.4 F (38 C); a tape-shaped itchy rash is likely an allergy (supports plain petrolatum over antibiotic ointment).
  13. Sources to confirm: the exact titles of 'MedlinePlus, Bleeding (first aid)', 'MedlinePlus, Skin abscess', 'AAD, How to treat an ingrown toenail'. Journal citations are author/journal/year only: Smack 1996 JAMA; Elmets 2019 JAAD (phototherapy); Heidelbaugh and Lee 2009 Am Fam Physician.

Preparing for skin surgery proc-preparing-for-skin-surgery

Patient sees: Getting ready for your skin surgery · Clinician summary: Medicines and blood thinners, smoking, alcohol, what to tell us, what to bring, and driving home.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H407
Section		Your medicines

Keep taking your usual medicines on the day of surgery unless we told you otherwise.

  • Blood thinners: never stop a blood thinner (such as warfarin, apixaban, rivaroxaban, clopidogrel, or aspirin your doctor prescribed for your heart) on your own. Stopping can raise the risk of a stroke or heart attack. If you have questions, we will talk with the doctor who prescribed it.
  • Supplements: some, like fish oil, vitamin E, ginkgo and garlic pills, can add to bleeding. Ask us whether to pause any you take on your own.
  • For pain before surgery, acetaminophen is usually the safer choice. Ask us before taking ibuprofen or naproxen.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H408
Section		Tell us before surgery if you
  • Have a pacemaker or implanted defibrillator.
  • Have an artificial joint, heart valve, or have had a heart infection.
  • Are allergic to numbing medicine, latex, tape, or antibiotics.
  • Are pregnant or might be.
  • Get cold sores and the surgery is near your mouth.
  • Have had a keloid (a thick, raised scar).
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H409
Section		Smoking and alcohol

Smoking and vaping nicotine cut down blood flow to the skin. This slows healing and raises the chance of infection and a poor scar. If you can, stop or cut back for as long as possible before and after surgery. Even a few days helps.

Alcohol can increase bleeding. Most people are asked to avoid it for a day or two before and after surgery.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H410
Section		On the day
  • Eat a normal meal before you come, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Shower and wash your hair. You may need to keep the area dry for a day or two afterward.
  • Leave makeup, lotion and jewelry off the surgery area.
  • Wear loose, comfortable clothes. A shirt that buttons in front is easier for surgery on the head, neck or upper body.
  • Bring a list of your medicines and allergies, and your glasses or hearing aids if you use them.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H411
Section		Getting home

Most skin surgery uses only local numbing medicine, so you are awake and many people can drive. Plan for a driver if the surgery is near your eye (a bandage may block your vision), on your hand or foot, if it is a larger surgery, or if you are given any medicine to relax you.

Plan to take it easy for the rest of the day. For larger surgeries, avoid heavy lifting and hard exercise for 1 to 2 weeks, so it helps to plan around work, travel and sports.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H412
Stop and call if		You were told to stop a blood thinner and are not sure if that is right: call before you stop it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H413
Stop and call if		You get sick, have a fever, or have a new rash or sore near the surgery area in the days before surgery.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H414
Stop and call if		You start a new medicine or supplement before your surgery date.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American College of Mohs Surgery, patient information on preparing for skin surgery · American Academy of Dermatology, patient education on skin cancer surgery

Skin biopsy aftercare (shave and punch) proc-biopsy-aftercare

Patient sees: Caring for your skin biopsy site · Clinician summary: Daily washing, petrolatum and a bandage for shave and punch biopsies; healing time; stitches; getting results.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H415
Section		What was done

A biopsy removes a small piece of skin so it can be looked at under a microscope.

  • Shave biopsy: the top layers of skin were shaved off. It leaves a shallow, open spot like a scrape.
  • Punch biopsy: a small round tool removed a deeper core of skin. The spot is often closed with one or more stitches.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H416
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Leave the first bandage on and keep it dry for 24 hours, unless we told you otherwise.
  • After that, wash the spot gently once a day with mild soap and water. Rinse and pat dry.
  • Put on a thin layer of petrolatum ointment and a fresh bandage.
  • Repeat each day until the skin has healed over.
  • You may shower after the first day. Avoid soaking in a bath, pool or hot tub until it has healed or the stitches are out.

Don't let a hard, dry scab form. Wounds kept moist and covered usually heal faster and leave a better scar.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H417
Section		What to expect
  • Mild soreness, a pink rim and a little clear or yellowish fluid are normal in the first few days.
  • A shave biopsy usually heals in 1 to 3 weeks. Spots on the legs can take longer.
  • If you have stitches, they usually come out in 1 to 2 weeks, depending on where they are. Dissolving stitches do not need to be removed.
  • Most biopsies leave a small mark that is pink at first and fades over months.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H418
Section		Your results

Results usually take 1 to 2 weeks. Ask us how you will hear about them. If you have not heard from us within the time we gave you, please call. No news is not always good news.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Wound care after excision with stitches proc-excision-wound-care

Patient sees: Caring for your stitches after skin surgery · Clinician summary: Pressure bandage, daily cleaning, activity limits, swelling and pain control after an excision closed with stitches.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H426
Section		The first 1 to 2 days
  • Leave the pressure bandage on and keep it dry for 24 to 48 hours, unless we told you otherwise. It helps prevent bleeding.
  • Rest and keep the area raised if you can. For surgery on the face or head, sleep with your head raised on 2 pillows for the first couple of nights.
  • An ice pack wrapped in a cloth, held over the bandage for 10 to 20 minutes every hour or two while awake, can ease swelling and pain.
  • For pain, acetaminophen is usually the first choice, as directed on the label, unless we told you otherwise.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H427
Section		Daily care after that

Once a day until the stitches come out:

  • Remove the bandage and wash the wound gently with mild soap and water. You can let shower water run over it. Pat dry.
  • Clean off any crust gently. Don't scrub.
  • Put on a thin layer of petrolatum ointment.
  • Cover with a nonstick bandage.

If you have tape strips on the wound, leave them on and let them fall off on their own.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H428
Section		Protecting the stitches

Stretching or strain can open the wound or cause bleeding.

  • Avoid heavy lifting, hard exercise, bending over and contact sports for 1 to 2 weeks, or as long as we told you. This matters most for wounds on the back, shoulders and legs.
  • Don't soak the wound in a bath, pool, hot tub or the ocean until the stitches are out.
  • Don't pick at the stitches or crusts.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H429
Section		What is normal
  • Some swelling, bruising and tenderness for several days. Bruising on the face can spread down toward the eyes or neck.
  • Numbness or tingling around the scar, which can last weeks to months.
  • A firm ridge under the scar, which softens over a few months.
  • Stitches usually come out in about 1 to 2 weeks, depending on where they are. Some stitches dissolve on their own.

Mohs surgery: what to expect on the day proc-mohs-day-of-surgery

Patient sees: Your Mohs surgery day · Clinician summary: How Mohs works step by step, how long the day takes, what to eat, wear and bring, and getting home.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H437
Section		What Mohs surgery is

Mohs surgery is a way to remove skin cancer one thin layer at a time. Each layer is checked under a microscope while you wait. The surgeon only removes more skin where cancer is still seen. This saves as much healthy skin as possible and has a very high cure rate for the skin cancers it is used for.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H438
Section		How the day goes
  • The area is numbed with an injection. You stay awake.
  • The surgeon removes the visible cancer and a thin layer around it. This part usually takes only a few minutes.
  • A bandage goes on and you wait, usually about an hour or more, while the layer is prepared and checked.
  • If cancer is still seen, the surgeon removes another layer only in that spot. This repeats until no cancer is seen.
  • Then the wound is repaired. It may be closed with stitches, covered with nearby skin (a flap) or a skin graft, or left to heal on its own. Sometimes the repair is done on another day or by another surgeon.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H439
Section		Plan for the whole day

Most people are done in a few hours, but it is hard to know ahead of time how many layers are needed. Plan to be with us most of the day and don't schedule anything else.

  • Eat a normal breakfast and take your usual medicines, including blood thinners, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Wear comfortable clothes in layers. A shirt that buttons in front is easiest for surgery on the head or neck.
  • Bring a snack, a drink, something to read or do, a list of your medicines, and your glasses or hearing aids.
  • Leave jewelry and valuables at home. Don't wear makeup on the area.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H440
Section		Getting home

You will leave with a thick pressure bandage. Bring a driver if the surgery is near your eye, if a large repair is likely, or if you would feel more comfortable not driving. One adult companion is usually welcome to wait with you.

Plan to rest for the next day or two and to avoid hard exercise and heavy lifting for 1 to 2 weeks, or as long as we tell you.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H441
Stop and call if		You take a blood thinner and were told to stop it: call us before you stop it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H442
Stop and call if		You have a pacemaker, defibrillator, artificial joint or heart valve and have not told us.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H443
Stop and call if		You are sick, have a fever, or have a new sore or rash near the surgery area before your surgery date.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American College of Mohs Surgery, patient information on what to expect during Mohs surgery · Skin Cancer Foundation, "Mohs surgery" (patient education) · MedlinePlus, "Mohs microscopic surgery" (patient education)

Mohs surgery aftercare (incl. flaps and grafts) proc-mohs-aftercare

Patient sees: Healing after your Mohs surgery · Clinician summary: Pressure bandage, swelling and bruising, daily wound care, extra care for flaps and grafts, and what healing looks like.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H444
Section		The first 48 hours
  • Leave the pressure bandage on and dry for 24 to 48 hours, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Rest. Avoid bending over, lifting and straining.
  • For surgery on the face or head, sleep with your head raised on 2 or 3 pillows.
  • An ice pack wrapped in a cloth, held over the bandage for 10 to 20 minutes every hour or two while awake, eases swelling.
  • For pain, acetaminophen is usually the first choice, as directed on the label, unless we told you otherwise.

Swelling and bruising are often worst on days 2 and 3. After surgery on the forehead, nose or near the eyes, the eyelids can swell and bruise. This usually fades over 1 to 2 weeks.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H445
Section		Daily care after that

Once a day, until the stitches come out or the wound has healed:

  • Wash gently with mild soap and water. Pat dry.
  • Put on a thin layer of petrolatum ointment.
  • Cover with a nonstick bandage.

If your wound was left to heal on its own, keep up this daily care until new skin covers it. This can take several weeks.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H446
Section		If you have a flap or graft

A flap moves nearby skin to cover the wound. A graft is skin taken from another spot (the donor site) and placed over the wound.

  • If a padded dressing (a bolster) is stitched on top of a graft, leave it alone and keep it dry until we remove it, usually in about a week.
  • Don't press, rub or stretch the flap or graft. It needs a steady blood supply to take.
  • Care for the donor site as we showed you.
  • Smoking and nicotine raise the risk that a flap or graft won't heal well. Avoid them.
  • A graft may look dark, purple or patchy at first. It usually lightens over weeks to months.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H447
Section		Over the coming months
  • Numbness, tingling or itching around the scar can last for months.
  • Lumps, firmness and a raised or pink scar are common and usually soften and flatten over 6 to 12 months or longer.
  • Protect the scar from the sun with clothing, a hat or broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher once healed.
  • Having one skin cancer raises the chance of another. Keep up your regular skin checks.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H448
Step · As directed		Healing ointment (OTC; picker search “petrolatum”)
After washing, a thin layer of plain petrolatum ointment on the wound with a clean cotton swab. Keep the wound moist until it has fully healed.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H449
Step · As directed		Bandage (OTC; picker search “nonstick bandage”)
Cover with a nonstick pad and tape, or an adhesive bandage. Change it once a day, or sooner if it gets wet or dirty.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H450
Stop and call if		Bleeding that doesn't stop after 20 minutes of firm, steady pressure, or a fast-growing, tight, painful swelling under the wound.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H451
Stop and call if		Increasing pain, warmth, swelling or redness after the first 2 to 3 days, pus, or a fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H452
Stop and call if		Part of a flap or graft turns black, or the wound edges pull apart.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H453
Stop and call if		The bolster or stitches come loose early.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H454
Stop and call if		Swelling around the eye that keeps it from opening, or any change in your vision.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Stitch removal and caring for your scar proc-stitch-removal-scar-care

Patient sees: After your stitches come out: caring for your scar · Clinician summary: Typical stitch-removal timing, care in the weeks after, sun protection, silicone, massage, and how scars change over a year.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H455
Section		When stitches come out

Stitches usually come out in about 1 to 2 weeks. Areas that heal fast, like the face, often come out sooner. Areas under more stretch, like the back, arms and legs, often stay longer. We will tell you your timing. Dissolving stitches do not need to be removed.

Removing stitches takes a few minutes and is usually not painful. Please don't remove them yourself unless we told you to.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H456
Section		The first weeks after removal

A new scar is still weak for several weeks, even when it looks closed.

  • If tape strips were put on, leave them until they fall off, usually within a week.
  • Keep the scar moist with petrolatum ointment until any crusts are gone.
  • Keep avoiding heavy lifting and stretching of the area for a few more weeks if the scar is on the back, shoulders, chest or a joint.
  • You can usually swim and bathe normally once the wound is fully closed with no open spots or crusts.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H457
Section		Helping your scar look its best
  • Sun protection: UV light can darken a new scar. Cover it with clothing or use a broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher sunscreen every day for at least a year.
  • Silicone gel or sheets: once the skin is fully closed, these can help a scar flatten and soften. Use as directed on the label, usually for at least 2 to 3 months.
  • Massage: if we suggest it, gently massage the scar for a few minutes a day once it is healed.
  • Don't smoke. It slows healing.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H458
Section		What to expect

Scars change for a long time. A new scar is often pink, red or a little raised and firm. Over 12 to 18 months, most scars become flatter, softer and paler. Numbness around a scar can also take months to improve.

If you have had keloids (thick scars that grow past the wound) before, tell us. Early treatment can help.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H459
Step · As directed		Silicone scar gel (OTC; picker search “silicone scar gel”)
Once the skin is fully closed with no scabs: a thin layer on the scar as directed on the label, usually for at least 2 to 3 months.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H460
Step · Morning		Sunscreen (OTC; picker search “SPF 30”)
Every morning on the scar if it is not covered by clothing: broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher. Reapply every 2 hours outdoors.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H461
Stop and call if		The wound opens after the stitches come out.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H462
Stop and call if		Redness, warmth, swelling, pus or increasing pain around the scar.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H463
Stop and call if		A stitch or piece of stitch pokes out of the scar weeks later, or a small pimple-like bump forms on the scar line.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H464
Stop and call if		The scar becomes thick, raised, itchy or grows past the edges of the wound.☐ Approve

Bleeding after skin surgery (pressure technique) proc-bleeding-after-surgery

Patient sees: What to do if your wound bleeds · Clinician summary: Normal oozing vs. bleeding, the 20-minute firm-pressure technique, ice and rest, and when to call or seek urgent care.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H465
Section		What is normal

A little blood or pink fluid on the bandage in the first day or two is normal, especially if you take a blood thinner. Bleeding is most likely in the first 24 to 48 hours. It is often set off by bending over, lifting, straining or hard exercise.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H466
Section		If blood soaks through the bandage
  • Sit or lie down and stay calm. Raise the area above your heart if you can.
  • Don't take off the bandage. Place clean gauze or a clean folded cloth on top of it.
  • Press firmly and steadily, right over the wound, for 20 minutes by the clock.
  • Don't lift to peek. Each peek restarts the clock and can restart the bleeding.
  • If blood soaks through, add more gauze on top and keep pressing.
  • If it still bleeds after 20 minutes, press for another 20 minutes.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H467
Section		Once it stops
  • Leave the bandage alone, or add a fresh bandage over the old one.
  • An ice pack wrapped in a cloth for 10 to 20 minutes can help.
  • Rest for the rest of the day. Avoid bending, lifting and straining.
  • Keep taking your blood thinner unless the prescriber tells you otherwise. Don't stop it on your own.
  • Avoid alcohol for a couple of days, and ask us before taking aspirin or ibuprofen for pain.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H468
Stop and call if		Bleeding that keeps going after two rounds of 20 minutes of firm pressure: call us, or go to urgent care or the emergency room if we can't be reached.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H469
Stop and call if		Bleeding that is spurting or pouring.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H470
Stop and call if		A fast-growing, tight, painful swelling or lump under the wound (blood collecting under the skin).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H471
Stop and call if		You feel faint, dizzy or very weak (call 911).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American College of Mohs Surgery, patient information on wound care after Mohs surgery · MedlinePlus, "Bleeding" (first aid)
