Notes:

Signs of wound infection proc-wound-infection-signs

Patient sees: Is my wound infected? · Clinician summary: Normal healing vs. signs of infection, allergic tape/ointment rashes, and how to lower the chance of infection.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H472
Section		Normal healing

Most skin surgery wounds heal without infection. These are normal in the first days:

  • A thin pink or red rim right around the wound.
  • Mild swelling, bruising and tenderness that get better each day after day 2 or 3.
  • A small amount of clear, pink or light yellow fluid.
  • A soft yellow film in the base of an open wound. This is part of healing, not pus.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H473
Section		Signs of infection

Infections usually show up 3 to 7 days after surgery. Watch for:

  • Redness that spreads outward, or red streaks leading away from the wound.
  • Pain, swelling or warmth that gets worse after the first 2 to 3 days instead of better.
  • Thick, cloudy, yellow-green or bad-smelling drainage (pus).
  • Fever or chills, or feeling unwell.

If you notice these, call us. Don't start leftover antibiotics on your own.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H474
Section		Rash from tape or ointment

An itchy red rash in the exact shape of the tape or bandage, or wherever ointment is applied, is often an allergic reaction (contact dermatitis), not an infection. It is usually itchy more than painful. Antibiotic ointments are a common cause, which is why we suggest plain petrolatum. Let us know if this happens.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H475
Section		Lowering your risk
  • Wash your hands before touching the wound or changing the bandage.
  • Clean the wound gently once a day and keep it covered with petrolatum and a clean bandage.
  • Don't soak the wound in baths, pools or hot tubs until it has healed.
  • Don't pick at scabs or stitches.
  • Avoid smoking, which slows healing.
  • If you have diabetes, keep your blood sugar under good control.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H476
Stop and call if		Redness spreading outward from the wound, or red streaks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H477
Stop and call if		Pain, swelling or warmth getting worse after the first 2 to 3 days.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H478
Stop and call if		Pus or bad-smelling drainage.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H479
Stop and call if		Fever of 100.4 F (38 C) or higher, or chills.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H480
Stop and call if		An itchy rash where the tape or ointment touches the skin.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: MedlinePlus, "Surgical wound infection - treatment" (patient education) · MedlinePlus, "Surgical wound care - closed" (patient education) · Smack DP et al. Infection and allergy incidence in ambulatory surgery patients using white petrolatum vs bacitracin ointment. JAMA 1996

Cryotherapy (liquid nitrogen) aftercare proc-cryotherapy-aftercare

Patient sees: After liquid nitrogen freezing treatment · Clinician summary: Redness, swelling and blisters after freezing; caring for blisters and scabs; color changes; repeat treatments.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H481
Section		What was done

Liquid nitrogen is very cold. It freezes and destroys growths such as warts, precancerous spots (actinic keratoses) and some harmless growths. Treated skin heals over the next few weeks, and the growth usually comes off with the scab.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H482
Section		What to expect
  • Stinging or burning during and for a short time after treatment.
  • Redness and swelling within hours. Swelling near the eyes can be more noticeable the next morning, especially after treatment on the forehead or temples.
  • A blister may form within a day. It can be clear or filled with blood. This is normal.
  • The blister dries into a scab that usually falls off in 1 to 3 weeks. Spots on the legs can take longer.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H483
Section		Caring for it at home
  • You can wash the area normally with mild soap and water and pat dry.
  • Leave blisters alone. The blister roof protects the skin underneath.
  • If a blister breaks, wash it gently, apply petrolatum ointment and cover it with a bandage.
  • A bandage also helps if clothing rubs the spot.
  • Don't pick at scabs. Let them fall off on their own.
  • For soreness, acetaminophen as directed on the label can help.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H484
Section		After it heals
  • The treated skin may be lighter or darker than the skin around it. This is more common in darker skin and can be long-lasting.
  • Some growths, especially warts, need more than one treatment, often a few weeks apart.
  • Protect treated skin from the sun while it heals and afterward.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H485
Stop and call if		A large, tense or very painful blister.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H486
Stop and call if		Redness spreading beyond the treated spot, warmth, pus, or fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H487
Stop and call if		Swelling around the eye that keeps it from opening.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H488
Stop and call if		A spot that has not healed in 6 weeks, or a growth that comes back.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Warts: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Actinic keratosis: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education)

Electrodesiccation and curettage (ED&C) aftercare proc-edc-aftercare

Patient sees: Caring for your skin after scraping and cautery (ED&C) · Clinician summary: What ED&C is, daily moist wound care for an open wound, how long healing takes, and the scar to expect.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H489
Section		What was done

Electrodesiccation and curettage (ED&C) treats some skin cancers that are on the surface of the skin. After numbing the area, the growth was scraped away with a small, sharp, spoon-shaped tool (a curette). Then the base was treated with an electric needle to stop bleeding and destroy any remaining cancer cells. This was usually repeated two or three times.

The wound is left open to heal on its own, like a deep scrape. There are no stitches.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H490
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Leave the first bandage on and keep it dry for 24 hours, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Then, once a day, wash the wound gently with mild soap and water. You can let shower water run over it. Pat dry.
  • Put on a thin layer of petrolatum ointment and a fresh nonstick bandage.
  • Keep doing this every day until new skin has covered the wound.

Keeping the wound moist and covered helps it heal faster and leaves a better scar than letting a hard scab form.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H491
Section		What to expect
  • Mild soreness for a few days. Acetaminophen, as directed on the label, can help.
  • Clear or yellowish fluid and a yellow film in the base of the wound are normal parts of healing.
  • Healing usually takes 2 to 4 weeks. Larger wounds and wounds on the legs can take 6 weeks or more.
  • Once healed, the scar is usually round, flat and lighter than the skin around it. It may be pink at first and fades over months.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H492
Section		Looking ahead

Protect the area from the sun once healed. Having had one skin cancer raises the chance of another, so keep your regular skin checks. Let us know if you notice a sore, bump or scaly spot coming back in the scar.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H493
Step · As directed		Healing ointment (OTC; picker search “petrolatum”)
After washing, a thin layer of plain petrolatum ointment on the wound with a clean cotton swab. Keep the wound moist until it has fully healed.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H494
Step · As directed		Bandage (OTC; picker search “nonstick bandage”)
Cover with a nonstick pad and tape, or an adhesive bandage. Change it once a day, or sooner if it gets wet or dirty.		☐ Approve

☐ Cut
H495

H496

Incision and drainage of a boil or cyst proc-incision-drainage

Patient sees: Caring for your skin after a boil or cyst was drained · Clinician summary: Packing, drainage, warm compresses, antibiotics as prescribed, preventing spread, and why a cyst may need removal later.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H499
Section		What was done

A boil (abscess) is a pocket of pus under the skin, usually from a bacterial infection. An inflamed cyst can also fill with pus or thick material. We numbed the area, made a small cut and let the pus drain out. This takes the pressure off and usually eases pain quickly.

The wound may have been left open, and sometimes a strip of gauze (packing) is placed inside to keep it draining.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H500
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Keep the bandage on and change it when it gets soaked, at least once a day. Wash your hands before and after.
  • Some drainage of blood or pus for a few days is normal.
  • If you have packing, leave it in until we remove it or tell you how to take it out.
  • After the first day, you can usually shower and let water run over the wound. Don't soak in a bath, pool or hot tub until it has closed.
  • A warm, damp cloth held on the area for 10 to 15 minutes a few times a day can help it drain, unless we told you otherwise.
  • If you were given antibiotics, take them as prescribed and finish them.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H501
Section		Preventing spread

The bacteria from a boil can spread to other people and other parts of your body.

  • Throw used bandages away in a sealed bag.
  • Don't share towels, razors, washcloths or clothes.
  • Wash towels and sheets in hot water.
  • Keep the wound covered at work, school and the gym.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H502
Section		What to expect

The wound usually heals from the bottom up over 1 to 3 weeks. For a cyst, draining does not remove the cyst wall, so it can fill up again. Once it has calmed down, we may talk about removing the whole cyst.

If you keep getting boils, tell us. There are steps that can lower the chance they come back.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H503
Stop and call if		Redness spreading outward or red streaks, or the area becoming more swollen and painful after 2 days.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H504
Stop and call if		Fever or chills.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H505
Stop and call if		The wound fills up again or new boils appear.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H506
Stop and call if		Bleeding that doesn't stop after 20 minutes of firm, steady pressure.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H507
Stop and call if		The packing falls out early, if we told you it needs to stay in.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: MedlinePlus, "Skin abscess" (patient education) · MedlinePlus, "Surgical wound care - open" (patient education) · Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, patient information on preventing the spread of staph and MRSA skin infections

Cyst removal aftercare proc-cyst-removal-aftercare

Patient sees: Caring for your skin after cyst removal · Clinician summary: Wound care and activity limits after a cyst is cut out, normal lumps and drainage, and the chance it comes back.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H508
Section		What was done

A cyst is a sac under the skin filled with thick, cheesy or oily material. To keep it from coming back, we removed the sac along with its contents. The wound was usually closed with stitches. Some stitches are deeper and dissolve on their own.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H509
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Leave the bandage on and keep it dry for 24 to 48 hours, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Then, once a day, wash gently with mild soap and water and pat dry.
  • Put on a thin layer of petrolatum ointment and a fresh nonstick bandage.
  • Don't soak the wound in a bath, pool or hot tub until the stitches are out.
  • An ice pack wrapped in a cloth for 10 to 20 minutes at a time can ease swelling.
  • For pain, acetaminophen is usually the first choice, as directed on the label, unless we told you otherwise.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H510
Section		Protecting the wound

Cysts are often on the back, shoulders, neck and scalp, where the skin stretches a lot.

  • Avoid heavy lifting, hard exercise and reaching or twisting movements for 1 to 2 weeks, or as long as we told you.
  • Too much stretch can open the wound or make the scar wider.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H511
Section		What to expect
  • Some swelling, bruising and tenderness for a few days.
  • A small amount of clear or pink drainage in the first days.
  • A firm area under the scar for several weeks. This usually softens over time.
  • Stitches usually come out in 1 to 2 weeks. Ones on the back are often left a bit longer.
  • Even when the whole sac is removed, a cyst can sometimes come back.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H512
Step · As directed		Healing ointment (OTC; picker search “petrolatum”)
After washing, a thin layer of plain petrolatum ointment on the wound with a clean cotton swab. Keep the wound moist until it has fully healed.		☐ Approve

Cover with a nonstick pad and tape, or an adhesive bandage. Change it once a day, or sooner if it gets wet or dirty.		☐ Approve

☐ Cut
H514
Stop and call if		A fast-growing, tight, painful swelling under the wound, or bleeding that doesn't stop after 20 minutes of firm pressure.☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Steroid injections into skin lesions or scalp proc-intralesional-steroid-injections

Patient sees: Steroid shots into the skin: what to expect · Clinician summary: Why steroid injections are used (keloids, alopecia areata, cysts, plaques), how they feel, side effects like dents or light spots, and repeat visits.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H518
Section		What it is

A steroid (cortisone) medicine is injected with a small needle straight into the skin problem. This calms inflammation right where it is needed, with much less of the medicine reaching the rest of the body than a pill would.

It is used for many problems, including:

  • Keloids and thick, raised scars.
  • Patches of hair loss from alopecia areata.
  • Swollen, painful acne cysts or inflamed cysts.
  • Stubborn patches of psoriasis, eczema, lichen planus and other conditions.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H519
Section		What to expect
  • Each injection stings or pinches briefly. Several small injections may be needed for a larger area or on the scalp.
  • A tiny bit of bleeding or a small bruise is common.
  • The spot may be tender for a day or two.
  • Results usually take a few weeks to show. Many people need repeat treatments, often every 4 to 6 weeks.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H520
Section		Possible side effects
  • A small dent in the skin (thinning under the skin) where the medicine was injected. This often improves over several months.
  • A lighter patch of skin, which is more noticeable in darker skin. This usually fades over months.
  • Small visible blood vessels at the injection spot.
  • If you have diabetes, your blood sugar may run higher for a day or two. Check it more often.

Tell us at your next visit if you notice any of these, so we can adjust your treatment.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H521
Section		After your injections
  • You can usually go back to your normal activities right away.
  • You can usually wash your skin or hair as usual the next day, unless we told you otherwise.
  • An ice pack wrapped in a cloth for a few minutes can ease soreness.
  • Tell us if you are pregnant or breastfeeding before treatment.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H522
Stop and call if		Redness, warmth, swelling or pus at an injection site, or fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Stop and call if		A dent, light patch or thinning skin that is getting worse rather than better.☐ Approve

Photodynamic therapy (PDT) aftercare proc-pdt-aftercare

Patient sees: After photodynamic therapy (PDT): protecting your skin from light · Clinician summary: Strict light avoidance for 48 hours, the sunburn-like reaction and peeling that follow, and home care while it heals.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H525
Section		What it is

Photodynamic therapy (PDT) treats precancerous spots (actinic keratoses) and some other skin problems. A medicine that makes the skin sensitive to light was put on your skin. After it soaked in, the skin was treated with a special light. This activates the medicine and destroys damaged cells.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H526
Section		Avoiding light for 48 hours

The medicine stays in your skin after the visit. Strong light can cause a painful, burn-like reaction. For 48 hours after treatment, unless we told you otherwise:

  • Stay indoors as much as you can, away from windows. Sunlight through glass and through clouds still counts.
  • If you must go out, cover the treated skin fully with a wide-brimmed hat, clothing, gloves or a scarf.
  • Avoid bright indoor lights, such as exam lamps, makeup mirror lights and lights very close to your skin. Normal room light is fine.
  • Sunscreen alone does not protect you, because the medicine reacts to visible light, not just UV light.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H527
Section		What to expect
  • Burning or stinging during and just after the light treatment.
  • Redness and swelling like a sunburn for several days. After treatment of the forehead or scalp, swelling around the eyes can show up the next morning.
  • Crusting, scaling and peeling, usually over about 1 to 2 weeks.
  • Most people look much better in 2 to 4 weeks. Some spots may need another treatment.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H528
Section		Caring for your skin
  • Cool compresses (a clean cloth soaked in cool water) for 10 to 15 minutes can ease burning.
  • Wash gently with lukewarm water and a mild cleanser. Pat dry.
  • Apply a bland moisturizer or petrolatum ointment often.
  • Don't pick at crusts or peeling skin.
  • Skip retinoids, acids and scrubs until your skin has healed.
  • Once healed, use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher every day to protect your skin.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H529
Stop and call if		Severe pain, blisters, or open sores in the treated area.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H530
Stop and call if		Signs of infection: pus, yellow crusts, spreading redness, or fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H531
Stop and call if		Small painful blisters or tingling, especially if you get cold sores.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H532
Stop and call if		Swelling around the eyes that keeps them from opening.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H533

Narrowband UVB phototherapy: what to expect proc-nbuvb-phototherapy

Patient sees: Light treatment (narrowband UVB): what to expect · Clinician summary: How phototherapy works, visit schedule and how long it takes, eye and skin protection, side effects, and medicine checks.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H534
Section		What it is

Narrowband UVB is a type of ultraviolet light used to treat skin conditions such as psoriasis, eczema, vitiligo and some itchy conditions. You stand in a booth (or place your hands or feet in a smaller unit) for a short, measured dose of light. It is different from a tanning bed. The light and the dose are carefully controlled.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H535
Section		The schedule
  • Treatments are usually 2 to 3 times a week, on days that are not back to back.
  • The first treatments last only seconds. The time is slowly increased.
  • Most people see improvement after about 15 to 25 treatments. Vitiligo can take longer.
  • Keeping to the schedule matters. If you miss several visits, the dose may need to be lowered.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H536
Section		Each visit
  • Wear the protective goggles we give you every time.
  • Men should cover the genitals. Cover your face too, if we ask you to.
  • Wear the same type of clothing and underwear each time so the same skin is exposed.
  • Don't put lotion, perfume, oils or sunscreen on treated skin just before your visit, unless we told you to.
  • Tell us about any new medicine or supplement before your next visit. Some, like certain antibiotics and water pills, make skin more sensitive to light.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H537
Section		Side effects and care
  • Mild pinkness, warmth or itch for a day is common. Moisturize often.
  • A sunburn can happen. Tell us before your next treatment.
  • Cold sores can flare.
  • Your skin may tan, and over many years phototherapy can add to skin aging and possibly skin cancer risk. We will check your skin over time.
  • Avoid extra sun on treatment days. Use sunscreen and protective clothing outdoors.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H538
Stop and call if		Painful redness, blistering or a burn after a treatment (skip your next session until we see it).☐ Approve

Patch testing: before, during and after proc-patch-testing

Patient sees: Patch testing: your guide to the appointment · Clinician summary: Medicines to ask about, no sun on the back, keeping the back dry, the reading visits, and delayed reactions.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H542
Section		What it is

Patch testing finds out whether a rash is caused by an allergy to something that touches your skin (allergic contact dermatitis). Small amounts of common substances, like metals, fragrances, preservatives and rubber chemicals, are taped to your back on patches.

It usually takes 3 visits in about a week: one to put the patches on, one about 2 days later to take them off and do a first reading, and a final reading a day or more after that.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H543
Section		Before your appointment
  • Avoid sun, tanning beds and self-tanner on your back for at least 1 to 2 weeks.
  • Don't use steroid creams or other prescription creams on your back for about a week.
  • Ask us about these medicines. They can change the results. Never stop them without talking to the doctor who prescribed them: steroid pills or shots (like prednisone), and medicines that calm the immune system (like methotrexate, cyclosporine, azathioprine, mycophenolate, biologics or JAK inhibitors).
  • Antihistamine pills usually don't affect results, so you can usually keep taking them.
  • Bring your skin care, hair care and work products if we ask.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H544
Section		While the patches are on
  • Keep your back completely dry. No showers on the back, baths, swimming or sweaty exercise. You can wash other parts of your body with a cloth.
  • Keep your back out of the sun.
  • Wear an old T-shirt. The marking ink can stain clothes.
  • Try not to scratch, and avoid heavy lifting or stretching that loosens the patches.
  • If a patch edge comes loose, press it back or cover it with medical tape.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H545
Section		After the patches come off
  • Keep the marks on your back until your final reading. Don't scrub them off.
  • You can usually shower after the final reading.
  • Some allergy reactions show up late. Look at your back (or have someone take a photo) for about a week after the last visit.
  • If you have an allergy, we will give you a list of what to avoid and safe products to use. It can take several weeks of avoiding the allergen for your skin to clear.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H546
Stop and call if		Severe itching, burning or blistering under a patch before your next visit.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H547
Stop and call if		A new red, itchy spot on your back in the week after your final reading.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H548
Stop and call if		You are unsure whether to stop a medicine before testing: ask before stopping it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Contact Dermatitis Society, patient information on patch testing · American Academy of Dermatology, "Contact dermatitis: Diagnosis and treatment" (patient education)

Ingrown toenail and nail surgery aftercare proc-nail-surgery-aftercare

Patient sees: Caring for your toe or finger after nail surgery · Clinician summary: Elevation, bandage care, soaks if advised, shoes and activity, expected drainage, and how the nail regrows.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H549
Section		What was done

Your toe or finger was numbed, and part or all of the nail was removed, or a sample was taken from the nail or nail bed. For an ingrown toenail, the edge of the nail is often removed and the nail root at that edge is treated with a chemical so that part does not grow back.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H550
Section		The first day
  • The numbness wears off over a few hours. Take acetaminophen, as directed on the label, before it wears off, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Rest and keep your foot or hand raised above your heart as much as you can for the first 24 to 48 hours. This eases throbbing and bleeding.
  • Keep the bandage on and dry for 24 to 48 hours, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Some blood on the bandage is normal.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H551
Section		Daily care after that

Once or twice a day until it has healed:

  • Remove the bandage. If it sticks, soak it off in warm water.
  • Wash gently with mild soap and water, or soak for 10 to 15 minutes in warm, soapy water if we advised it. Pat dry.
  • Apply petrolatum ointment and a fresh nonstick bandage.

If the root was treated with a chemical, some clear or yellowish drainage is normal and can last 2 to 6 weeks.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H552
Section		Shoes and activity
  • Wear open-toed shoes, sandals or roomy, soft shoes for 1 to 2 weeks.
  • Avoid running, sports and long walks for about 1 to 2 weeks, or as we told you.
  • Don't swim or use a hot tub until the area has healed.
  • A toenail can take 12 to 18 months to grow back fully, and a fingernail about 6 months. If part of the root was treated, that part of the nail stays narrower.
  • To help prevent ingrown nails, cut toenails straight across and avoid tight shoes.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Vascular laser aftercare (blood vessels and redness) proc-vascular-laser-aftercare

Patient sees: After laser treatment for redness and blood vessels · Clinician summary: Swelling, bruising and redness after vascular laser, cool compresses, sun protection, makeup and activities, and repeat sessions.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H559
Section		What it is

Vascular lasers use light that is absorbed by blood vessels. The heat closes off small vessels so the body can clear them. They are used for broken blood vessels, facial redness (rosacea), port-wine stains, cherry angiomas and some red scars.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H560
Section		What to expect
  • A warm, sunburned feeling for a few hours.
  • Redness and swelling for a few days. Swelling under the eyes is often worse in the morning.
  • Depending on the laser and settings, the skin may bruise purple or gray. Bruising usually fades in 1 to 2 weeks.
  • Most people need several treatments, usually spaced about 4 to 6 weeks apart. Results build gradually.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H561
Section		Caring for your skin
  • Cool compresses or a wrapped ice pack for 10 to 15 minutes at a time can ease heat and swelling.
  • Sleep with your head raised the first night if your face was treated.
  • Wash gently with lukewarm water and a mild cleanser. Pat dry. Don't scrub.
  • Use a bland moisturizer. If any spot crusts or blisters, apply petrolatum ointment and don't pick.
  • Avoid hot showers, saunas, hard exercise and alcohol for a day or two, as heat can increase redness and swelling.
  • You can usually wear makeup the next day if the skin is not broken.
  • Skip retinoids, acids and scrubs on treated skin for a few days, or until we tell you to restart.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H562
Section		Sun protection

Avoid tanning before and after treatment. Tanned skin raises the risk of burns and dark or light spots. Use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher every day and wear a hat outdoors. Sun exposure can also bring redness back over time.

☐ Approve

Cantharidin (blister beetle) wart treatment aftercare proc-cantharidin-wart-aftercare

Patient sees: After cantharidin (blister) treatment for warts · Clinician summary: Washing off on time, the expected blister, caring for it, keeping it away from eyes and mouth, and ring warts.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H567
Section		What it is

Cantharidin is a liquid that comes from the blister beetle. When painted on a wart, it makes a blister form under the wart. The blister lifts the wart off the skin. Applying it does not hurt.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H568
Section		Washing it off
  • Leave the tape or bandage on and keep the area dry for the time we told you. This is often a few hours.
  • Then remove the tape and wash the area well with soap and water.
  • Wash it off sooner if you feel strong burning or pain.
  • Wash your hands after touching the treated spot.
  • Keep the medicine away from the eyes, mouth and other areas. Don't let children suck on treated fingers or touch their eyes. If the medicine gets in an eye, rinse it with lots of water and call us.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H569
Section		What to expect
  • A blister usually forms within 1 to 2 days. It can be tender, and it may be filled with clear fluid or blood.
  • The blister dries and peels off in about 1 to 2 weeks, often taking the wart with it.
  • Warts often need more than one treatment.
  • Sometimes a ring of small warts grows around the edge of the old blister (a ring wart). Let us know if this happens.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H570

  • Leave the blister alone. Don't pop it or cut it.
  • If it breaks, wash gently with soap and water, apply petrolatum ointment and cover with a bandage.
  • A bandage or padding can help if the blister is on the foot or a spot that rubs.
  • For soreness, acetaminophen, as directed on the label, can help.
  • Tell us before treatment if you are pregnant or might be.
☐ Approve

Notes:

Cosmetic (16)

Start here: what the drafter was least sure of
  1. Isotretinoin isn't mentioned anywhere (policy). Instead the peel, resurfacing and pre-procedure handouts ask 'tell us about any acne medicine you have taken in the past year'; the usual 6 to 12 month wait isn't stated.
  2. Fillers: blocked-vessel signs are 'call us right away' stop rules, and vision change means go to the emergency room. The handout says hyaluronic acid filler can often be dissolved.
  3. Sclerotherapy: 911 stop rules for chest pain or shortness of breath, vision change, one-sided weakness or trouble speaking. Rare; confirm the clinic wants these.
  4. Common practice with weak evidence: botulinum toxin, stay upright about 4 hours with no rubbing or exercise that day; fillers, avoid dental work about 2 weeks before and after; no hot tubs or sweating for 24 to 48 hours after laser hair removal or microneedling; PRP, ibuprofen-type drugs 'may make PRP work less well'.
  5. Timings stated as 'usually': stop retinoids 3 to 7 days before (about a week before medium peels and resurfacing); avoid sun or tanning about 4 weeks before lights and lasers; session spacing for IPL, laser hair removal, tattoo removal, RF microneedling and PRP; cryolipolysis takes 35 to 60 minutes.
  6. RF microneedling: pacemaker or metal-implant screening included as a precaution; 'numb or sunken area' stop rule is a judgment call.
  7. Deoxycholic acid: 'several sessions at least 1 month apart'; the label's maximum of 6 sessions isn't stated. Cryolipolysis: paradoxical fat growth described in plain words; the painful bulge, nausea or vomiting (hernia) stop rule is extrapolated from the contraindication.
  8. PRP: a stop rule about vision change or a pale scalp comes from rare case reports; cut it if too alarming. PRP is not called off-label.
  9. Tattoo removal: some white, skin-tone and red cosmetic inks can turn darker with the laser. IPL: not right for every skin tone; brown spots darken ('coffee grounds') as expected.
  10. Sources: AAD and ASDS pages are cited generically ('ASDS patient information on ...'); exact page titles are not confirmed.

Botulinum toxin injections: before and after cos-botulinum-toxin

Patient sees: Your botulinum toxin treatment · Clinician summary: What botulinum toxin does, how to prepare, aftercare, when results show and how long they last.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H575
Section		What it is

Botulinum toxin is a medicine injected in tiny amounts into certain muscles. It relaxes those muscles for a while, which softens lines caused by movement, such as frown lines, forehead lines and crow's feet.

The effect wears off over time, so treatments are repeated if you want to keep the result.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H576
Section		Before your visit
  • Tell us about all medicines and supplements you take, including antibiotics and blood thinners.
  • Tell us if you have a nerve or muscle condition, such as myasthenia gravis.
  • Tell us if you are pregnant, may be pregnant or are breastfeeding. Treatment is usually postponed.
  • Fish oil, vitamin E and pain relievers like ibuprofen can make bruising more likely. Ask us before stopping anything. Never stop a prescribed blood thinner unless the doctor who prescribed it agrees.
  • If you have a big event coming up, plan treatment at least 2 weeks ahead in case of bruising.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H577
Section		What to expect

The injections take a few minutes and feel like small pinches. Small bumps at the injection spots usually fade within an hour.

  • Results usually start in 3 to 5 days and reach their full effect in about 2 weeks.
  • Results usually last about 3 to 4 months, and this varies from person to person.
  • Mild bruising, tenderness or a headache can happen and usually settle in a few days.
  • Rarely, an eyelid or brow can droop for a few weeks. This wears off as the medicine does.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H578
Section		Caring for it at home

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Do not rub, press or massage the treated areas for the rest of the day.
  • Stay upright for about 4 hours. Avoid lying face down.
  • Skip hard exercise, saunas and hot tubs for the rest of the day.
  • A cold pack wrapped in a cloth can help with swelling or bruising. Hold it on gently.
  • You can usually wash your face and wear makeup later the same day, using a light touch.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H579
Section		Helpful tips

Wait the full 2 weeks before judging your result. If something looks uneven at that point, let us know. Small adjustments are often possible.

Tell us before your next treatment if you started any new medicines.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H580
Stop and call if		Trouble swallowing, speaking or breathing, or new muscle weakness away from the treated area, in the days or weeks after (get help right away; call 911 for trouble breathing).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H581
Stop and call if		Double vision, blurred vision, or an eyelid that droops enough to block your vision.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H582
Stop and call if		Eye dryness, irritation or trouble closing your eye fully.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H583
Stop and call if		A bruise or swelling that keeps getting bigger or more painful after the first day.☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Botulinum toxin therapy: FAQs" (patient education) · FDA prescribing information for botulinum toxin products (boxed warning: distant spread of toxin effect; warnings and precautions)

Dermal fillers: before and after cos-dermal-fillers

Patient sees: Your dermal filler treatment · Clinician summary: Preparing for filler, normal swelling and bruising, aftercare, and urgent warning signs of a blocked blood vessel.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H584
Section		What it is

A dermal filler is a gel injected under the skin to add volume or smooth lines. Common areas are the lips, cheeks, the lines from the nose to the mouth, and under the eyes.

Most fillers we use are made of hyaluronic acid, a substance your skin makes naturally. Your body slowly breaks it down over months. If there is a problem with hyaluronic acid filler, it can often be dissolved with another injection.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H585
Section		Before your visit
  • Tell us about all medicines and supplements, and any past filler or other treatments in the area.
  • If you have ever had cold sores and are having lip or mouth-area filler, tell us. We may prescribe an antiviral medicine to take as prescribed.
  • Fish oil, vitamin E and pain relievers like ibuprofen can increase bruising. Ask us before stopping anything, and never stop a prescribed blood thinner on your own.
  • Many clinics suggest avoiding dental work for about 2 weeks before and after filler.
  • Treatment is usually postponed during pregnancy and breastfeeding.
  • Plan at least 2 weeks before a big event.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H586
Section		What to expect
  • Swelling, tenderness and redness are common. Swelling is often worst on day 1 to 3, especially in the lips, and usually settles within 1 to 2 weeks.
  • Bruising can happen and usually fades in 1 to 2 weeks.
  • The area may feel a little firm or lumpy at first. This usually softens over 2 weeks.
  • Your final look is easier to judge after about 2 weeks.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H587
Section		Caring for it at home

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Do not press on, massage or rub the area unless we showed you how.
  • Use a cold pack wrapped in a cloth for a few minutes at a time to ease swelling.
  • Avoid hard exercise, saunas, hot tubs and strong heat for 24 to 48 hours.
  • Keep makeup off the injection spots until the next day.
  • Sleep with your head a little raised the first night if you have swelling.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H588
Section		A rare but serious risk

Very rarely, filler can block a blood vessel and cut off blood flow to the skin nearby or, even more rarely, to the eye. This usually shows up during treatment or within the first hours or days. Treating it quickly makes a big difference, so please read the warning signs at the end of this handout before you leave.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H589
Stop and call if		Pain that is severe, getting worse, or much more than expected, especially away from the injection spots (call us right away).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H590
Stop and call if		Skin near the filler that turns white, or a dusky, blue-gray, purple or blotchy net-like patch (call us right away).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H591
Stop and call if		Any change in vision, eye pain, or a drooping eyelid after filler: this is an emergency. Call us and go to the nearest emergency room.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H592
Stop and call if		Small blisters, scabs or open sores near the treated area in the days after treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H593
Stop and call if		A red, warm, tender or growing lump, days to months after treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Superficial chemical peel cos-superficial-peel

Patient sees: Your light chemical peel · Clinician summary: Light (superficial) peel: preparation, what peeling looks like, gentle aftercare and sun protection.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H594
Section		What it is

A light chemical peel uses a mild acid solution to remove the very top layer of skin. It can help with dullness, uneven tone, mild sun damage and some types of acne.

Light peels are usually done as a series, often every 2 to 4 weeks. Results build slowly over several sessions.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H595
Section		Before your peel

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Stop retinoid creams (such as tretinoin, adapalene or retinol) and acid products about 3 to 7 days before.
  • Do not wax, use hair-removal creams, or have laser treatment on the area for about 1 week before.
  • Avoid tanning and sunburn. We may reschedule if your skin is sunburned or irritated.
  • Tell us if you get cold sores, if you are pregnant or breastfeeding, or about any acne medicine you have taken in the past year.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H596
Section		What to expect

During the peel you may feel warmth, tingling or stinging for a few minutes.

  • Your skin may look pink, like a mild sunburn, for 1 to 3 days.
  • Light flaking or dryness often starts on day 2 or 3 and usually ends within a week.
  • Some people barely peel at all. The peel still works.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H597
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Do wash gently with lukewarm water and a mild cleanser. Pat dry.
  • Do use a plain, fragrance-free moisturizer as often as your skin feels dry.
  • Do use broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher every day.
  • Don't pick, peel or scrub flaking skin. Let it come off on its own.
  • Don't use retinoids, acids, scrubs or other strong products until the flaking stops, usually about a week.
  • Makeup is usually fine the next day if your skin is not stinging.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H598
Section		Helpful tips

If you have darker skin, dark spots can sometimes appear after a peel. Strict sun protection lowers this risk. Tell us early if you notice any.

Medium-depth chemical peel cos-medium-peel

Patient sees: Your medium-depth chemical peel · Clinician summary: Medium-depth peel: preparation (incl. antiviral), the 1 to 2 week healing course, wound-style aftercare, sun avoidance.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H606
Section		What it is

A medium-depth peel uses a stronger acid solution to remove the top layers of skin. It can help with sun damage, uneven color, rough patches and fine wrinkles.

It has more healing time than a light peel. Most people plan for about 1 to 2 weeks of time at home.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H607
Section		Before your peel

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • We may ask you to use certain creams for a few weeks before to prepare your skin. Use them as prescribed.
  • Stop retinoid and acid products as directed, usually about a week before.
  • If you have ever had cold sores, tell us. We often prescribe an antiviral medicine to start before the peel.
  • Avoid tanning and sunburn for several weeks before.
  • Tell us about any acne medicine in the past year, a history of raised scars (keloids), or if you are pregnant or breastfeeding. Treatment is usually postponed in pregnancy and breastfeeding.
☐ Approve

  • The peel stings and burns for several minutes. We may give you something to ease this.
  • Swelling is common in the first 2 to 3 days, especially around the eyes.

  • Fresh pink skin underneath can stay pink for a few weeks.
☐ Approve

  • Do keep taking the antiviral medicine as prescribed if we gave you one.
  • Don't pick, pull or rub peeling skin. This can cause scars or dark marks.
  • Don't use makeup, retinoids, acids or scrubs until we say your skin has healed.
  • Don't go in the sun. After healing, use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher every day and a wide-brimmed hat.

Microneedling cos-microneedling

Patient sees: Your microneedling treatment · Clinician summary: Microneedling: preparation, the 1 to 3 day redness, gentle aftercare, what to keep off the skin, sessions and timing.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H617
Section		What it is

Microneedling uses a device with many very fine needles to make tiny channels in the skin. As the skin repairs itself, it makes new collagen, a protein that gives skin its firmness. It is used for acne scars, fine lines and skin texture.

Most people need a series of treatments, often 3 to 6 sessions spaced about 4 to 6 weeks apart. Improvement shows gradually over months.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H618
Section		Before your treatment

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Stop retinoid creams and acid products about 3 to 5 days before.
  • Avoid sunburn and tanning.
  • Tell us if you get cold sores, have active acne or a rash in the area, form raised scars (keloids), or are pregnant or breastfeeding.
  • Come with clean skin and no makeup. We usually apply a numbing cream first.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H619
Section		What to expect
  • Your skin will look red, like a sunburn, and may feel warm and tight for 1 to 3 days.
  • Some swelling and tiny dots of bleeding can happen on the day of treatment.
  • Mild dryness or flaking for a few days is common.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H620
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Do wash gently with lukewarm water and a mild cleanser starting that evening or the next morning.
  • Do use a plain, fragrance-free moisturizer.
  • Do use broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher once your skin is no longer raw, and stay out of direct sun.
  • Don't put on makeup for about 24 hours.
  • Don't use any serums or products we did not approve for the first few days. The tiny channels can let ingredients go deeper and cause irritation.
  • Don't use retinoids, acids or scrubs for about 1 week.
  • Avoid heavy sweating, swimming, saunas and hot tubs for 24 to 48 hours.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Radiofrequency microneedling cos-rf-microneedling

Patient sees: Your radiofrequency microneedling treatment · Clinician summary: RF microneedling: device and implant screening, swelling and grid marks, aftercare, series timing.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H628
Section		What it is

Radiofrequency (RF) microneedling combines very fine needles with gentle heat delivered below the skin surface. The heat helps the skin make new collagen, which can improve skin texture, acne scars, fine lines and mild looseness.

A series of about 3 sessions, spaced 4 to 6 weeks apart, is common. Changes appear slowly, often over 3 to 6 months after your last session.

  • Stop retinoid creams and acid products about 3 to 5 days before.

Laser resurfacing (ablative and fractional) cos-laser-resurfacing

Patient sees: Your laser resurfacing treatment · Clinician summary: Ablative vs fractional resurfacing: preparation (antiviral, sun, retinoids), healing timeline, wound-style aftercare.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H637
Section		What it is

Laser resurfacing uses laser light to treat wrinkles, sun damage, scars and uneven skin.

  • Ablative lasers remove thin layers of skin. Results can be more noticeable, but healing takes longer, often 1 to 2 weeks or more.
  • Fractional lasers treat tiny columns of skin and leave the skin in between untouched, so healing is faster. They are often done as a series.

We will tell you which type you are having and what your healing time is likely to be.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H638
Section		Before your treatment

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • If you have ever had cold sores, tell us. We often prescribe an antiviral medicine to start before treatment. Take it as prescribed.
  • Stop retinoid creams and acid products about 1 week before.
  • Avoid tanning, self-tanner and sunburn for at least 4 weeks before.
  • Tell us about any acne medicine in the past year, raised scars (keloids), past radiation to the area, or pregnancy or breastfeeding. Treatment is usually postponed in pregnancy.
  • If we give you medicine that makes you drowsy, arrange a ride home.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H639
Section		What to expect
  • Fractional: redness and swelling for a few days, like a sunburn. The skin may look bronzed and feel sandpapery, then flake off within about a week.
  • Ablative: the skin will be raw, swollen and may ooze and crust for the first week or so. New skin forms over 1 to 2 weeks.
  • Pinkness after ablative treatment can last weeks to a few months.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H640
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Do clean gently as we showed you, and keep treated skin covered with a thin layer of plain petrolatum ointment until it has healed.
  • Do sleep with your head raised for the first few nights.
  • Do keep taking any antiviral or other medicine as prescribed.
  • Don't pick, scratch or rub crusts or peeling skin.
  • Don't wear makeup or use retinoids, acids or scrubs until we say your skin has healed.
  • Don't go in the sun. After healing, use broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher daily and a wide-brimmed hat.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit

IPL / broadband light cos-ipl

Patient sees: Your intense pulsed light (IPL) treatment · Clinician summary: IPL/BBL for redness and sun spots: tan and medicine screening, the 'coffee grounds' darkening of spots, aftercare, series.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H648
Section		What it is

Intense pulsed light (IPL), also called broadband light, uses flashes of bright light to treat brown sun spots, redness, small broken blood vessels and uneven skin tone.

Most people need a series of about 3 to 5 sessions, spaced about 3 to 4 weeks apart. IPL is not a good fit for every skin tone or for tanned skin. We will check your skin first.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H649
Section		Before your treatment

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Avoid sun, tanning beds and self-tanner for about 4 weeks before. Treating tanned skin raises the risk of burns and color changes.
  • Tell us about all medicines, including antibiotics and other medicines that make you sensitive to light.
  • Tell us if you have melasma, get cold sores, or are pregnant.
  • Stop retinoid creams and acid products a few days before.
  • Come with clean skin, with no makeup, lotion or self-tanner on the area.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H650
Section		What to expect

Each flash feels like a quick snap of a rubber band with warmth.

  • Redness and mild swelling usually last a few hours to a few days.
  • Brown spots often turn darker, like coffee grounds, then flake off over 1 to 2 weeks. This is expected.
  • Small blood vessels may look darker or bruised for a short time before they fade.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H651
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Do use a cold pack wrapped in cloth if your skin feels warm.
  • Do wash gently and use a plain, fragrance-free moisturizer.
  • Do use broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher every day and stay out of strong sun between sessions.
  • Don't scrub, pick or peel the darkened spots. Let them flake off on their own.
  • Don't use retinoids, acids or scrubs for a few days, or until any redness settles.
  • Avoid hot showers, saunas and hard exercise for the first day.

Laser hair removal cos-laser-hair-removal

Patient sees: Your laser hair removal treatment · Clinician summary: Shave-don't-wax prep, sun and tan avoidance, normal bumps and shedding, aftercare, realistic results and session spacing.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H656
Section		What it is

Laser hair removal uses light that is absorbed by the pigment in the hair. The heat damages the hair root so it grows back less.

It reduces hair, but it usually does not remove all of it. Most people need about 6 or more sessions. Face sessions are often spaced about 4 weeks apart and body sessions about 6 to 8 weeks apart. Some people need touch-up sessions later. Very light, gray, white or red hair usually does not respond well.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H657
Section		Before your treatment

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Shave the area the day before. Shaving is fine.
  • Do not wax, pluck, thread or use an epilator for about 4 to 6 weeks before. The laser needs the hair root in place.
  • Avoid sun, tanning beds and self-tanner for about 4 weeks before.
  • Tell us about medicines that make you sensitive to light, a history of cold sores if we are treating the face, and whether you are pregnant.
  • Come with clean skin, with no lotion, deodorant or makeup on the area.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H658
Section		What to expect
  • Each pulse can feel like a snap of a rubber band with heat.
  • Redness and small bumps around the hairs, like goosebumps, are common for a few hours to 2 days.
  • Over 1 to 3 weeks, treated hairs fall out. This can look like regrowth at first.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H659
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Do use a cold pack wrapped in cloth if the area feels warm.
  • Do use broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher on treated skin that is exposed to sun, for the whole course of treatment.
  • Do shave between sessions if you like.
  • Don't wax, pluck or thread between sessions.
  • Avoid hot tubs, saunas and heavy sweating for 24 to 48 hours.
  • Avoid deodorant or perfumed products on treated skin for a day if it stings.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H660
Stop and call if		Blisters, crusting, open sores or burn marks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H661
Stop and call if		Redness, swelling or pain that gets worse after the first 2 days.☐ Approve

☐ Cut
H662
Stop and call if		Dark or light patches of skin in the treated area.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H663
Stop and call if		Tingling, then small clustered blisters, especially on the face (possible cold sore).☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Laser tattoo removal cos-tattoo-removal

Patient sees: Your laser tattoo removal treatment · Clinician summary: Realistic expectations (many sessions, color matters), the normal whitening, blisters and crusting, wound-style aftercare.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H664
Section		What it is

Laser tattoo removal uses very short pulses of light to break the tattoo ink into tiny pieces. Your body then slowly clears the ink over the following weeks.

Most tattoos need many sessions, often 6 or more, spaced about 6 to 8 weeks apart. Black and dark blue ink usually fade best. Some colors, such as green, light blue and yellow, are harder to remove. Complete removal is not always possible, and some fading of skin color or a change in skin texture can remain.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H665
Section		Before your treatment

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Avoid sun, tanning and self-tanner on the area for about 4 weeks before.
  • Tell us if the tattoo is cosmetic (such as eyebrows, lip liner or eyeliner). Some white, skin-tone and red inks can turn darker with laser.
  • Tell us if you ever had an allergic reaction to a tattoo, or if you form raised scars (keloids).
  • Tell us if you are pregnant.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H666
Section		What to expect
  • Right after a pulse, the tattoo turns white for about 20 minutes. This is normal.
  • Redness, swelling and tenderness are common for a few days. There may be tiny spots of bleeding.
  • Blisters often form within 1 to 3 days, and a thin crust can follow. These usually heal in 1 to 2 weeks.
  • The tattoo fades a little more over the weeks after each session.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H667
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Do keep the area clean. Wash gently with mild soap and water once a day and pat dry.
  • Do apply a thin layer of plain petrolatum ointment and a non-stick bandage until the skin has healed.
  • Do use a cold pack wrapped in cloth for swelling.
  • Don't pop blisters or pick scabs. Let them heal on their own.
  • Don't soak the area in a bath, pool or hot tub until it has healed.
  • Once healed, use broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher on the area.
☐ Approve

Sclerotherapy for leg veins cos-sclerotherapy

Patient sees: Your sclerotherapy treatment · Clinician summary: Clot-risk screening, compression and walking, expected bruising and staining, timeline, and urgent signs of a clot.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H674
Section		What it is

Sclerotherapy treats spider veins and small varicose veins in the legs. We inject a solution into the vein that irritates its lining, so it closes and slowly fades.

Spider veins usually fade over 3 to 6 weeks. Larger veins can take 3 to 4 months. Many people need 2 or more sessions, often spaced about 4 to 6 weeks apart. Treatment does not stop new veins from forming in the future.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H675
Section		Before your treatment
  • Tell us if you have ever had a blood clot, if you take hormones (such as birth control pills or hormone therapy), or if you take a blood thinner. Never stop a prescribed blood thinner unless the doctor who prescribed it agrees.
  • Tell us if you are pregnant or breastfeeding. Treatment is usually postponed.
  • Tell us about any recent surgery or long trips by plane or car.
  • On the day, do not put lotion on your legs. Wear loose shorts or bring them.
  • Bring compression stockings if we asked you to get them.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H676
Section		What to expect
  • The injections feel like small pinches. Some solutions sting or cramp briefly.
  • Bruising, redness and small raised bumps at the injection spots are common and fade over days to weeks.
  • Treated veins may look darker or lumpy at first. Brown lines along the veins can take months to fade.
  • Sometimes a fine network of tiny red veins appears near a treated area. It often fades on its own.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H677
Section		Caring for it at home

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Do walk for at least 10 to 20 minutes right after treatment and every day after.
  • Do wear compression stockings as directed.
  • Don't sit or stand still for long periods. Take breaks to walk.
  • Avoid hard exercise and heavy lifting for a few days.
  • Avoid hot baths, hot tubs and saunas for a few days. Short lukewarm showers are fine.
  • Keep treated areas out of the sun until bruising fades, to lower the risk of brown marks.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Deoxycholic acid injections (under-chin fat) cos-deoxycholic-acid

Patient sees: Your under-chin fat injection treatment · Clinician summary: Deoxycholic acid for submental fat: screening, expected swelling and numbness, aftercare, series, nerve and swallowing warnings.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H683
Section		What it is

Deoxycholic acid is a medicine injected into fat under the chin. It breaks down fat cells in the treated area, and your body clears them over the following weeks.

Most people need more than one session. Sessions are spaced at least 1 month apart, and several sessions are common. Results show gradually after each one. It is not a weight-loss treatment.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H684

  • Tell us if you have had trouble swallowing, an infection in the area, or surgery or cosmetic treatment on your face, neck or chin.
  • Tell us about bleeding problems and all medicines, including blood thinners and aspirin. Never stop a prescribed blood thinner on your own.
  • Tell us if you are pregnant or breastfeeding. Treatment is usually postponed.
  • Plan for visible swelling for 1 to 2 weeks. It may be more noticeable after the first session.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H685
Section		What to expect

We give a number of small injections under the chin. Burning or stinging during treatment is common.

  • Swelling under the chin is expected and can be large. It usually goes down over 1 to 2 weeks.
  • Bruising, tenderness, numbness and firmness under the chin are common. Numbness and firmness can last several weeks.
  • Each session's results usually take 4 to 6 weeks to show.

  • Don't massage the area.
  • Avoid hard exercise and tight collars or chin straps for a few days.

Cryolipolysis (fat freezing) cos-cryolipolysis

Patient sees: Your fat freezing (cryolipolysis) treatment · Clinician summary: Cold-condition screening, what the session feels like, expected numbness and delayed soreness, results timeline, rare enlargement.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H692
Section		What it is

Cryolipolysis cools a pinchable bulge of fat to a temperature that damages fat cells but not the skin. Your body slowly clears the damaged fat cells over the next few months.

It is meant for small areas of fat that don't respond to diet and exercise, such as the belly, flanks or under the chin. It is not a weight-loss treatment, and it does not treat loose skin or cellulite. Some people want a second session on the same area.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H693
Section		Before your treatment

Tell us if you have:

  • A condition triggered by cold, such as cryoglobulinemia, cold agglutinin disease or paroxysmal cold hemoglobinuria. Treatment is not safe with these.
  • Raynaud's (fingers that turn white or blue in the cold), cold hives, or poor feeling in the area.
  • A hernia, recent surgery or scars in the area.
  • A pregnancy, or are breastfeeding.

Wear comfortable clothes. Bring something to read or watch: a session can take about 35 to 60 minutes per area.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H694
Section		What to expect
  • You will feel pulling and intense cold for the first several minutes. The area then usually goes numb.
  • After the applicator comes off, the area may look red and firm. We may massage it briefly, which can be uncomfortable.
  • Redness, swelling, bruising, tingling and tenderness are common for days to a couple of weeks.
  • Numbness can last several weeks.
  • Some people feel aching, itching or sharp pain a few days later. This usually fades within a couple of weeks.
  • Changes usually show after 1 to 3 months.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H695
Section		Caring for it at home
  • You can usually go back to normal activities right away.
  • Wear loose, comfortable clothing over the area.
  • For soreness, an over-the-counter pain reliever as directed on the label can help, unless you've been told to avoid it.
  • Don't put heating pads or ice on numb skin. You may not feel it if the skin is getting hurt.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Platelet-rich plasma (PRP) for hair loss cos-prp-hair

Patient sees: Your platelet-rich plasma (PRP) treatment for hair loss · Clinician summary: PRP for hair loss: what it is and evidence limits, screening, what to expect, scalp aftercare, session schedule.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H700
Section		What it is

Platelet-rich plasma (PRP) is made from a small sample of your own blood. We spin the blood in a machine to concentrate the platelets, the parts of blood that help with healing. The PRP is then injected into thinning areas of the scalp.

PRP may help some people with certain types of hair loss, but results vary and it does not work for everyone. It often works best together with other hair-loss treatments. A common plan is about 3 sessions about 1 month apart, then follow-up sessions every few months. Any change usually takes 3 to 6 months to see.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H701
Section		Before your treatment
  • Tell us if you have a bleeding or platelet disorder, cancer, an infection, or a scalp condition.
  • Tell us about all medicines, including blood thinners and aspirin. Pain relievers like ibuprofen may make PRP work less well. Ask us whether to pause them, and never stop a prescribed blood thinner or aspirin on your own.
  • Eat a meal and drink plenty of water beforehand to lower the chance of feeling faint during the blood draw.
  • Wash your hair the morning of treatment, and skip hair sprays and gels.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H702
Section		What to expect
  • The scalp injections can sting. Numbing is often used.
  • Tenderness, mild swelling, a headache or tiny spots of bleeding are common for a day or two.
  • Swelling can move down to the forehead or around the eyes the next day. It usually fades within a few days.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H703
Section		Caring for it at home

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Wait until the next day to wash your hair. Then use a gentle shampoo and lukewarm water.
  • For discomfort, acetaminophen as directed on the label is usually preferred over ibuprofen or similar pain relievers for a few days.
  • Avoid hair dye, perms, relaxers and other chemical treatments for a few days.
  • Avoid hard exercise, saunas and hot tubs for the rest of the day.
  • Keep using your other hair-loss treatments as directed, unless we told you to pause them.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H704

☐ Edit

H705

Preparing skin before cosmetic procedures cos-pre-procedure-prep

Patient sees: Getting your skin ready for a cosmetic procedure · Clinician summary: General prep: pausing retinoids and actives, sun and tan avoidance, cold-sore history, medicines and supplements, day-of tips.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H708
Section		Why preparation matters

Getting your skin ready helps lower the chance of irritation, infection and color changes, and helps your skin heal well. The steps below are general. Your care team may give you different timing for your specific treatment. If so, follow their instructions.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H709
Section		Pause strong skin products

Unless we told you otherwise:

  • Stop retinoids (tretinoin, adapalene, tazarotene, retinol) about 3 to 7 days before peels, lasers, light treatments and microneedling.
  • Stop acid products and scrubs (glycolic, salicylic or lactic acid, and grainy scrubs) for the same time.
  • Do not wax, use hair-removal creams or have other treatments on the area for about 1 week before.
  • Keep using a gentle cleanser, a plain moisturizer and sunscreen.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H710
Section		Protect your skin from the sun

Tanned or sunburned skin is more likely to burn or develop dark or light patches after lasers, light treatments and peels.

  • Avoid sunbathing, tanning beds and self-tanner for about 4 weeks before light and laser treatments.
  • Use broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher every day, and wear a hat outdoors.
  • If you get a sunburn or tan before your visit, tell us. We may need to reschedule.
☐ Approve

  • Acne medicines you have taken in the past year.
  • Raised scars (keloids), dark marks after injuries, or melasma.
  • Pregnancy or breastfeeding. Most cosmetic procedures are postponed.

Notes:

Caring for your skin between treatments cos-between-treatments

Patient sees: Caring for your skin between cosmetic treatments · Clinician summary: Daily sun protection, restarting products, why sessions are spaced, realistic expectations, and what to tell us before the next visit.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H720
Section		Why spacing matters

Many cosmetic treatments work best as a series. Your skin needs time between sessions to heal fully and to build new collagen, which happens slowly over weeks.

  • Sessions are often spaced about 4 weeks apart or more, depending on the treatment.
  • Going back too soon can raise the risk of irritation, color changes and scarring, without better results.
  • Please don't add treatments elsewhere, such as peels, lasers or facials, without telling us first.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H721
Section		Sun protection every day

Sun exposure can undo results and cause dark patches, especially after lasers, light treatments and peels.

  • Use broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher every morning, even on cloudy days.
  • Reapply every 2 hours outdoors and after swimming or sweating.
  • Wear a wide-brimmed hat and seek shade, especially midday.
  • Skip tanning beds and self-tanner during your treatment series.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H722
Section		Your daily routine
  • Use a gentle cleanser and a plain moisturizer.
  • Restart retinoids, acids and other active products only once your skin has fully healed, and when we say it's okay. Start slowly, every other night at first.
  • Stop these products again a few days before your next session, as directed.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H723
Section		Realistic expectations

Most cosmetic treatments improve the skin gradually. They do not make it perfect, and results vary from person to person.

  • Changes often take weeks to months to show.
  • Taking photos in the same light every few weeks can help you see slow changes.
  • Aging and sun exposure continue, so many results need maintenance over time.
  • If you're not happy with your progress, talk with us. We can review your goals and options.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Notes:

Pediatric dermatology (18)

Start here: what the drafter was least sure of
  1. Hemangioma: most growth done by about 5 months; high-risk features named (near eye, nose, lip, ear or diaper area; 5 or more; a large facial patch; beard area). Medicine 'with or right after a feeding' and warning signs (very sleepy, sweaty, cold or clammy, wheezing, not eating) clearly describe propranolol without naming it.
  2. Impetigo: back to school after at least 24 hours of antibiotics (hedged). A stop rule covers puffy face or cola-colored urine in the following weeks (post-strep kidney inflammation); decide if that belongs in a parent handout.
  3. Scabies (CDC): wash items used in the past 3 days, bag unwashables at least 3 days, back to school the day after treatment; treat scalp and face in babies; ask first for infants, pregnancy or breastfeeding.
  4. Insect repellent: no DEET under 2 months, no oil of lemon eucalyptus under 3 (AAP/CDC). Hydrocortisone: ask first under age 2.
  5. Teen acne: OTC adapalene 'for ages 12 and up' (OTC label); acne under age 7 or with early puberty signs needs a visit; retinoid pregnancy caution.
  6. Head lice: products differ on conditioner, second-treatment timing and household cleaning; hedged with 'check the label' and 'follow our directions'.
  7. Birthmarks: 6+ café-au-lait spots or armpit/groin freckling prompts a neurofibromatosis check; a forehead or periocular port-wine stain needs eye checks; earlier laser 'often works better'.
  8. Alopecia areata: 'newer medicines by mouth for some teens' (JAK inhibitors, unnamed). Tinea capitis: some oral antifungals absorb better with food; generic liver-symptom stop rule. Warts: salicylic acid warnings from the OTC label; duct tape omitted (mixed evidence).
  9. Infant sunscreen: shade and clothing first under 6 months; 'ask us' about small amounts on small areas (AAP/FDA).
  10. Sources: some HealthyChildren.org titles are approximate ('Skin Conditions in Newborns', 'Tinea Infections'). Papers cited by author/journal/year only: Krowchuk 2019, Nolt 2022, Balk 2011, Eichenfield 2013 and 2014, Reynolds 2024.

Eczema in babies and children peds-eczema

Patient sees: Caring for your child's eczema · Clinician summary: What eczema is, daily bathing and moisturizing ('soak and seal'), handling flares, and itch tips for parents.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H731
Section		What it is

Eczema (also called atopic dermatitis) makes the skin dry, itchy and easily irritated. In babies it often starts on the cheeks, scalp and outside of the arms and legs. In older children it often shows up in the bends of the elbows and knees, the wrists and the ankles.

Eczema is not contagious, and it is not caused by anything you did. It tends to run in families with eczema, asthma or hay fever. Many children's eczema gets milder as they grow.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H732
Section		Bathing and moisturizing

Daily care keeps the skin barrier strong and is the most important part of treatment.

  • Give a short bath in lukewarm (not hot) water, about 5 to 10 minutes, daily or every other day unless we told you otherwise.
  • Use a small amount of a mild, fragrance-free cleanser only where needed.
  • Pat the skin gently with a towel so it is still a little damp.
  • Within 3 minutes, put on any prescribed medicine first, then a thick fragrance-free moisturizer all over.
  • Moisturize again at least once more during the day, even when the skin looks clear.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H733
Section		When eczema flares

A flare is when patches become red, rough, very itchy or weepy. Use the flare medicine we prescribed exactly as prescribed: on the red, rough patches only, and for only as long as we said. Then go back to daily moisturizing.

Do not stop moisturizing during a flare. Do not use a stronger medicine, or an adult's prescription, on your child without asking us.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H734
Section		Helping with itch and triggers
  • Keep fingernails short and smooth. Soft cotton mittens or sleepers can help babies at night.
  • Dress your child in soft, breathable cotton. Avoid wool and scratchy fabrics.
  • Use fragrance-free laundry detergent and skip fabric softener sheets.
  • Avoid overheating. Sweat can make itch worse.
  • Rinse off after swimming, then moisturize.
  • Common triggers include dry air, heat, sweat, soaps, fragrances, and colds or other infections.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H735
Section		Helpful tips

Eczema comes and goes. Even with good care, most children have some flares. That does not mean you are doing something wrong.

Please talk with us before cutting foods out of your child's diet. Removing foods without testing and guidance can affect growth and nutrition, and usually does not clear eczema.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Diaper rash peds-diaper-rash

Patient sees: Caring for diaper rash · Clinician summary: Causes of diaper rash, frequent changes, air time and barrier cream, and signs of yeast or infection.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H743
Section		What it is

Diaper rash is red, irritated skin in the diaper area. It is very common. It usually happens when skin stays wet or touches urine and stool for too long. Rubbing from the diaper, new foods, diarrhea, and antibiotics can also play a part.

Sometimes a yeast infection develops on top of the rash. Yeast rash is often bright red, goes into the skin folds, and has small red spots around the edges.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H744
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Change diapers often, and as soon as possible after a stool.
  • Clean gently with lukewarm water and a soft cloth, or fragrance-free, alcohol-free wipes. Pat dry; don't rub.
  • Let your baby go without a diaper for some time each day when you can.
  • At every change, spread a thick layer of barrier cream or ointment (zinc oxide or petrolatum), like frosting a cake.
  • You do not need to scrub off all of the old cream at each change. Gently wipe off what is soiled and add more.
  • Fasten diapers a bit loosely so air can get in.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H745
Section		What to avoid
  • Baby powder, including talc and cornstarch. Powder can be breathed in.
  • Wipes or soaps with fragrance or alcohol, which can sting.
  • Tight plastic pants over the diaper.
  • Steroid creams, antifungal creams or other medicines unless we recommended them. If we prescribed one, use it as prescribed and put the barrier cream on top.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H746
Section		What to expect

With good care, most diaper rash gets better in 2 to 3 days. A rash that lasts longer, keeps coming back, or looks like yeast may need a prescription cream. Let us know if you are worried.

☐ Approve

Cradle cap peds-cradle-cap

Patient sees: Caring for cradle cap · Clinician summary: What cradle cap is, gentle washing and loosening scales, and when it needs a visit.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H753
Section		What it is

Cradle cap is greasy, yellow or white scale on a baby's scalp. Doctors call it infant seborrheic dermatitis. It can also appear on the eyebrows, behind the ears, on the eyelids, or in the diaper area.

Cradle cap is common and harmless. It is not caused by poor hygiene, it is not an allergy, and it is not contagious. It usually does not itch or bother babies.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H754
Section		What to expect

Cradle cap usually starts in the first few months of life. It often clears on its own over weeks to months, and most babies are past it by their first birthday. Home care helps the scale come off but does not always make it go away faster.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H755
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Wash your baby's scalp with a mild, fragrance-free baby shampoo once a day or every few days.
  • While the shampoo is in, gently brush the scalp with a soft baby brush or soft toothbrush to loosen scale.
  • If scale is thick, rub a small amount of petroleum jelly or a few drops of mineral oil into the scalp. Let it sit for a few minutes, then brush gently and wash it out well.
  • Rinse well and pat dry.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H756
Section		What to avoid
  • Picking or scratching off the scale. This can make the skin sore and cause infection.
  • Adult dandruff shampoos or medicated creams unless we recommended them. If we did, use them only as we directed and keep them out of your baby's eyes.
  • Leaving oil on the scalp for long periods. Wash it out.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H757
Stop and call if		The skin under the scale is very red, swollen, cracked, oozing or bleeding.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H758
Stop and call if		The rash spreads to the face, body or diaper area, or seems itchy or uncomfortable.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H759
Stop and call if		Cradle cap is still heavy after several weeks of home care, or is still there after 12 months of age.☐ Approve

H760

Infantile hemangioma peds-hemangioma

Patient sees: Your baby's hemangioma · Clinician summary: How infant hemangiomas grow and shrink, what to watch for, why some need early treatment, and caring for a sore.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H761
Section		What it is

An infantile hemangioma (he-man-jee-OH-muh) is a growth of extra blood vessels in the skin. It is sometimes called a strawberry mark. It is not cancer and is not caused by anything that happened during pregnancy.

Some are bright red and raised. Others are deeper under the skin and look bluish or like a soft bump. Many are not visible at birth, or show only as a faint mark, and appear in the first few weeks of life.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H762
Section		What to expect

Hemangiomas follow a typical pattern:

  • Growth: most growth happens in the first few months. Much of it is usually done by about 5 months of age.
  • Slowing: growth then slows and stops.
  • Shrinking: over several years the hemangioma slowly fades and flattens.

Many small hemangiomas need no treatment. Some leave behind extra skin, color changes or a scar, which can be treated later if needed.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H763
Section		Why some need early care

Because growth happens early, an early visit matters. We may recommend treatment if a hemangioma:

  • Is near the eye, nose, lip or ear, or in the diaper area
  • Is large, growing fast, or forms a sore
  • Is one of several (5 or more) hemangiomas on the skin
  • Is in a large patch on the face, or in the beard area (chin, jaw, neck)

We may talk with you about a prescription medicine, given by mouth or put on the skin, and what to watch for while your baby uses it. If your baby is on a medicine by mouth, give it exactly as prescribed, usually with or right after a feeding. Ask us what to do if your baby is sick, vomiting or not eating.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H764
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Watch the hemangioma. Taking a photo every week or two in the same light helps you and us see changes.
  • Protect it from rubbing, such as from diaper edges or clothing seams.
  • If the surface breaks open (an ulcer), keep it clean and covered with petroleum jelly and a non-stick bandage, and call us.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H765
Stop and call if		The hemangioma breaks open, bleeds, crusts, or seems painful.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H766
Stop and call if		It is growing quickly, or is near the eye and is pushing on or partly closing the eye.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H767
Stop and call if		You notice 5 or more hemangiomas on your baby's skin.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H768
Stop and call if		Noisy breathing, a hoarse cry, or trouble feeding, especially with a hemangioma on the chin, jaw or neck.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H769
Stop and call if		On medicine: your baby is very sleepy, sweaty, cold or clammy, wheezing, or not eating (call right away).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: Krowchuk DP et al. Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Infantile Hemangiomas. Pediatrics 2019 (American Academy of Pediatrics) · American Academy of Pediatrics, HealthyChildren.org, "Hemangiomas" (patient education) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Hemangioma" (patient education)
