Molluscum contagiosum peds-molluscum

Patient sees: Molluscum in children · Clinician summary: What molluscum is, how long it lasts, preventing spread at home, and treatment choices to discuss.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H770
Section		What it is

Molluscum contagiosum (mo-LUS-kum con-tay-jee-OH-sum) is a common skin infection caused by a virus. It makes small, smooth, skin-colored or pink bumps. Many have a tiny dimple in the center. The bumps can show up anywhere, often on the trunk, arms, legs and in skin folds.

Molluscum is harmless and usually does not hurt. It is very common in young children and is more common in children who have eczema.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H771
Section		What to expect

Molluscum goes away on its own once the body's immune system recognizes the virus. This usually takes months, and sometimes 1 to 2 years. New bumps may appear while old ones fade.

As bumps start to clear, they may become red, swollen or crusted, and the skin around them may get itchy. This is often a sign the body is fighting the virus, not an infection.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H772
Section		Preventing spread
  • Try to keep your child from scratching or picking the bumps. This spreads them.
  • Keep nails short.
  • Don't share towels, washcloths or bath water with siblings.
  • Cover bumps with clothing or a bandage for swimming and contact sports when you can.
  • Treat any eczema around the bumps, since itching spreads molluscum.
  • Children with molluscum can go to school and child care.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H773
Section		Treatment choices

Because molluscum goes away by itself, waiting is a reasonable choice for many families. Treatment may help if there are many bumps, they are spreading, they are itchy or bothersome, or your child feels self-conscious.

Options include treatments done in our office and prescription medicines. Some treatments can cause blistering, soreness or a mark. We will talk with you about what fits your child. Please don't try to remove the bumps at home.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H774
Stop and call if		Bumps become very painful, warm, swollen, or drain pus, or your child has a fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H775
Stop and call if		Bumps on or near the eyelids, or your child has a red, irritated eye.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H776
Stop and call if		Bumps in the genital area (please tell us at your visit).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H777
Stop and call if		Blisters that are large or very painful after an office treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Molluscum contagiosum: diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · American Academy of Pediatrics, HealthyChildren.org, "Molluscum Contagiosum" (patient education) · Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, "Molluscum Contagiosum" (patient information)

Warts in children peds-warts

Patient sees: Warts in children · Clinician summary: Common and plantar warts in kids: what to expect, safe home treatment with salicylic acid, and preventing spread.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H778
Section		What it is

Warts are small, rough growths on the skin caused by a common virus (human papillomavirus, or HPV). They are very common in children. They often show up on the hands and fingers. Warts on the soles of the feet are called plantar warts and can be flat and tender.

Tiny black dots in a wart are small blood vessels, not seeds.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H779
Section		What to expect

Many warts in children go away on their own, though this can take months to a couple of years. Treatment can speed things up, but no treatment works every time, and warts can come back. Treatment usually takes patience and several weeks.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H780
Section		Treating warts at home

Salicylic acid wart medicine, sold without a prescription, is a common first step for warts on the hands and feet. Use it as directed on the label, and check the label for age limits.

  • Soak the wart in warm water for 5 to 10 minutes, then pat dry.
  • Gently file the top with an emery board or pumice stone that is used only for the wart.
  • Apply the medicine only to the wart, not the skin around it.
  • Keep going for several weeks. Stop for a few days if the skin gets sore or red.

Do not use wart medicine on the face, genitals or broken skin. Ask us first if your child has diabetes or poor circulation.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H781
Section		Preventing spread
  • Try to keep your child from picking or biting warts.
  • Don't share towels, nail files or pumice stones.
  • Have your child wear sandals or shower shoes in pool areas and locker rooms.
  • Keep feet dry and change sweaty socks.
  • Children with warts can go to school, sports and swimming.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H782
Section		Office treatments

If home treatment does not help, we may suggest freezing or other office treatments. Some can be uncomfortable for young children, and several visits are often needed. We will talk with you about what is right for your child.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H783
Stop and call if		A wart is painful, bleeding, changing quickly, or looks different from your child's other warts.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H784
Stop and call if		Redness, swelling, pus or pain around a wart you are treating.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H785
Stop and call if		Warts on the face or in the genital area (please don't treat these at home; tell us).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H786
Stop and call if		Warts keep spreading or don't improve after 2 to 3 months of home treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Warts: diagnosis and treatment" and "Warts: tips for managing" (patient education) · American Academy of Pediatrics, HealthyChildren.org, "Warts" (patient education)

Head lice peds-head-lice

Patient sees: Getting rid of head lice · Clinician summary: How lice spread, treatment and combing, checking the family, and what household cleaning is (and isn't) needed.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H787
Section		What it is

Head lice are tiny insects that live on the scalp and lay eggs (nits) on the hair close to the scalp. They are common in school-age children. Lice are not a sign of being dirty, and they do not spread disease.

Lice cannot jump or fly. They spread mostly by direct head-to-head contact, and less often by sharing hats, brushes or pillows. Pets do not spread head lice.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H788
Section		Treatment

There are several lice treatments, some sold without a prescription and some by prescription. Ask us which treatment is right for your child's age and weight.

  • Use the product exactly as directed on the label or as prescribed. Do not leave it on longer or use more than directed.
  • Many products need a second treatment about a week later to kill newly hatched lice. Follow the label or our directions.
  • Some products work best on hair that has not been washed with conditioner first. Check the label.
  • Never use gasoline, kerosene, or pet lice products on a child.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H789
Section		Combing

Combing helps whatever treatment you use.

  • Wet the hair and add conditioner to make combing easier (after treatment, unless the label says otherwise).
  • Use a fine-tooth nit comb. Comb small sections from the scalp to the ends.
  • Wipe the comb on a paper towel after each stroke.
  • Repeat every 2 to 3 days for about 2 weeks, until you find no live lice.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H790
Section		Family and home
  • Check everyone in the household. Treat only those who have live lice, at the same time.
  • Wash bedding, hats and clothes used in the last 2 days in hot water and dry them on high heat.
  • Items you can't wash can be sealed in a plastic bag for 2 weeks.
  • Soak combs and brushes in hot water for 5 to 10 minutes.
  • Vacuum floors and furniture. Lice sprays for the home are not needed and can be harmful.

Your child can usually stay in school. Ask the school about its policy.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H791
Stop and call if		You still see live, moving lice after finishing treatment as directed.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H792
Stop and call if		The scalp is red, swollen, oozing or crusted, or your child has swollen glands in the neck.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H793
Stop and call if		Burning, a rash or other skin reaction after using a lice product.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H794
Stop and call if		Your child is a baby or toddler, or has asthma or other health problems, and you are not sure which treatment is safe.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: Nolt D et al. Head Lice. Clinical Report, Pediatrics 2022 (American Academy of Pediatrics) · Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, "Head Lice: Treatment" (patient information) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Head lice: diagnosis and treatment" (patient education)

Scabies in children and the household peds-scabies

Patient sees: Treating scabies in your child and family · Clinician summary: What scabies is, treating everyone at the same time, cleaning bedding and clothes, and why itch lasts after treatment.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H795
Section		What it is

Scabies is a very itchy rash caused by tiny mites that burrow into the top layer of skin. The itch is often worst at night. In older children it is common between the fingers, on the wrists, waist, and in the armpits and groin. In babies and young children it can also appear on the palms, soles, scalp and face.

Scabies spreads through close skin-to-skin contact, so it often passes between family members. It is not a sign of being dirty. Scabies from people does not come from pets.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H796
Section		Treating everyone

We will prescribe a medicine for your child. Use it exactly as prescribed.

  • Everyone in the home and other close contacts (such as caregivers) should be treated at the same time, even if they don't itch. Otherwise scabies passes back and forth.
  • Most creams go on all of the skin from the neck down, including between fingers and toes, under nails, and in skin folds.
  • For babies and young children, we may ask you to include the scalp, face and neck, avoiding the eyes and mouth.
  • Re-apply to hands if they are washed before the medicine should be rinsed off.
  • A second treatment is often needed. Follow our directions.

Ask us before treating a baby, or anyone who is pregnant or breastfeeding.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H797
Section		Cleaning the home

On the day of treatment:

  • Wash bedding, towels and clothing used in the past 3 days in hot water and dry on high heat.
  • Seal items that can't be washed (such as stuffed toys) in a plastic bag for at least 3 days.
  • Vacuum floors and furniture.

Sprays and fumigating the home are not needed.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H798
Section		What to expect

Itching often continues for 2 to 4 weeks after successful treatment. This does not mean the treatment failed. Moisturizers and the itch remedies we suggest can help. Keep nails short to prevent scratching sores.

Children can usually return to school or child care the day after treatment.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H799
Stop and call if		New burrows or new itchy bumps appear more than 2 to 4 weeks after treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H800
Stop and call if		Sores that are crusted, oozing, have yellow crusts or pus, or spreading redness (possible infection).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H801
Stop and call if		Thick, crusted rash, especially on the hands or feet.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H802
Stop and call if		Itch keeps your child from sleeping despite treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, "Scabies: Treatment" and "Scabies: Prevention and Control" (patient information) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Scabies: diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · American Academy of Pediatrics, HealthyChildren.org, "Scabies" (patient education)

Impetigo peds-impetigo

Patient sees: Caring for impetigo · Clinician summary: What impetigo is, cleaning and using prescribed antibiotics, preventing spread, and returning to school.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H803
Section		What it is

Impetigo (im-peh-TIE-go) is a common skin infection caused by bacteria. It often starts as red sores that break open and form honey-colored crusts, often around the nose and mouth. Some children get fluid-filled blisters instead.

Impetigo is contagious. It spreads by touching the sores, and by sharing towels or clothing. It often starts in skin that is already broken, such as a scrape, bug bite or eczema.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H804
Section		Treatment

We usually treat impetigo with an antibiotic ointment, or with antibiotic medicine by mouth if there are many sores. Use it exactly as prescribed, and finish the full course even if the sores look better.

The sores usually start to improve within a few days of treatment.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H805
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Gently wash the sores with soap and water once or twice a day, and pat dry with a clean towel.
  • Soaking crusts with a warm, wet cloth can help them come off gently. Don't scrub.
  • Apply any prescribed ointment after washing.
  • Cover sores loosely with gauze or a bandage if your child is likely to touch them.
  • Wash your hands before and after caring for the sores.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H806
Section		Preventing spread
  • Keep your child's nails short and discourage scratching.
  • Use a separate towel for your child, and wash towels, washcloths, sheets and clothing in hot water.
  • Clean scrapes and bug bites and keep eczema treated, since impetigo starts more easily in broken skin.

Most children can return to school or child care after at least 24 hours on antibiotics, if the sores are covered. Check with us and your child's school.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H807
Stop and call if		Sores are spreading, or not better after 3 days of treatment.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H808
Stop and call if		Fever, or redness, warmth or swelling spreading around the sores.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H809
Stop and call if		Large blisters, or sores that are deep or very painful.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H810
Stop and call if		In the weeks after: puffy eyes or face, or dark, cola-colored urine (call promptly).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Impetigo: diagnosis and treatment" (patient education) · American Academy of Pediatrics, HealthyChildren.org, "Impetigo" (patient education) · Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, "Impetigo: All You Need to Know" (patient information)

Hand, foot and mouth disease peds-hfmd

Patient sees: Hand, foot and mouth disease · Clinician summary: What to expect with HFMD, comfort care and fluids, avoiding spread, and signs of dehydration.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H811
Section		What it is

Hand, foot and mouth disease is a common illness caused by a virus. It is most common in children under 5, but older children and adults can get it.

It often starts with fever, sore throat, tiredness and poor appetite. A day or two later, painful sores appear in the mouth, and a rash of small red spots or blisters shows up on the hands and feet. The rash can also appear on the buttocks, legs and around the mouth.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H812
Section		What to expect

Most children get better on their own in 7 to 10 days. Antibiotics do not help because it is caused by a virus.

The biggest concern is drinking enough, because mouth sores can make swallowing painful. A few weeks after the illness, some children's fingernails or toenails peel or fall off. This is harmless, and the nails grow back.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H813
Section		Comfort care at home
  • Offer fluids often: water, milk, or cold drinks. Ice pops can soothe the mouth.
  • Soft, cool foods such as yogurt, applesauce or pudding are easier to eat.
  • Avoid salty, spicy and acidic foods and drinks, such as orange juice, which can sting.
  • For pain or fever, ask us or your child's doctor which pain reliever and dose is right for your child's age and weight. Use it as directed on the label.
  • Never give aspirin to children or teens.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H814
Section		Preventing spread
  • Wash hands often with soap and water, especially after diaper changes and using the toilet. The virus can stay in stool for weeks.
  • Don't share cups, utensils or towels.
  • Clean toys and surfaces that are touched often.
  • Keep your child home while they have a fever or are drooling from mouth sores. Ask your child's school or child care when they can return.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H815
Stop and call if		Signs of dehydration: fewer wet diapers or trips to pee, dry mouth, no tears, or unusual sleepiness.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H816
Stop and call if		Your child refuses to drink, or can't keep fluids down.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H817
Stop and call if		Fever lasting more than 3 days, or a baby younger than 3 months with any fever of 100.4 F (38 C) or higher.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H818
Stop and call if		Stiff neck, severe headache, confusion, unusual weakness, trouble walking, or a seizure (seek care right away).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H819
Stop and call if		Symptoms are not better after 10 days.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, "Hand, Foot, and Mouth Disease" (patient information) · American Academy of Pediatrics, HealthyChildren.org, "Hand, Foot, and Mouth Disease" (patient education)

Scalp ringworm (tinea capitis) peds-tinea-capitis

Patient sees: Scalp ringworm in children · Clinician summary: Why scalp ringworm needs medicine by mouth, using medicated shampoo, stopping spread at home, and pet checks.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H820
Section		What it is

Scalp ringworm (tinea capitis) is an infection of the scalp and hair caused by a fungus, not a worm. It can cause scaly patches, broken hairs, bald spots, black dots where hairs broke off, or bumps. Glands in the neck may be swollen.

It is most common in young children. It spreads by contact with an infected person or pet, and by sharing combs, brushes, hats, pillows and towels.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H821
Section		Treatment

Creams alone do not cure scalp ringworm, because the fungus lives deep in the hair roots. Your child will need an antifungal medicine by mouth.

  • Give the medicine exactly as prescribed, for the full time we prescribed, usually several weeks or more.
  • Some of these medicines are absorbed better when taken with food. Follow our directions.
  • Do not stop early, even if the scalp looks better.
  • We may want to check your child's scalp again before stopping.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H822
Section		Medicated shampoo

We may also recommend a medicated shampoo, such as one with selenium sulfide or ketoconazole. The shampoo does not cure the infection but helps stop it from spreading. Use it as directed on the label or as we told you. We may suggest that other people in the household use it too.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H823
Section		Preventing spread
  • Don't share combs, brushes, hats, hair ties, pillows or towels.
  • Wash combs and brushes in hot, soapy water, or replace them.
  • Wash pillowcases, hats and towels in hot water.
  • Check other children in the home for scaly patches or hair loss, and tell us if you see any.
  • If a pet has patches of hair loss, have it checked by a vet.

Children can usually go to school once treatment has started.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H824
Section		What to expect

Hair usually grows back after the infection clears, but this can take several months. Rarely, a severe infection can leave a bald spot, which is one reason early treatment matters.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H825
Stop and call if		A painful, swollen, soft or oozing lump on the scalp.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H826
Stop and call if		A new itchy rash elsewhere on the body after starting the medicine.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H827
Stop and call if		On medicine: vomiting, a new rash, yellow skin or eyes, dark urine, or your child seems unwell.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H828
Stop and call if		The scalp is not improving after several weeks on the medicine, or hair loss is spreading.☐ Approve

Birthmarks: overview (congenital moles, port-wine stains, cafe-au-lait spots) peds-birthmarks

Patient sees: Your child's birthmark · Clinician summary: An overview of congenital moles, port-wine stains and cafe-au-lait spots: what they are, what to watch, and why some need follow-up.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H829
Section		About birthmarks

Birthmarks are marks on the skin that are present at birth or appear soon after. Most are harmless and need no treatment. Some types need regular checks or can be treated if they cause problems or bother your child. This handout covers three common types. We will tell you which one your child has.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H830
Section		Congenital moles

A congenital mole (also called a congenital nevus) is a brown or black mole present at birth or in the first months of life. It grows in proportion to your child, may darken, become raised, or grow hair over time. These changes are usually normal.

Most small and medium moles cause no problems. Large moles need closer follow-up with us.

  • Take photos now and then to track changes.
  • Protect the mole from sun with clothing and sunscreen (from 6 months of age).
  • Tell us about a new lump, a sore that doesn't heal, bleeding, or a fast change.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H831
Section		Port-wine stains

A port-wine stain is a flat, pink, red or purple mark caused by extra small blood vessels in the skin. It is present at birth, grows with your child, and does not go away on its own. Over the years it may darken and become thicker or bumpy.

Laser treatment can lighten many port-wine stains. Starting earlier often works better, so we may discuss it early.

A port-wine stain on the forehead or around the eye needs eye checks and sometimes other tests, because it can occasionally be linked to eye or brain problems. We will guide you.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H832
Section		Cafe-au-lait spots

Cafe-au-lait spots are flat, light brown patches, named for the color of coffee with milk. They are common, and having one or a few is normal. They grow with your child and do not turn into cancer.

If a child has 6 or more spots, or freckles in the armpits or groin, we may want to check for a genetic condition called neurofibromatosis. Let us know if you notice new spots.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H833
Stop and call if		A mole that changes quickly, or develops a lump, a sore that won't heal, bleeding, or itch.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H834
Stop and call if		A birthmark that breaks open, bleeds, or becomes painful.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H835
Stop and call if		A port-wine stain on the forehead or eyelid, with eye redness, a cloudy or enlarged eye, or a seizure (seek care promptly).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H836
Stop and call if		You count 6 or more cafe-au-lait spots, or notice freckles in the armpits or groin.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H837
Stop and call if		A birthmark is affecting your child's eye, feeding or breathing.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Newborn skin: milia, baby acne, erythema toxicum peds-newborn-skin

Patient sees: Your newborn's skin · Clinician summary: Common harmless newborn rashes (milia, baby acne, erythema toxicum), gentle skin care, and fever warning signs.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H838
Section		Common and harmless

Newborn skin changes a lot in the first weeks. Many spots and rashes are normal and go away without treatment. Peeling skin in the first weeks, especially on the hands and feet, is also normal.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H839
Section		Milia

Milia are tiny, firm, white or yellow bumps, often on the nose, cheeks and chin. They are small bumps of trapped skin protein (keratin). When they appear in the mouth or on the gums, they are called Epstein pearls.

Milia usually go away on their own in the first few weeks to months. Don't squeeze or scrub them.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H840
Section		Baby acne

Baby acne (neonatal acne) is small red or white bumps on the cheeks, forehead and nose. It often appears at around 2 to 4 weeks of age and is linked to normal hormones after birth.

Baby acne usually clears on its own within a few months.

  • Wash your baby's face once a day with lukewarm water, or a mild fragrance-free baby cleanser.
  • Pat dry. Don't scrub or squeeze.
  • Don't use oils, lotions or adult acne products on the face unless we recommend them.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H841
Section		Erythema toxicum

Despite the name, erythema toxicum (air-ih-THEE-muh TOX-ih-kum) is not toxic and is not an infection. It is red blotches with small yellow or white bumps in the center. It usually appears in the first few days of life, can come and go, and moves to different places on the body.

It does not bother babies and usually goes away within 1 to 2 weeks, with no treatment needed.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H842
Section		Gentle newborn skin care
  • Bathe with plain lukewarm water or a mild, fragrance-free baby cleanser.
  • Skip perfumes, powders and scented lotions.
  • Wash new clothes and bedding before use with a fragrance-free detergent.
  • Keep your baby out of direct sun. Use shade and light clothing.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H843
Stop and call if		Your baby is younger than 3 months and has a temperature of 100.4 F (38 C) or higher: call right away.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H844
Stop and call if		Blisters, pus-filled bumps, crusting, or skin that looks raw or is peeling in sheets.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H845
Stop and call if		Your baby is unusually sleepy, hard to wake, not feeding well, or has yellow skin or eyes.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H846
Stop and call if		Baby acne lasts beyond 3 to 4 months, has blackheads or deep bumps, or leaves marks.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H847
Stop and call if		A rash that does not fit what is described here, or you are worried.☐ Approve

Notes:

Sun protection for babies and children peds-sun-protection

Patient sees: Protecting your child from the sun · Clinician summary: Sun safety by age: shade and clothing for babies under 6 months, sunscreen from 6 months, and sunburn care.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H848
Section		Why it matters

Children's skin burns easily, and sunburns in childhood raise the risk of skin cancer later in life. Good sun habits started early are easier to keep. Sun protection matters for children of every skin color, and on cloudy days too.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H849
Section		Babies under 6 months

Keep babies younger than 6 months out of direct sunlight.

  • Use shade: a tree, umbrella, or stroller canopy.
  • Dress your baby in lightweight long sleeves and long pants, and a wide-brimmed hat.
  • Avoid the strongest sun, about 10 a.m. to 4 p.m., when you can.
  • Watch for overheating, and offer feedings often in hot weather.

Sunscreen is not recommended for babies this young in most cases. If shade and clothing aren't possible, ask us or your baby's doctor about using a small amount on small areas, such as the face and backs of the hands.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H850
Section		Children 6 months and older
  • Use a broad-spectrum sunscreen, SPF 30 or higher, that is water-resistant.
  • Apply it about 15 minutes before going outside, and use enough to cover all skin that clothing won't cover, including ears, nose, neck and the tops of feet.
  • Reapply at least every 2 hours, and after swimming or sweating.
  • Mineral sunscreens (zinc oxide or titanium dioxide) are often gentler on sensitive skin.
  • Don't spray sunscreen directly on the face. Spray it into your hands first, and avoid breathing it in.
  • Test a new sunscreen on a small area first if your child has sensitive skin.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H851
Section		Beyond sunscreen
  • Seek shade, especially from about 10 a.m. to 4 p.m.
  • Choose wide-brimmed hats and clothing that covers the skin. Clothing labeled UPF gives extra protection.
  • Use sunglasses that block 100% of UV rays.
  • Take extra care near water, sand and snow, which reflect the sun.
  • Indoor tanning is not safe at any age.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H852
Section		If your child gets a sunburn
  • Get out of the sun and cool the skin with cool baths or cool, damp cloths.
  • Use a gentle, fragrance-free moisturizer.
  • Offer extra fluids.
  • Ask us or your child's doctor before giving any pain reliever, and use it as directed on the label for your child's age and weight.
  • Don't pop blisters.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Acne in preteens and teens peds-teen-acne

Patient sees: Acne: a guide for teens and parents · Clinician summary: Why acne happens, a simple daily routine with benzoyl peroxide and adapalene, what to expect, and when to come back.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H859
Section		What it is

Acne is very common in preteens and teens. During puberty, hormones make the skin produce more oil. Oil and dead skin cells clog pores, and bacteria in the pores cause redness and swelling. This leads to blackheads, whiteheads, red bumps and sometimes deeper, painful bumps.

Acne is not caused by dirt, and it is not anyone's fault. Scrubbing harder does not help and can make it worse.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H860
Section		A simple daily routine
  • Wash the face twice a day and after sweating with a gentle cleanser and fingertips. No scrubs or rough washcloths.
  • Use acne treatments on the whole area where acne appears, not just on spots, to help prevent new ones.
  • Benzoyl peroxide (a wash or gel) kills acne bacteria. It can bleach towels, pillowcases and clothing, so use white towels.
  • Adapalene gel unclogs pores. It is sold without a prescription for ages 12 and up. Use a pea-sized amount for the whole face at night.
  • Use an oil-free, fragrance-free moisturizer if skin gets dry.
  • Use a broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher sunscreen, since some treatments make skin burn more easily.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H861
Section		What to expect

Acne treatments work slowly. Most people see improvement in 8 to 12 weeks of daily use. Some dryness, redness or peeling in the first few weeks is common. Using less often for a while, or adding moisturizer, usually helps.

Keep using the routine even when skin looks better. Acne often comes back when treatment stops.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H862
Section		Helpful tips
  • Try not to pick, pop or squeeze pimples. This can lead to dark marks and scars.
  • Choose makeup, sunscreen and hair products labeled oil-free or non-comedogenic (won't clog pores).
  • Wash sports gear, helmets and pads that rub on the skin, and shower soon after sports.
  • Anyone who is pregnant or could become pregnant should talk with us before using adapalene or other retinoids.

Acne can affect how a teen feels about themselves. If acne is affecting mood, sleep or school, let us know. Prescription treatments can help.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H863
Step · Morning		Benzoyl peroxide wash (OTC; picker search “benzoyl peroxide wash”)
Lather onto damp skin where acne appears, leave on 1 to 2 minutes, then rinse. Can bleach fabric.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H864
Step · Night		Gentle cleanser (OTC; picker search “gentle cleanser”)
Wash with lukewarm water and fingertips, rinse and pat dry.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H865
Step · Night		Adapalene gel (OTC; picker search “adapalene gel”)
Ages 12 and up, or as we direct: a pea-sized amount for the whole face on dry skin at night. Start every other night if skin gets dry.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H866
Step · Morning and night		Oil-free moisturizer (OTC; picker search “oil-free moisturizer”)
A fragrance-free, non-comedogenic moisturizer after treatment, more often if skin feels dry or tight.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H867

☐ Edit

Alopecia areata in children peds-alopecia-areata

Patient sees: Alopecia areata in children · Clinician summary: What alopecia areata is, what to expect with regrowth, treatment options to discuss, and emotional support.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H872
Section		What it is

Alopecia areata (al-oh-PEE-shuh air-ee-AH-tuh) causes hair to fall out, usually in smooth, round patches on the scalp. It can also affect the eyebrows, eyelashes or other body hair. Some children have small dents (pits) in their nails.

It happens when the body's immune system attacks the hair roots by mistake. The hair roots are not destroyed, so hair can grow back. Alopecia areata is not contagious, and it is not caused by anything you or your child did.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H873
Section		What to expect

Alopecia areata is hard to predict. In many children with a few small patches, hair grows back within months to a year, even without treatment. New patches can appear while others regrow, and hair loss can come back in the future.

Regrowing hair is sometimes white or fine at first, and usually returns to its normal color over time. Some children have more widespread hair loss.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H874
Section		Treatment options

There is no cure, but treatment can help hair regrow. The choice depends on your child's age and how much hair is affected. Options include:

  • Prescription creams, ointments or solutions applied to the patches
  • Injections into the patches for older children and teens who can tolerate them
  • For widespread hair loss, other prescription treatments, including newer medicines by mouth for some teens

Use any treatment exactly as prescribed. It can take several months to see regrowth.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H875
Section		Supporting your child

Hair loss can be hard for children and teens. It can help to:

  • Talk openly with your child and let them decide how much to share with others.
  • Let teachers know, so they can watch for teasing.
  • Offer hats, scarves, headbands or wigs if your child wants them.
  • Protect bare scalp from the sun with a hat or sunscreen.
  • Connect with support groups for families living with alopecia areata.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H876
Stop and call if		Hair loss is spreading quickly, or includes most of the scalp, eyebrows or eyelashes.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H877
Stop and call if		The scalp in the bald patches is red, scaly, itchy, painful or has bumps (this may be a different condition).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H878
Stop and call if		Your child seems sad, anxious, withdrawn, or is being teased or avoiding school.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H879
Stop and call if		Side effects from a treatment, such as skin thinning, irritation, or new symptoms after starting a medicine.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: American Academy of Dermatology, "Alopecia areata: diagnosis and treatment" and "Alopecia areata: tips for managing" (patient education) · National Alopecia Areata Foundation, information for parents of children with alopecia areata · American Academy of Pediatrics, HealthyChildren.org, "Hair Loss in Children" (patient education)

Insect bites and papular urticaria peds-insect-bites

Patient sees: Insect bites and bite reactions in children · Clinician summary: Why some children get long-lasting itchy bumps from bites, itch relief, safe insect repellents by age, and finding the source.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H880
Section		What it is

Some children react strongly to bites from mosquitoes, fleas, bed bugs or other insects. This is called papular urticaria (PAP-yoo-ler er-tih-KAIR-ee-uh). It causes very itchy, red bumps, often in groups or lines, on the arms, legs and other uncovered skin.

The bumps can last for days to weeks. New bites can make old bumps flare again. It is most common in young children and is usually outgrown as the body gets used to the bites. It is not contagious.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H881
Section		Relieving the itch
  • Use cool compresses or a cool bath.
  • Calamine lotion can soothe itchy bumps.
  • A low-strength hydrocortisone cream, sold without a prescription, can help for short periods. Use it as directed on the label, and ask us first for a child under 2.
  • An allergy medicine (antihistamine) by mouth may help with itch. Ask us which one and what dose is right for your child's age and weight.
  • Keep nails short, and discourage scratching to prevent infection.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H882
Section		Preventing bites
  • Dress children in light long sleeves and long pants outdoors, and use netting over strollers.
  • Use screens on windows and doors.
  • Insect repellent: ask us which product and strength fits your child's age. Repellents with DEET should not be used on babies younger than 2 months. Do not use oil of lemon eucalyptus on children under 3.
  • Put repellent on your hands first, then on your child. Keep it off the hands, eyes, mouth and broken skin. Wash it off at the end of the day.
  • Don't use products that combine sunscreen and repellent.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H883
Section		Finding the source

Finding and removing the source of bites is the best way to stop new bumps.

  • If you have pets, ask your vet about flea control, and wash pet bedding.
  • Vacuum carpets and furniture often.
  • Check mattress seams and bed frames for bed bugs or small dark spots.
  • Bites may come from places your child spends time, such as a relative's home or child care.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H884
Stop and call if		Bites become very red, warm, swollen, painful, or drain pus, or red streaks spread out (possible infection).☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H885
Stop and call if		Fever, joint pain, or a rash like a bull's-eye after a tick bite.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H886
Stop and call if		New bumps keep appearing for weeks despite your efforts, or itch keeps your child from sleeping.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H887
Stop and call if		A large area of swelling around a bite that keeps growing.☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Sources: American Academy of Pediatrics, HealthyChildren.org, "Choosing an Insect Repellent for Your Child" and "Insect Bites and Stings" (patient education) · Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, "Preventing Mosquito Bites" (patient information; repellent use in children) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Bug bites and stings: when to see a dermatologist" (patient education)

Keratosis pilaris in children peds-keratosis-pilaris

Patient sees: Keratosis pilaris in children · Clinician summary: What keratosis pilaris is, gentle skin care and moisturizing, smoothing lotions for older children, and what to expect.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H888
Section		What it is

Keratosis pilaris (ker-uh-TOE-sis pih-LAIR-iss) causes small, rough bumps that can feel like sandpaper. The bumps are often on the backs of the upper arms, the thighs and the buttocks. In young children, it can also appear on the cheeks, sometimes with redness.

The bumps are plugs of keratin, a protein in skin, around hair follicles. Keratosis pilaris is harmless, very common, and often runs in families. It is more common in children with dry skin or eczema. It is not contagious.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H889
Section		What to expect

Keratosis pilaris often gets milder as children grow, though it may last into adulthood. It is often worse in winter when skin is dry and better in summer. Treatment can make the skin feel smoother, but the bumps usually come back if care stops.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H890
Section		Caring for it at home
  • Give short baths or showers in lukewarm water. Use a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser.
  • Pat dry, then put on a fragrance-free moisturizer within a few minutes, while skin is still a little damp.
  • Moisturize every day, even when the skin looks better.
  • Don't scrub hard or pick the bumps. This can cause redness, irritation and marks.
  • A humidifier can help in dry winter months.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H891
Section		Smoothing lotions

For older children, a moisturizer with urea, lactic acid or salicylic acid can help soften the bumps. These can sting, especially on irritated or broken skin. Ask us before using them on a young child or on the face, and use them as directed on the label. If we prescribe a cream, use it as prescribed.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H892
Step · Morning and night		Moisturizer (every day) (OTC; picker search “fragrance-free moisturizer”)
After the bath on slightly damp skin, and again once a day. Fragrance-free cream or lotion.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H893
Step · As directed		Smoothing lotion (if we recommend it) (OTC; picker search “urea lotion”)
Only as we direct, on the rough areas. Skip it if it stings or the skin is irritated.		☐ Approve

Notes:

Using your treatment (17)

Start here: what the drafter was least sure of
  1. Bleach baths: about 1/2 cup regular-strength plain bleach per full tub, 1/4 cup per half tub, 1 teaspoon per gallon for a baby tub; 10 minutes, rinse after; often 2 to 3 times a week; ask if the bottle says concentrated. Check against clinic practice.
  2. 5-FU: approximate phases (weeks 1 to 2, peak weeks 2 to 4, 1 to 3 weeks to heal); pet-toxicity warning; a whole-body-symptom stop rule reflecting the DPD-deficiency label warning.
  3. Imiquimod: apply at bedtime, wash off in the morning 'unless we told you otherwise'; skin color change 'can be permanent'.
  4. Calcineurin inhibitors: boxed warning framed as mainly animal high-dose data plus case reports, with reassuring large human studies since; pediatric age limits and 'avoid continuous long-term use' left out ('follow the schedule we gave you' keeps proactive use possible). Please confirm the framing.
  5. Oral antibiotics: upright at least 30 minutes; separate from minerals and antacids by 'a few hours'; probiotics not needed; birth-control interaction deliberately omitted; stop rules for severe headache with vision change, and minocycline pigmentation.
  6. Spironolactone: potassium 'usually not a problem for healthy young people', labs only if ordered; no potassium supplements or salt substitutes; 'a breast lump' stop rule; breastfeeding only 'tell us'.
  7. Minoxidil: toxic to cats (uncited); shedding the first few weeks to 2 months; results at 4 to 6 months, full effect about a year; one line on prescribed pills.
  8. Hydroquinone: 'a few months then a break' without exact cycles; lightening expected in 1 to 3 months, stop rule if none by about 3 months; warning against lighteners bought online.
  9. Biologics: check before any vaccine, live vaccines usually avoided; inject about 2 inches from the belly button; ask before a dose during an infection.
  10. Sun protection after skin cancer: nicotinamide deliberately omitted (mixed evidence); vitamin D only as 'food or supplements are safer than sun; ask us'. Sunscreen: about a teaspoon for face and neck; 3-year shelf life if undated.
  11. Sources less certain: the FDA consumer warning about pets and topical fluorouracil; Eisen et al. 2021 AAD actinic keratosis guideline (cited by year).

Topical steroids: using them safely tx-topical-steroids

Patient sees: Using your steroid cream or ointment safely · Clinician summary: Fingertip units, where to be careful (face, folds), how long to use, tapering, and side effects to watch for.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H897
Section		What it is

Topical steroids (corticosteroids) are creams, ointments, lotions and solutions that calm redness, swelling and itch. They come in many strengths, from mild to very strong. We chose the strength for the part of your body being treated.

Used the way we showed you, they are safe and work well. Using too little, or stopping too soon, is a common reason a rash doesn't get better.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H898
Section		How much to use: the fingertip unit

A fingertip unit is the amount squeezed from the tube in a line from the tip of an adult's index finger to the first crease. One fingertip unit covers an area about the size of two adult palms (with the fingers together).

  • Rub a thin layer in gently until it disappears.
  • Put it only on the rash, not on clear skin, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Wash your hands afterward, unless you are treating your hands.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H899
Section		Face, skin folds and children

Some skin is thinner and absorbs more medicine: the face, eyelids, armpits, groin, under the breasts and the diaper area. Use only the product we prescribed for these areas.

  • Do not use a body-strength steroid on the face or folds unless we said to.
  • Keep it away from the eyes unless we prescribed it for the eyelids.
  • Don't cover treated skin with plastic, tight bandages or a diaper unless we told you to. Covering makes the medicine stronger.
  • For children, use exactly the amount and areas we discussed.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H900
Section		How long to use it

Use it as prescribed, usually until the rash is smooth and the itch is gone, and for no longer than the time we set. Stronger steroids are usually used for shorter periods.

If you have used a steroid every day for several weeks, don't stop suddenly unless we told you to. We may have you taper: use it less often (for example, every other day, then only a few days a week) before stopping. This helps keep the rash from bouncing back.

Keep using your moisturizer every day, even when the skin is clear.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H901
Section		Side effects to watch for

Most people have no problems. With too much use for too long, the skin can change:

  • Thin, shiny or fragile skin, or easy bruising
  • Stretch marks
  • Small visible blood vessels (spider veins)
  • Acne-like bumps or redness around the mouth on the face
  • Lighter skin where the steroid was used

These are much less likely when you use the right amount, in the right place, for the right length of time.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H902
Stop and call if		Thinning skin, stretch marks, easy bruising or spider veins where you use the steroid.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H903
Stop and call if		The rash is no better after 2 weeks, or comes back quickly every time you stop.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H904
Stop and call if		Signs of infection: yellow crusts, pus, spreading redness, warmth or fever.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H905
Stop and call if		Red bumps around the mouth or eyes, or burning when you try to stop using it on the face.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H906
Stop and call if		You need more refills than we planned, or you are unsure how much or how long to use it.☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Topical retinoids: getting started tx-topical-retinoids

Patient sees: Starting your retinoid cream or gel · Clinician summary: How to apply a topical retinoid, building up slowly, handling irritation, sun care and pregnancy cautions.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H907
Section		What it is

Retinoids are vitamin A medicines that you put on the skin. They unclog pores, help prevent new acne, and over time can even out skin tone and texture. Tretinoin, adapalene and tazarotene are common examples.

Retinoids work slowly. Most people see real improvement after 8 to 12 weeks of regular use.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H908
Section		How to use it
  • Use it at night, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Wash with a gentle cleanser and pat dry. Waiting until your skin is fully dry can reduce stinging.
  • Use a pea-sized amount for the whole face. Dot it on the forehead, cheeks and chin, then spread a thin layer.
  • Treat the whole area where acne appears, not just single spots.
  • Keep it away from the corners of the eyes, nose and mouth.
  • Put on a moisturizer afterward if your skin feels dry.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H909
Section		Starting slowly and handling irritation

Dryness, peeling, redness and mild stinging are common in the first few weeks. They usually ease as your skin adjusts. Some people also get a few extra breakouts at first.

  • Many people start every second or third night, then use it more often as their skin allows, unless we gave you a different plan.
  • If your skin gets very irritated, skip a night or two, use moisturizer, then start again less often.
  • Avoid scrubs, toners with alcohol, and other peeling products unless we said they are OK.
  • Don't wax areas where you use a retinoid. The skin can tear or peel.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H910
Section		Sun and other products

Retinoids can make your skin burn more easily. Use a broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher every morning.

Some retinoids are broken down by benzoyl peroxide. Unless your product is made to be used with it, or we told you otherwise, use benzoyl peroxide in the morning and the retinoid at night.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H911
Section		Pregnancy and breastfeeding

Retinoids are generally not used during pregnancy. If you are pregnant, trying to become pregnant, or breastfeeding, tell us before you start. If you become pregnant while using it, stop and let us know.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H912
Stop and call if		Redness, burning, swelling or peeling that is severe, or doesn't settle after a few days off the medicine.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H913
Stop and call if		You become pregnant, are planning a pregnancy, or are breastfeeding.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H914
Stop and call if		A bad sunburn on treated skin.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H915
Stop and call if		No improvement after about 12 weeks of regular use.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Sources: Reynolds RV et al. Guidelines of care for the management of acne vulgaris. J Am Acad Dermatol 2024 (AAD) · FDA labels: tretinoin, adapalene and tazarotene topical products (dosage and administration; pregnancy) · American Academy of Dermatology, "Acne: Tips for managing" (patient education)

Benzoyl peroxide: how to use it tx-benzoyl-peroxide

Patient sees: Using benzoyl peroxide · Clinician summary: Washes vs leave-on products, avoiding dryness, bleaching of fabric and hair, pairing with other acne medicines.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H916
Section		What it is

Benzoyl peroxide is an acne medicine that kills the bacteria involved in acne and helps unclog pores. Bacteria don't become resistant to it. That's why it is often used together with antibiotic creams or pills.

It comes as washes, gels, creams and lotions. Lower strengths often work about as well as higher ones and are less irritating.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H917
Section		How to use it

Wash or cleanser:

  • Lather onto damp skin and leave it on for 1 to 2 minutes before rinsing, so it has time to work.
  • It works well for the chest, back and shoulders.

Leave-on gel or cream:

  • Apply a thin layer to the whole area where acne appears, not just single spots.
  • Use it as directed on the label, or as we told you.

Results usually take 6 to 8 weeks or longer.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H918
Section		Dryness and irritation

Dryness, peeling and mild redness are common at first.

  • Start once a day. Use it more often only if your skin is comfortable.
  • Use a fragrance-free moisturizer.
  • If it stings or burns, use it less often or switch to a wash.

A small number of people are allergic to it. Signs are an itchy, red, swollen rash where you applied it. If that happens, stop and call us.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H919
Section		It bleaches fabric and hair

Benzoyl peroxide can leave white or orange spots on towels, sheets, pillowcases and clothes. It can also lighten hair, including eyebrows.

  • Use white towels and pillowcases.
  • Let leave-on products dry fully before you get dressed or go to bed.
  • Rinse washes off well, including the hairline.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H920
Section		Using it with other medicines

Some retinoids are broken down by benzoyl peroxide. Unless your product is made to be used with it, or we told you otherwise, use benzoyl peroxide in the morning and the retinoid at night.

Use a broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher every morning.

5-fluorouracil cream for sun damage tx-5-fluorouracil

Patient sees: Your 5-fluorouracil (5-FU) cream treatment · Clinician summary: How to apply 5-FU for precancers (actinic keratoses), the expected reaction phases, healing, pets and pregnancy.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H928
Section		What it is

5-fluorouracil (5-FU) is a cream that treats actinic keratoses. These are rough, scaly spots caused by sun damage that can sometimes turn into skin cancer. The cream also finds and treats damaged spots you can't see or feel yet.

The treated skin will look worse before it looks better. That reaction is expected and means the cream is working.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H929
Section		How to use it
  • Use it on the area and for the number of days or weeks we prescribed.
  • Wash and dry the skin, then rub in a thin layer with a fingertip or cotton swab.
  • Keep it away from the eyes, inside the nose and the mouth.
  • Wash your hands well right after, unless we told you to use gloves.
  • Don't cover the area with a bandage unless we told you to.
  • Avoid the sun and tanning beds during treatment. Wear a hat and stay in the shade.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H930
Section		What to expect: the reaction phases

Everyone reacts a little differently, but most people go through these phases:

  • First days: little change, maybe mild pinkness.
  • About week 1 to 2: redness, burning or itching, and the rough spots stand out.
  • About week 2 to 4: more redness, crusting, scabs, oozing or raw spots. It may be sore. This is often the peak.
  • After you stop: the skin usually heals over 1 to 3 weeks, and may stay pink for a few weeks more.

If the reaction is too uncomfortable, call us. We may change your plan.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H931
Section		Caring for your skin
  • Wash gently with a mild cleanser and lukewarm water.
  • Cool, damp cloths can ease burning.
  • Ask us before using any other creams on the area. After you finish, a plain moisturizer or petroleum jelly usually helps healing.
  • Don't pick at scabs.
  • Plan social events and photos for after you have healed.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H932
Section		Safety: pregnancy and pets

Do not use 5-FU if you are pregnant or could become pregnant. Tell us if you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy or breastfeeding.

This cream is very dangerous to dogs and cats, even in tiny amounts. Store the tube where pets can't reach it. Throw away used tissues and swabs safely. Don't let pets lick your treated skin or hands.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

☐ Edit
☐ Cut

☐ Cut

Imiquimod cream tx-imiquimod

Patient sees: Your imiquimod cream treatment · Clinician summary: How to apply imiquimod, the expected skin reaction, flu-like symptoms, rest breaks, and cautions for genital use.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H938
Section		What it is

Imiquimod is a cream that helps your immune system attack abnormal skin cells. It is used for some sun-damage spots (actinic keratoses), some shallow skin cancers, and warts on the genitals.

The treated skin usually gets red and irritated. That reaction is expected and often means the cream is working.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H939
Section		How to use it
  • Use it on the days and for the number of weeks we prescribed. More is not better.
  • Apply it at bedtime, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Wash and dry the area. Rub in a thin layer until it disappears.
  • Leave it on overnight, then wash it off with mild soap and water in the morning, or after the time we told you.
  • Don't cover it with a tight or airtight bandage unless we told you to.
  • Wash your hands before and after.
  • If it comes in single-use packets, throw away any leftover cream in an opened packet.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H940
Section		What to expect

Common reactions where you apply it:

  • Redness, swelling, itching or burning
  • Flaking, crusting, scabs or small open sores

Some people feel like they have the flu: tired, achy, headache or a low fever. Treated skin may also become lighter or darker, and this can be permanent.

If the reaction becomes too strong, call us. We may have you take a few days off (a rest period) before starting again. Don't stop or restart on your own without checking with us.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H941

  • Protect treated skin from the sun with clothing, a hat and shade.
  • Keep the cream away from the eyes, lips and inside the nose.
  • For genital warts: avoid sexual contact while the cream is on the skin. The cream can weaken condoms and diaphragms.
  • Tell us if you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy or breastfeeding.
  • Healing usually takes a few weeks after you finish. We will check the area to make sure the treatment worked.

Tacrolimus / pimecrolimus (topical calcineurin inhibitors) tx-calcineurin-inhibitors

Patient sees: Using tacrolimus ointment or pimecrolimus cream · Clinician summary: Non-steroid eczema creams: burning at first, sun care, and what the boxed warning means in plain words.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H947
Section		What it is

Tacrolimus ointment and pimecrolimus cream are non-steroid medicines that calm eczema and some other rashes. They are called topical calcineurin inhibitors.

They do not thin the skin the way steroids can. That makes them a good choice for the face, eyelids, neck and skin folds, and for longer-term use on areas that tend to flare.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H948
Section		How to use it
  • Apply a thin layer to the rash as prescribed, and rub it in gently.
  • Use it only on the areas we discussed.
  • Your plan may include using it on areas that often flare, even when they look clear. Follow the schedule we gave you.
  • Use your moisturizer every day. Ask us whether to put it on before or after.
  • Wash your hands afterward, unless you are treating your hands.
  • Don't use it on skin that looks infected.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H949
Section		Burning at first is common

Many people feel warmth, burning, stinging or itching where they apply it in the first days. This usually fades within about a week as the skin heals. Applying it to fully dry skin may help.

Some people get a red, flushed face if they drink alcohol while using these medicines.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H950
Section		The boxed warning, in plain words

The labels carry a boxed warning. It says that rare cases of cancer, including skin cancer and lymphoma (a cancer of the immune system), were reported in people using these medicines. It does not say the medicine caused them.

The warning was based mainly on animal studies with high doses and a small number of reports. Large studies of people since then have been reassuring, and dermatology guidelines continue to recommend these medicines. To use them safely:

  • Use them only where and as long as we prescribed.
  • Protect treated skin from the sun and avoid tanning beds.
  • Keep regular follow-up so we can check how it's going.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Wet wrap therapy tx-wet-wraps

Patient sees: Wet wrap therapy for eczema · Clinician summary: Step-by-step wet wraps for eczema flares: bathing, medicine and moisturizer, damp and dry layers, safety tips.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H955
Section		What it is

Wet wraps are a way to calm a bad eczema flare. After a bath, you put on medicine and moisturizer, then cover the skin with a damp layer of clothing or gauze and a dry layer on top. The wraps cool the skin, ease itch, help the skin hold water, and help the medicine work.

Wet wraps are usually used for a few days to a week or two during a flare, as we direct.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H956
Section		What you need
  • Your prescribed medicine and a thick, fragrance-free moisturizer
  • Damp layer: cotton pajamas, a long-sleeved shirt and leggings, tube socks for arms and legs, or gauze
  • Dry layer: a second, dry set of cotton pajamas or clothes that fits over the damp layer
  • A bowl of lukewarm water
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H957
Section		How to do it
  • Take a short, lukewarm bath or shower. Gently pat the skin until it is just damp.
  • Apply the medicine to the rash, exactly where and how we prescribed.
  • Apply moisturizer everywhere else.
  • Soak the damp layer in lukewarm water and wring it out until it's damp, not dripping.
  • Put on the damp layer, then the dry layer right over it.
  • Leave the wraps on for several hours or overnight, as we told you. Re-wet the damp layer if it dries out.
  • When you take the wraps off, put on moisturizer again.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H958
Section		Helpful tips
  • Keep the room warm, and use a blanket so you or your child don't get chilled.
  • For children, distraction helps: a movie, a story or a favorite toy.
  • Wraps make the medicine absorb more. Use only the medicine and amount we prescribed under the wraps.
  • Don't use wet wraps on skin that looks infected.
  • Wash the wraps after each use.
☐ Approve

A thick fragrance-free cream or ointment, all over, on damp skin after bathing and when the wraps come off.		☐ Approve

Dilute bleach baths tx-bleach-baths

Patient sees: How to take a bleach bath · Clinician summary: Dilute bleach baths for eczema with frequent infections: mixing safely, soaking, rinsing and moisturizing.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H964
Section		What it is

A bleach bath is a bath with a very small amount of household bleach mixed into the water. It is about as strong as a swimming pool. It can lower the number of bacteria on the skin and may help eczema that often gets infected.

Use bleach baths only if we recommended them, and as often as we told you. Many people take them 2 to 3 times a week.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H965
Section		Mixing the bath

Use plain, unscented household bleach. Do not use splash-less, scented or color-safe bleach. Use the amount we gave you.

A commonly used recipe for regular-strength bleach:

  • Full standard bathtub: about half a cup of bleach
  • Half-full bathtub: about a quarter cup
  • Baby bathtub: about 1 teaspoon per gallon of water

Some bleach is labeled concentrated and is stronger. Ask us how much to use if your bottle says concentrated.

Fill the tub with lukewarm water first, then pour in the bleach and stir before anyone gets in.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H966
Section		Taking the bath
  • Soak for about 10 minutes, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Keep the head out of the water. Keep bath water away from the eyes and mouth.
  • Rinse off with fresh lukewarm water, then gently pat the skin dry.
  • Right away, apply any prescribed medicine to the rash, and moisturizer everywhere else.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H967
Section		Safety tips
  • Never put undiluted bleach on the skin.
  • Never mix bleach with other cleaners, especially ones with ammonia. This makes a dangerous gas.
  • Always stay with children in the bath.
  • Store bleach out of reach of children.
  • Very raw or cracked skin may sting. Tell us if it hurts.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Medicated (antifungal) shampoo tx-antifungal-shampoo

Patient sees: Using your medicated shampoo · Clinician summary: How to use antifungal shampoo on the scalp, face, beard or body: contact time, maintenance, and avoiding dryness.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H973
Section		What it is

Antifungal shampoos lower the amount of yeast on the skin. They are used for dandruff and seborrheic dermatitis (flaky, red, itchy skin on the scalp, face or chest). They are also used as a body wash for tinea versicolor (light or dark patches on the chest and back), and sometimes to help stop the spread of scalp ringworm.

Common ingredients include ketoconazole, selenium sulfide, ciclopirox and zinc pyrithione.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H974
Section		How to use it

The key is letting the shampoo sit before you rinse it off.

  • Wet your hair and skin.
  • Massage the shampoo into the scalp, not just the hair. It can also go on a beard, eyebrows, sides of the nose, behind the ears or the chest if we told you to.
  • Leave it on for about 5 minutes, or as long as the label or we said.
  • Rinse well.

For tinea versicolor, spread it over the patches and the skin around them, leave it on as directed, then rinse.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H975
Section		How often

Use it as often as we prescribed or as the label directs. Often this is a few times a week until things clear up, then about once a week to keep it from coming back.

Seborrheic dermatitis and tinea versicolor tend to come back. A regular maintenance wash is the best way to keep them quiet.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H976
Section		Helpful tips
  • Medicated shampoos can dry the hair. Use your regular conditioner afterward.
  • You can use your regular shampoo on other days.
  • Keep it out of your eyes. If it gets in, rinse with water.
  • Selenium sulfide can discolor light, gray or color-treated hair. Rinse very well.
  • Mild itching or irritation can happen. Tell us if it doesn't settle.
☐ Approve

Oral antibiotics for acne or rosacea tx-oral-antibiotics-acne-rosacea

Patient sees: Taking an antibiotic pill for acne or rosacea · Clinician summary: Tetracycline-class antibiotics: how to take them (water, stay upright), sun sensitivity, what to separate them from, side effects.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H981
Section		What it is

Antibiotics such as doxycycline and minocycline are used for acne and rosacea. They calm redness and swelling, and lower the bacteria involved in acne.

They are usually used for a limited time, often a few months, together with creams or gels. When your skin is better, we will usually stop the pill and keep you on the creams to keep it clear.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H982
Section		How to take it
  • Take it as prescribed. Don't take extra or skip doses.
  • Swallow it with a full glass of water.
  • Stay upright (sitting or standing) for at least 30 minutes after. Don't take it right before bed. This helps keep the pill from irritating your food pipe.
  • If it upsets your stomach, taking it with food may help, unless we told you otherwise.
  • Take it a few hours apart from antacids, calcium, iron, magnesium, zinc and multivitamins. These can stop it from being absorbed. Ask your pharmacist about your own timing.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H983
Section		Sun sensitivity

These antibiotics, especially doxycycline, can make you sunburn much more easily, even with short time outdoors.

  • Use broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher every day.
  • Wear a hat and clothing that covers your skin.
  • Seek shade, and avoid tanning beds.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H984
Section		Other things to know
  • Most people improve slowly over 6 to 12 weeks.
  • Mild stomach upset is common.
  • Some people get a vaginal yeast infection.
  • You do not need to take probiotics with this medicine.
  • Do not take these antibiotics if you are pregnant. Tell us right away if you become pregnant.
  • They are not usually used in children under 8, because they can stain growing teeth.
  • Tell us and your pharmacist about all other medicines you take.
☐ Approve

Spironolactone for acne tx-spironolactone

Patient sees: Taking spironolactone for acne · Clinician summary: How spironolactone helps hormonal acne, timeline, common side effects, no use in pregnancy, potassium and labs.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H991
Section		What it is

Spironolactone is a pill first used for blood pressure and fluid. In lower amounts it is also used to treat acne in women. It blocks the effects of hormones called androgens. Androgens make oil glands more active, which can lead to acne along the jaw, chin and neck.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H992
Section		What to expect
  • Take it as prescribed. Don't change the amount on your own.
  • It works slowly. Most people start to see improvement after about 3 months.
  • Keep using your acne creams or gels unless we told you to stop.
  • If you stop the medicine, acne often comes back over time.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H993
Section		Common side effects
  • Needing to urinate more often
  • Feeling lightheaded or dizzy, especially when you stand up quickly. Get up slowly and drink enough fluids.
  • Changes in your period, such as spotting between periods
  • Breast tenderness
  • Feeling tired, or mild headache

These are often mild and may settle with time. Tell us if they bother you.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H994
Section		Pregnancy

Do not take spironolactone if you are pregnant. It could affect the development of a baby. If you could become pregnant, talk with us about birth control while you take it.

If you become pregnant, stop taking it and call us. Also tell us if you are breastfeeding.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H995
Section		Potassium and lab tests

Spironolactone can raise the level of potassium in your blood. For most healthy young people this is not a problem. If we order blood tests, please get them done when we asked.

  • Don't take potassium supplements or use salt substitutes (which often contain potassium) unless we said it's OK.
  • Tell us about all your medicines, especially for blood pressure, heart or kidney problems.
  • Tell us if you have kidney disease.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Minoxidil for hair loss tx-minoxidil

Patient sees: Using minoxidil for hair loss · Clinician summary: How to apply topical minoxidil, the early shedding phase, why consistency matters, side effects, pets and pregnancy.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H1001
Section		What it is

Minoxidil helps hair grow by lengthening the growing phase of hair and making thin hairs thicker. It comes as a liquid or foam that you put on the scalp. It is used most often for pattern hair loss in men and women.

It works best to keep the hair you have and regrow some thinning hair. It does not usually regrow hair on fully bald areas.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1002
Section		How to use it
  • Use it as directed on the label or as we told you.
  • Apply it to a dry scalp, on the thinning areas, not to the hair itself.
  • Spread it with your fingertips, then wash your hands well.
  • Let it dry fully before you style your hair, put on a hat or go to bed. This keeps it from rubbing onto your face or pillow.
  • Don't use more than directed. More does not work better.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1003
Section		What to expect: early shedding

In the first few weeks to 2 months, many people notice more hair shedding. This is a normal sign. Old resting hairs are being pushed out by new growing ones. Keep going.

  • It usually takes at least 4 to 6 months to see results.
  • It can take about a year to see the full effect.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1004
Section		Consistency matters

Minoxidil only works while you use it. If you stop, the hair it helped usually falls out over several months, and hair loss continues as before.

Use it every day as directed. If you miss a dose, just restart. Don't double up.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1005
Section		Side effects and safety
  • Itchy, dry or flaky scalp. The foam may be less irritating than the liquid. Tell us if this bothers you.
  • Unwanted hair growth on the face or forehead, often from product spreading there. It usually goes away after stopping.
  • Do not use it if you are pregnant or breastfeeding.
  • Minoxidil is very toxic to cats, even in small amounts. Keep it away from pets, and don't let them touch or lick treated skin, hands or bedding.
  • If we prescribed minoxidil pills instead, take them only as prescribed.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1006
Stop and call if		Chest pain, a fast or pounding heartbeat, fainting or dizziness.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1007
Stop and call if		Sudden weight gain, or swelling of the hands, feet, ankles or face.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1008
Stop and call if		Redness, burning or rash on the scalp that doesn't settle.☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1009
Stop and call if		Shedding that is still heavy after 2 months, or no improvement after 6 months.☐ Approve
☐ Edit

Hydroquinone for dark spots tx-hydroquinone

Patient sees: Using hydroquinone for dark spots · Clinician summary: How to apply hydroquinone, using it in cycles with breaks, irritation, sun protection, and stopping if skin darkens.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H1011
Section		What it is

Hydroquinone is a cream that lightens dark spots. It slows the skin's production of melanin (the pigment that gives skin its color). It is used for melasma, dark marks left after acne or rashes, and some sun spots.

It works slowly. Most people start to see lightening after about 1 to 3 months.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1012
Section		How to use it
  • Use it as prescribed, usually at night.
  • Wash and dry your skin first.
  • Apply a thin layer to the dark spots only. It can lighten the normal skin around them.
  • Keep it away from the eyes, lips and inside the nose.
  • Wash your hands afterward.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1013
Section		Using it in cycles

Hydroquinone is not meant to be used every day without end. It is usually used for a few months at a time, followed by a break, as we direct.

  • Follow the schedule we gave you.
  • Keep your follow-up visits so we can decide when to pause or restart.
  • During breaks, sunscreen and any other products we suggested help keep spots from coming back.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1014
Section		Irritation and sun protection

Some redness, dryness or stinging can happen. If it is mild, using it less often and adding a gentle moisturizer may help.

Sun makes dark spots darker and can undo your progress. Use a broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher every day, all year. A tinted mineral sunscreen can help with melasma. Wear a hat outdoors.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1015
Section		Important cautions
  • Stop and call us if the treated skin gets darker, or turns gray or blue-black. This is rare, but it can happen with long-term use.
  • Do not use it if you are pregnant or breastfeeding unless we said it's OK.
  • Do not buy skin lighteners online or from stores outside the U.S. Some contain mercury, strong steroids or unlisted ingredients.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut

Starting a biologic injection tx-biologic-injections

Patient sees: Starting your biologic injections · Clinician summary: General self-injection tips, storage and travel, injection-site reactions, and infections and other changes to report.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H1020
Section		What it is

Biologics are medicines made from living cells. They block specific parts of the immune system that drive skin conditions such as psoriasis, eczema or hidradenitis suppurativa. Most are given as a shot under the skin with a prefilled pen or syringe.

Your medicine comes with Instructions for Use from the maker. Follow those, and your schedule as prescribed. The tips below apply to most biologics.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1021
Section		Storing your medicine
  • Keep it in the refrigerator, in its original box to protect it from light, unless the label says otherwise.
  • Never freeze it. Don't use it if it has been frozen.
  • Don't shake it.
  • Some products can be kept at room temperature for a set number of days. Check your label.
  • When traveling, keep it cool (not frozen) in an insulated bag with an ice pack. Carry it with you, not in checked luggage.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1022
Section		Injection-day tips
  • Take it out of the fridge and let it reach room temperature for the time the label says. This makes the shot more comfortable. Don't warm it any other way.
  • Check the expiration date. Look at the liquid if there is a window: it should match what the label describes.
  • Wash your hands. Clean the skin with an alcohol wipe and let it dry.
  • Common sites are the front of the thighs and the belly (staying about 2 inches from the belly button). Someone else can use the back of your upper arm.
  • Change sites each time. Avoid skin that is bruised, tender, scarred or affected by your skin condition.
  • Put used pens and syringes in a sharps container.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1023
Section		What to expect

Mild redness, swelling, itching or a small bruise where you inject is common. It usually fades within a few days. A cool compress can help.

Most biologics take weeks to months to reach their full effect. Keep taking it on schedule.

If you miss a dose, follow the label or call us. Don't double up.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1024
Section		Before vaccines, surgery or pregnancy
  • Check with us before getting any vaccine. Live vaccines are usually avoided while on a biologic.
  • Tell us before any surgery or dental procedure.
  • Tell us if you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy or breastfeeding.
  • Tell every doctor and pharmacist that you take a biologic.
☐ Approve

Notes:

Sunscreen: choosing and using it tx-sunscreen

Patient sees: How to choose and use sunscreen · Clinician summary: SPF 30+, broad spectrum, water resistance, how much to apply, reapplying, and other sun protection.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H1031
Section		Choosing a sunscreen

Look for these three things on the label:

  • SPF 30 or higher. SPF measures protection from sunburn.
  • Broad spectrum. This means it protects against both UVA rays (which age the skin) and UVB rays (which burn). Both can cause skin cancer.
  • Water resistant (40 or 80 minutes), if you will swim or sweat. No sunscreen is waterproof.

Mineral sunscreens (zinc oxide, titanium dioxide) and chemical sunscreens both work. Mineral types may be gentler for sensitive skin. The best sunscreen is one you like enough to use every day.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1032
Section		How much to use

Most people use far too little.

  • Body: about 1 ounce for an adult, about enough to fill a shot glass.
  • Face and neck: about a teaspoon.
  • Don't forget the ears, back of the neck, tops of the feet, the scalp where hair is thin, and the lips (use an SPF lip balm).

Apply it about 15 minutes before you go outside. With sprays, spray until the skin looks wet, then rub it in. Don't spray directly on the face or breathe it in.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1033
Section		Reapplying
  • Reapply at least every 2 hours when outdoors.
  • Reapply right after swimming, heavy sweating or towel drying.
  • Use it every day, even when it's cloudy. UV rays pass through clouds.
  • Water, sand and snow reflect sunlight and raise your exposure.
  • Check the expiration date. If there is none, most sunscreens are made to last about 3 years.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1034
Section		More ways to protect your skin

Sunscreen works best with other protection:

  • Seek shade, especially between 10 a.m. and 4 p.m.
  • Wear long sleeves, pants, a wide-brimmed hat and UV-blocking sunglasses.
  • Avoid tanning beds.
  • Babies under 6 months: keep them out of direct sun. Use shade and clothing. Ask us before using sunscreen on a young baby.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1035
Step · Morning		Sunscreen (OTC; picker search “SPF 30”)
Every morning as the last step: broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher. Reapply every 2 hours outdoors and after swimming or sweating.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1036
Step · Morning		Lip balm with SPF (OTC; picker search “lip balm SPF 30”)
On the lips whenever you are outdoors. Reapply often, and after eating or drinking.		☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1037
Stop and call if		A rash, itching or burning where you put sunscreen (you may be sensitive to an ingredient).☐ Approve
