Notes:

Sun protection after skin cancer tx-sun-protection-after-skin-cancer

Patient sees: Protecting your skin after skin cancer · Clinician summary: Why risk of new skin cancers is higher, daily sun habits, scar care, follow-up visits and self-checks.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H1040
Section		Why it matters

Having had one skin cancer means you are more likely to get another one. This is usually because of past sun damage. It is not your fault, and it is never too late to protect your skin. Good sun habits lower your risk from here on, and regular checks help find any new spot early, when it is easiest to treat.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1041
Section		Every day
  • Use a broad-spectrum sunscreen SPF 30 or higher on your face, neck, ears, scalp if hair is thin, and the backs of your hands. Do this every day, even when it's cloudy or you'll only be out a short time.
  • Reapply every 2 hours outdoors, and after swimming or sweating.
  • Use a lip balm with SPF.
  • UVA rays pass through car side windows and many home windows. Sunscreen or UV-protective clothing helps when you drive a lot or sit by a window.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1042
Section		Outdoors
  • Plan outdoor time for early morning or late afternoon when you can. The sun is strongest between 10 a.m. and 4 p.m.
  • Seek shade.
  • Wear a wide-brimmed hat (baseball caps leave the ears and neck uncovered).
  • Wear long sleeves and pants. Clothing labeled UPF 30 or higher gives reliable protection.
  • Wear UV-blocking sunglasses.
  • Never use tanning beds.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1043
Section		Your scar

A new scar can darken if it gets sun while it heals. Once it has fully healed, cover it with clothing or sunscreen whenever you go outside, usually for at least a year.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1044
Section		Follow-up and self-checks
  • Keep your skin check visits on the schedule we set.
  • Check your own skin once a month, including your scalp, ears and back. Ask someone to help with hard-to-see areas.
  • If you are worried about vitamin D from avoiding the sun, ask us. Food or supplements are safer than sun exposure.
☐ Approve

☐ Cut

Step · Morning		Sunscreen (OTC; picker search “SPF 30”)
Every morning on all exposed skin: broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher. Reapply every 2 hours outdoors and after swimming or sweating.		☐ Approve

☐ Approve as written☐ Approve with edits☐ Don't publish
Notes:

Monthly skin self-exam tx-skin-self-exam

Patient sees: How to check your skin each month · Clinician summary: A step-by-step monthly self-exam, the ABCDEs of melanoma, the ugly duckling sign, and other spots to report.

#Text (the clinician can edit any of it before giving it out)Sign-off
H1050
Section		Why check your skin

Skin cancer is easiest to treat when it is found early. Checking your skin once a month helps you learn what's normal for you, so you notice when something new or changing appears.

You'll need a bright room, a full-length mirror, a hand mirror and a chair. A partner or family member can help check your back and scalp.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1051
Section		Step by step
  • Face, including the nose, lips, mouth and ears (front and back).
  • Scalp: part your hair in sections with a comb or hair dryer. Ask someone to help.
  • Hands: palms, backs, between the fingers and under the nails.
  • Arms, elbows and underarms.
  • Neck, chest and belly. Lift the breasts to check underneath.
  • Back, buttocks and backs of the legs, using the hand mirror and full-length mirror.
  • Sit down to check the legs, tops and soles of the feet, between the toes and under the toenails.
  • Genital area, using the hand mirror.
☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1052
Section		The ABCDEs of melanoma

Melanoma is a serious type of skin cancer. Look for moles or spots with:

  • A, Asymmetry: one half doesn't match the other.
  • B, Border: the edge is uneven, jagged or blurry.
  • C, Color: more than one color, or uneven shades of brown, black, tan, red, white or blue.
  • D, Diameter: bigger than about 6 millimeters (the size of a pencil eraser). Melanomas can be smaller.
  • E, Evolving: the spot is changing in size, shape or color, or starts to itch, crust or bleed.

Evolving, or changing, is one of the most important signs.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1053
Section		The ugly duckling sign

Most of your moles tend to look like each other. A mole that looks different from all the others, the ugly duckling, deserves a closer look. It may be bigger, darker, lighter, a different shape, or just look out of place.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
H1054
Section		Other spots to report and helpful tips

Not all skin cancers are dark. Also watch for:

  • A shiny, pearly or pink bump
  • A rough, scaly or crusted red patch
  • A sore that bleeds or doesn't heal
  • A new dark streak in a fingernail or toenail

Tips: take photos of your skin, with a ruler next to spots you are watching. Pick the same day each month, such as your birthday date.

☐ Approve
☐ Edit
☐ Cut
